Summary

Zosia never meant to fall for the woman behind her grandmother’s favorite romance novels.
Carol Sturka writes sweeping straight love stories and guards her private life carefully. But one late-night message turns curiosity into connection, and connection into something neither of them can ignore.

One lives in New York. The other in Albuquerque.
And falling in love might cost more than just the distance between them.

✓♥︎

Off the Record

by RunCriedTheCrawling

https://archiveofourown.org/works/79726156

    Chapter 1: Goodbye, babcia

     

    “Can you grab me one of those boxes?” Zosia asked her sister, nudging the shelf with her elbow. “I want to take these books home, if that’s okay.”

    Joanna followed her gaze. “Oh no. Not the Wycaro books.”

    “Yes, the Wycaro books.”

    “I still don’t understand what she liked about them so much,” Joanna said. “They’re all brooding stares and dramatic kissing. It’s exhausting.”

    Zosia smiled to herself. “We don’t have to understand it. Babcia liked them, and that’s enough.” She slid one book free, the spine soft and cracked from rereading. “Besides, if she went through these books this many times, something must’ve been working.”

    She studied the authors photo on the back. Blond. Intense blue eyes. An expression that suggested mild irritation with the entire concept of publicity. “Okay,” she said. “I might read one. Just to see.”

    Joanna laughed. “You’re going to hate it.”

    “Probably.”

    They taped up the final box and carried it outside. The van was already packed to the roof, stuffed with furniture, photo albums, old sweaters that still smelled faintly of mothballs and the perfume her grandma always used to wear. The door slammed shut with a dull finality.

    For a moment, neither of them spoke.

    Zosia leaned against the van and gave the house one last look. “Goodbye, babcia,” she said quietly. “Thank you for everything.”

    Joanna slipped an arm around her shoulders. “We really did have some good times here, didn’t we?”

    “We did,” Zosia said, her voice cracked.

    Joanna broke the moment with a laugh. “Remember those neighbors? The ones with the cute son?”

    Zosia groaned. “Please don’t.”

    “He followed you around like a lost puppy. I was so jealous.”

    “You could’ve had him,” Zosia replied. “He was annoying and smelled like Axe.”

    She shook her head. “You’re crazy.”

    “No, honey,” Zosia smiled faintly. “I’m gay. That’s something else entirely.”

    Joanna gave her a sideways look, amused. “Yeah. That became pretty obvious.”

    Zosia took a slow breath.

    “Okay. Let’s go put all of this in storage.”

     

    ——

     

    The drive passed mostly in silence, broken only by the hum of the engine and the rattle of boxes in the back. By the time they reached the storage facility, the sky had begun to dim, washed in pale oranges and gray.

    They worked without much talking, they hauled box after box down narrow corridors and into storage.

    After thirty boxes, two arguments about how to stack them, and a lot of sore muscles, they finally locked the metal unit.

    “That’s enough for one day,” Joanna said.

    Zosia nodded. “Definitely enough.”

     

    ——

     

    At home, Zosia lined the Wycaro books up on her coffee table and stared at them like they might explain themselves if she waited long enough. It would probably be easier than reading.

    She already knew the type. Heteronormative nonsense where the man swoops in to save the woman from herself because apparently she’s been doing life wrong this whole time. Sword fights. Brooding glances. Emotional repression marketed as romance.

    Still, babcia loved them.

    Zosia picked one up and turned it over, studying the authors photo again.

    Cute, she decided. 

    She made herself a cup of tea and stretched out on the couch. With a sigh that was half-curiosity, half-resignation, she opened the book.

     

    Chapter One

    Lucasia knew she would not slumber tonight. The angry moonsburn creeping up her forearms and hands was excruciating. To think, scant seconds beneath the emerald light of the twin moons had brought this wrath upon her. Before the Sickness, she had basked freely in their radiance, resplendent in their glow…

     

    Zosia blinked.

    “Well miss Sturka,” she muttered to the empty room, “you definitely know your way with words.”

    She read on. The prose was dramatic. Lush. Slightly unhinged. By page twenty she snorted aloud. “Oh come on. Nobody talks like that.”

    And yet, she kept turning pages.

    Somewhere around chapter five, one empty mug of tea, and her second glass of wine, she was emotionally invested and mildly ashamed of it.

    “Don’t you dare hurt him!” she whispered. “I swear to God.”

    She froze.

    Then she laughed softly, shaking her head. She sounded exactly like her grandmother.

    Babcia used to talk about the Wycaro characters the way other people talked about neighbors or old friends. Lucasia had been ‘too stubborn for her own good’. Raban was ‘hopeless, but charming’. 

    She used their names casually, fondly, like she expected them to show up for coffee one day.

    Zosia swallowed.

    She remembered a time, years ago, when her grandmother had been quiet for weeks. Not sad exactly, just…dimmer. Her mother had explained it carefully, in the way adults do when they don’t want children to worry.

    Looking back now, Zosia understood. Whatever had been weighing on her, these books had been an escape. A place to go when real life became too heavy.

    She reached for the next book and opened the cover.

    Inside was an inscription:

     

    Keep the winds at your back!

    Carol Sturka

     

    Zosia stared at it.

    “Oh,” she murmured, smiling despite herself. “I guess you’ve met her.”

    On impulse, she grabbed her phone and opened Instagram.

     

    Search: Carol Sturka

     

    There she was. Verified. Polished. Professional headshots and book marketing.

    But three posts down was a blurry photo from a signing, her smile crooked, hair falling out of its careful arrangement, one hand wrapped too tightly around a plastic cup.

    The caption read: ‘Tour life. Glamorous.’

    Zosia smiled. Maybe not as untouchable as she pretended.

    She kept scrolling.

    Carol’s captions were dry in a way that felt calculated. 

    'Thrilled to be awake.' Under a 7 AM airport photo. 

    'Living the dream.' Beneath a stack of tour schedules.

    But in the comments, she softened.

    She remembered people’s names. Asked about their dogs. Told one girl not to apologize for crying at a signing.

    That wasn’t branding. That was effort.

    Zosia felt something shift, small but undeniable.

    Before she could overthink it, she opened her DMs.

     

    Hi Carol,

    You don’t know me, and this might sound a bit strange, but I wanted to thank you. My grandmother was a huge fan, probably your biggest. She passed away last month, and I inherited her Wycaro collection.

    I found one book you signed for her. I’m pretty sure that must have been one of the best days of her life.

    She always said she picked up Wycaro in the first place because the pirate reminded her of me. I don’t see it, but I do find that funny.

    Anyway, thank you for making her happy. It meant more than you probably know.

    Zosia

     

    She hit send and stared at the screen already regretting this decision. 

    “Perfect,” she muttered. “Drunk-texting authors at midnight. Peak maturity.”

    She wasn’t drunk. Not really. But she knew she wouldn’t have done this if she’d stuck with tea instead of pouring a second, rather large, glass of wine.

    Zosia locked her phone, set it face-down on the table, and got up. “Okay.” she said to herself. “That’s enough emotional honesty for one day.”

    She stumbled into bed.

    As she drifted off, one last thought slipped through her mind.

    Her eyes really are very blue though.

     

    ----

    The next morning, Zosia woke up sore in places she’d forgotten existed.

    Her shoulders throbbed from lifting boxes. Her head pulsed faintly. Wine hadn’t helped. Grief hadn’t either.

    Aspirin. Shower. Coffee strong enough to qualify as punishment.

    By the time she sat at the kitchen table, she felt almost human.

    Out of habit, her fingers reached for her phone. 

    Instagram. A notification blinked at her.

    For a brief, blissful second, Zosia forgot about the DM she sent Carol Sturka. Carol “blue eyes” Sturka.

    "Oh dear God. I really need to stop drinking.”

    She opened the message.

     

    Dear Zosia,

    I’m sorry to hear about your grandmother.

    I hope my stories will give you some peace of mind during this difficult time.

    Carol Sturka

     

    Professional, she thought. Polite. Measured. Like someone who chose her words carefully. 

    Exactly what you’d expect from a famous author responding to a stranger who slid into her DMs at one in the morning.

    Zosia blinked. Swallowed. And, of course, hit reply.

     

    Thank you for your kind words.

    And who knows, maybe you’ll gain a fan.

     

    She sent it, immediately second-guessing herself, and then decided it didn’t matter. Life was short, and babcia would have told her to ‘just enjoy the absurdity of it’.

    Phone safely stowed in her bag, she grabbed her keys and stepped outside. 

    The chill morning air hit her face, clearing the last fog from her head. She sighed, mentally bracing herself for the day, and pushed open the door to her record store.

    The familiar chime of the bell greeted her, along with the comforting smell of vinyl and dust. 

    For a few minutes, she walked through the aisles, letting the routine ground her.

    Then her phone buzzed again.

    She fished it out of her bag.

    It was another message from Carol.

     

    If that happens, you are more than welcome at one of my upcoming book readings.

     

    Zosia froze, phone hovering mid-air. Her brain stuttered, trying to process what she had just read. She blinked. Slowly.

    “Well,” she muttered to the empty store, “that escalated quickly.”

    Without overthinking, she pulled up her browser and Googled Carol Sturka. 

    The official website popped up. Loads of purple, very much on-brand. 

    She scrolled, scanning through upcoming events, book signings and readings.

    There it was. A reading in two weeks. Just an hour’s drive from her apartment.

    Her stomach flipped. Should I?

    Her rational side argued. Don’t be ridiculous. It’s awkward.

    The other side however, the one babcia would have recognized instantly, smirked. Zosia, you’ve never been good at doing things that are normal. Just go. She would say.

    She sat down on the edge of her counter, chewing her lip. Could she justify it? Sure. Support the arts, honor her grandmother’s memory, meet the person who had brought so much happiness into babcia’s life.

    And she found herself smiling.

    Yeah, this is probably a terrible idea.

    I should go.

    Chapter 2: The Book Reading

     

    As the days went by, Zosia found herself thinking about Carol Sturka… a lot. Too much. Maybe unhealthily so for someone she had never met. She scolded herself each time. Stop obsessing. She was only being friendly. It’s her job.

    Still, Carol’s face kept drifting into her thoughts at odd moments. While alphabetizing records, while making coffee, while lying awake at night staring at the ceiling.

    She debated for a full hour whether she should let Carol know she was planning to go to the reading. Ultimately, she decided against it. Why the hell would Carol care? She was a professional who was friendly with her fans, nothing more. Just business. Anything else was projecting.

    The day of the reading arrived too quickly, yet not quickly enough.

    Zosia parked her car and lingered for a moment, hand on the door, heart thudding like she was about to walk into an exam she hadn’t studied for. Inside, the bookstore had been completely overtaken. Giant posters of Carol Sturka covered the walls. The cover of her latest book loomed everywhere. Stacked on tables, displayed in windows, propped on easels.

    No Raban on the cover this time. Maybe she’d finally killed him off. Zosia grinned.

    She found one of the last empty seats and settled in, listening to the excited chatter around her. Fans leaned toward each other, whispering theories, arguing passionately about character arcs. Speculating whether Raban, haughty Raban, would finally meet his end or suffer just a little longer. The room hummed with anticipation.

    Then the music stopped.

    Zosia inhaled sharply as Carol walked to the front and took her seat. 

    Seeing her in real life did not disappoint. If anything, it was worse. Carol seemed more solid, more vivid than on screen. Her presence sharpened the air around her. Her blue eyes scanned the room, alert and amused, like she was taking everything in at once.

    Zosia’s pulse kicked up. Get it together.

    After a simple hello and welcome, Carol began to read.

    Zosia barely registered the story. What she noticed instead was the way Carol’s hands moved as she spoke, how her voice dipped and rose, how she leaned into certain words like they mattered deeply to her. Every pause, every inflection gave the text weight. 

    It wasn’t just a reading. It was a performance. Something alive.

    When it ended, the spell broke all at once.

    Fans surged toward the signing table. Zosia strategically stayed back, letting the line stretch out in front of her. She wanted to be last. Partly to delay the inevitable. Mostly because she was starstruck and terrified that once she opened her mouth, only vowels would come out.

    Eventually, there was no one left between her and the table.

    She swallowed and stepped forward.

    “Hello,” Carol said, looking up with an easy smile.

    “Uh… hi.” Zosia replied. Two weeks of mental preparation evaporated instantly.

    She handed over the book like it was an offering.

    Carol took it. “To whom shall I sign this?”

    “Z…Zosia,” she managed.

    Carol’s eyes flicked up. “Wait, are you…?”

    “Yes,” Zosia blurted out, relief rushing in. “I messaged you. About my grandmother.”

    Recognition softened Carol’s expression. 

    “Ah. Yes. I see it now.” Her gaze lingered for a fraction longer than necessary. “You do resemble Raban a little. I tried to tell from your profile picture, but it was hard to make out.”

    Heat rushed to Zosia’s face.

    “You’re much prettier than Raban, though.” Carol added lightly, winking as she signed the book.

    Zosia forgot, briefly, how language worked. Heat climbed her neck and the room seemed to tilt, just enough to make her question every decision that had led her here.

    She took the signed copy with trembling hands, muttered a thank you, and somehow found herself outside moments later, standing on the sidewalk clutching Wycaro like proof that the interaction had actually happened.

    Across the street, a bar glowed warmly, amber light spilling onto the pavement like a promise. A promise to calm her down, lift her up. 

    Zosia stood there for a moment, keys in her hand, staring at her car.

    Coffee, she decided.

    She crossed the street and stepped inside. The bar was quiet at this hour. It was still more a café than a bar. She was met by the smell of espresso, citrus cleaner, and something faintly sweet.

    Zosia ordered at the bar, thanked the bartender, and slid onto a stool near the end, setting her bag at her feet like an anchor. Only then did she realize her hands were still trembling.

    She pulled out her phone.

    Instagram refreshed instantly, as if it had been waiting for her. Photos were already everywhere. Carol at the reading table, pen mid-stroke. Carol smiling at fans. Carol holding up her book like a trophy. Carol leaning in close, listening, attentive, generous.

    Zosia swallowed.

    “You are not allowed to be this attractive in real life,” she muttered, scrolling. “It’s deeply irresponsible.”

    Zosia was halfway through zooming in on one photo, purely to check the lighting, obviously, when a voice behind her said,

    “Is this seat taken?”

    Her stomach dropped.

    She turned slowly.

    Carol stood a few feet away, coat still on, fingers wrapped around her own phone like she hadn’t quite decided whether to approach. 

    Without stage lights, without the signing table between them, she looked…smaller. More human.

    “N…no,” Zosia managed. “It’s free.”

    Carol hesitated, just a fraction, then stepped forward.

    “I wasn’t sure if it would be strange,” she admitted lightly. “But you left so quickly.”

    Heat rushed up Zosia’s neck. “I didn’t want to take up your time.”

    A corner of Carol’s mouth lifted.

    “I think,” she said carefully, “that part was my decision.”

    She slipped onto the stool beside her, not too close. But close enough.

    For a moment, neither of them spoke.

    “I don’t usually,” Carol began, then stopped. Reconsidered. “I don’t usually join readers afterward.”

    Zosia’s heart stumbled. “Oh?”

    Carol glanced at her, something unreadable flickering behind her eyes.

    “But,” she added, quieter now, “you don’t seem like the typical reader.”

    Zosia lifted her chin slightly. “Who says I’m not?”

    Carol smiled. “Fair.”

    The bartender approached.

    “Red?” Carol asked, glancing sideways at Zosia.

    Zosia hesitated a beat too long. “Sure.”

    Carol ordered two glasses.

    When they arrived, she didn’t immediately lift hers. Instead, she rolled the stem between her fingers, watching Zosia over the rim.

    “So,” she said, voice lower now that there was no audience. “What’s the verdict?”

    “On?”

    “My life’s work,” Carol replied, faintly amused.

    Zosia swallowed. “It’s…very straight.”

    Carol didn’t laugh. She didn’t flinch either.

    Instead, she tipped her head slightly.

    “And that’s a flaw?” she asked carefully.

    “It’s just not my demographic.”

    Carol’s mouth curved slowly.

    “And what is your demographic?”

    The question was quiet. Neutral on the surface, weighted underneath.

    Color rose to her face before she could stop it.

    She held her gaze. “Women.”

    Silence.

    Carol’s fingers stilled on the glass. Something flickered across her expression, quick, contained, before she smoothed it away with a sip of wine.

    “So,” Carol said slowly, “what you’re saying is…” Her gaze returned to Zosia, sharp and curious. “…Raban should’ve been a woman?”

    Zosia looked up, startled, then smiled. “Absolutely.”

    Carol’s lips curved into something softer, more genuine. “Huh.” She lifted her glass. “Well. Cheers to that.”

    She clinked her glass gently against Zosia’s.

    “Cheers,” Zosia echoed, heart thudding a little harder than before.

    “So,” Carol said, easing back on her stool, voice gentler now, “tell me about your grandmother. What was her name?”

    “Moira,” Zosia said, smiling despite the small ache the name still carried.

    Carol’s face lit up instantly. “Craz…” She stopped herself and laughed under her breath. “Moira. Yes. I know exactly who that was.”

    Zosia blinked. “You do?”

    “Oh, very much so.” Carol took a sip of wine, shaking her head fondly. “She used to send me these…things. Little crocheted hats.”

    Zosia groaned. “Oh no.”

    “Purple ones, mostly,” Carol continued. “Occasionally green. No explanation. Just a note that said, For inspiration.”

    Zosia covered her face. “I am so, so sorry. I have an entire box of them at home.”

    Carol laughed so hard she had to lean against the bar. “I had one hanging from my desk lamp for years.”

    They were both laughing now, really laughing. Something in Zosia’s chest loosened by the strange comfort of realizing that this woman hadn’t just written stories her grandmother loved, she had known her, in an absolute absurd way.

    “She adored you,” Zosia said softly once the laughter faded. “Your books were her escape when things got hard.”

    Carol’s expression softened. “I’m glad,” she said. “That means more than you know.”

    They talked easily after that. As if they’d skipped past the awkward early chapters and landed somewhere better.

    Zosia told her about Moira’s bridge club, her brutal honesty, her belief that everyone should own at least one good hat and never apologize for wanting joy. Carol shared stories about fan letters, about the strange intimacy of writing for people she’d never meet.

    They talked about records. Vinyl versus digital, the sacred ritual of flipping a side, the heartbreak of warped pressings. Zosia’s hands came alive when she talked about her store, about finding someone the perfect album and watching their face change when the needle dropped.

    Carol listened. Really listened. Asked questions. Leaned in.

    And then, inevitably, time ruined everything.

    Zosia glanced at her phone and let out a quiet sigh. “I should probably go. My store opens at nine, and I still have an hour’s drive ahead of me.”

    Carol nodded, though something in her smile shifted. “Right. Responsibility. Hard to argue with that.”

    “It was really nice meeting you,” Zosia said.

    And then, in a moment of awkwardness, Zosia held out her hand like they were concluding a business deal instead of something far more confusing.

    Carol looked at it for a second, amused, then took it.

    Her hand was warm. Firm. She didn’t let go right away.

    “Drive safe,” Carol said, thumb brushing lightly over Zosia’s knuckles before she released her. “It was nice meeting you too.”

    Zosia’s brain absolutely short-circuited.

    She turned and walked out before she could say something catastrophically stupid. Her heart racing, cheeks warm, already replaying the moment over and over in her head.

    She resisted the urge to look back.

     

    ——

     

    Back home, Zosia kicked off her shoes and let her jacket slide to the floor.

    The apartment greeted her with silence. Not cozy silence. Judgmental silence. The kind that said, So. How was meeting the famous author, Romeo?

    “Stupid,” Zosia muttered to no one.

    Her phone was in her hand before she consciously decided to pick it up. Muscle memory with a hint of self-sabotage.

    Instagram.

    Nothing.

    Of course there was nothing. Carol Sturka charmed people for a living. Zosia had probably been catalogued under Nice Woman, strange grandma, said something gay about my book and was promptly forgotten.

    “Congratulations,” Zosia told her reflection in the dark window. “You had a moment.”

    She dropped onto the couch, phone hovering over her chest.

    She opened Instagram again.

    Refreshed.

    Still nothing.

    She let out a frustrated breath.

    There were exactly two people she could talk to about this without regretting it immediately.

    Joanna was asleep, and historically unhelpful. So she was left with only one other option.

    She opened FaceTime.

    Koumba Diabaté

    It rang twice.

    “Well helloooooo,” Koumba sang into the camera.

    The screen filled with his grinning face, sweaty and glowing, framed by pulsing colored lights. Music thumped in the background. Someone yelled something unintelligible in French.

    Zosia squinted. “Are you at a party?”

    “Yes. And I have made several bad decisions at once,” he said cheerfully. “There is alcohol, questionable flirting, and a woman who owns a hamster.”

    “Why are you like this?” she wondered out loud.

    “Because God loves chaos,” he replied. “Why are you calling me at midnight, ma cherie?”

    Zosia hesitated. “I did something stupid.”

    His grin softened immediately. “Ah. The good kind of stupid or the emotionally devastating kind?”

    “Unclear.”

    “Excellent. Tell me everything.”

    She opened her mouth, then closed it again. Suddenly, yelling over music felt impossible. “Actually…maybe tomorrow.”

    Koumba frowned. “Zos. You don’t FaceTime me at this hour unless something is eating you alive.”

    She sighed. “I’m okay. Just…processing.”

    He studied her for a second, the way he always did when he was deciding whether to push or not. “Alright. Tomorrow then. But I will pry.”

    “I know.” She smiled faintly. “Try not to adopt the hamster.”

    “No promises.”

     

    —————

     

    The next morning, Zosia unlocked her record store just as the sun crept over the street. The smell hit her instantly. Vinyl, dust, old cardboard and the faint smell of coffee from the café next door. It grounded her in a way nothing else did.

    She had just stepped inside when she heard a familiar voice, too loud for the time of day, behind her.

    “Hold the door!”

    She turned to see Koumba sprinting toward her, jacket slung over one shoulder, sunglasses still on despite the early hour.

    “You’ve got to be kidding me,” she laughed. “What are you doing here? Walk of shame?”

    “Run of shame,” he announced proudly. “Walking implies dignity.”

    She eyed him. “Did you at least have fun?”

    “Oh tremendously. Not with the original date, mind you. That woman disappeared mid-conversation. But then there was…”

    “Nope,” Zosia said, cutting him off. “It’s too early.”

    He laughed and dropped onto the old couch in the back of the store like it belonged to him. Koumba had always moved through the world that way. Taking up space without apology. Tall, charming, catastrophically handsome, and somehow never intimidating. Zosia had known him long enough to see the softness beneath the performance.

    “So,” he said finally, turning serious. “What did you want to tell me last night?”

    Zosia froze.

    He noticed immediately. “Oh,” he said slowly. “This is that kind of glow.”

    “I do not glow.”

    “You are practically illuminated,” he said. “Who is she?”

    Zosia sighed, leaning against the counter. “Some writer I met.”

    “Okay.”

    “Older.”

    “Even better.”

    “Fifty-three.”

    He grinned. “Still better.”

    “And probably straight,” she added, quieter.

    Koumba groaned. “Ah. Not better. We don’t do this, Zos.”

    “I know,” she said quickly. “It’s stupid. But we had a drink. And maybe there was a spark. Or maybe I imagined it.”

    He tilted his head. “Did she tell you she is straight?”

    “No. But she writes the straightest romance novels known to humankind.”

    “So?” He lifted one shoulder in a careless shrug and held out his hand. “Phone.”

    She stared at him. “Absolutely not.”

    “Zosia. I know you have her socials open. Let me see.”

    She groaned and handed it over.

    He scrolled once, twice, then made a quiet sound of approval. “She isn’t straight.”

    Zosia stared. “You cannot possibly know that.”

    “I can,” he said calmly. “The posture. The look in her eyes. Also, no straight woman wears a jacket like that.” He tilted the phone slightly toward her.

    Zosia groaned. “That is not scientific.”

    “It doesn’t have to be,” he replied, handing the phone back. “Gay.”

    He grabbed his jacket again. “Now I’m going home before I collapse in your jazz section.”

    “Good plan,” Zosia said, laughing despite herself.

    As he left, she stood there alone in her store, heart lighter than it had been in days.

    Maybe she was imagining things.

    But Carol hadn’t followed her into that bar for no reason.

    Chapter 3: Music-Girl

    Chapter Notes

    Thank you all so much for the nice comments. I really appreciate them!

     

    Three days later, Zosia was elbow-deep in a crate of Motown singles when her phone began vibrating insistently in her back pocket, the sound muffled but persistent enough to break through the steady rhythm of vinyl sleeves sliding against one another.

    She ignored it at first.

    It buzzed again.

    With a quiet sigh, she straightened, wiped her hands down the front of her jeans, and pulled out her phone, already bracing herself for disappointment.

    Instagram — 2 new messages.

    Her heart performed a small, humiliating acrobatic routine before she even opened them.

    Carol: Hi music-girl,

    How are you doing?

    Despite herself, Zosia felt the corner of her mouth lift. *Music-girl.* Apparently she had been officially categorized, labeled, filed away somewhere in Carol Sturka’s mind under a title that felt both harmless and strangely intimate.

    The next message followed almost immediately.

    Carol:  I was wondering… do you have “Georgia on My Mind” by Ray Charles in stock by any chance? I need a present for a friend.

    Zosia stared at the word friend a little longer than she meant to, as if it might explain itself if she kept looking at it.

    She sprinted towards the Soul & Blues section before she’d fully processed the message, weaving past crates like a woman on a mission. Fingers flew through the C’s.

    “Come on,” she muttered under her breath, “don’t embarrass me now.”

    She found it almost immediately.

    Ray Charles. “Georgia on My Mind.” Deluxe pressing. Thick sleeve. Near mint condition. The kind of vinyl that felt important in your hands.

    “Oh, you beautiful bastard,” she whispered reverently.

    She snapped a photo and sent it before she could reconsider the speed of her response.

    Zosia: Absolutely. I’ve got it.

    The typing dots appeared almost instantly.

    Carol: Until what time are you open?

    Zosia swallowed.

    Zosia: Until 6.

    A pause. Long enough for her to imagine seventeen different futures, twelve of which ended in heartbreak and one in her fainting dramatically between the record bins.

    Carol: No problem. Can you send me the address? I can swing by before heading to the airport.

    For a moment she simply stared at the screen.

    Swing by. Here?

    She sent the address with trembling hands and, before she could give herself time to spiral, called Koumba.

    He answered on the second ring.

    “Yes, my favorite lesbian.”

    “That is not a normal way to answer a phone,” Zosia said automatically.

    “It is,” he replied calmly, “if I already know exactly why you’re calling.”

    “She’s coming to the store.”

    There was a beat of silence.

    “Okay, I had no idea why you were calling,” he corrected. Then, more cautiously, “Who is coming?”

    “Carol.”

    A sharp inhale came through the speaker. “Oh.”

    “She needs a specific record.”

    Koumba snorted audibly. “Wow. That’s subtle. Like, incredibly subtle.”

    “Koumba,” she warned.

    “Fine. Stay calm,” he said. “I’ll be there in ten.”

    “That’s not why I…”

    She pinched the bridge of her nose. “Fine. See you in ten minutes.” She was already speaking to a dead line.

    He arrived in twenty-seven minutes.

    “You have terrible time management skills,” she informed him as he walked in.

    “Relax,” he said, scanning the store. “She’s not here yet, is she?” His gaze landed on an elderly woman browsing with her grandson. “Is that her?”

    Zosia slapped his shoulder lightly. “No.”

    “Okay, okay,” he said, raising both hands. “No need for violence.”

    “How do I look?” Zosia asked him.

    “You look fine,” he said. “Don’t worry about that.”

    The bell above the door chimed.

    Carol stood just inside the threshold.

    No stage lights. No microphone. No carefully measured public smile.

    Just Carol. Coat open, hair slightly wind-tossed, one hand still resting on the door as if she hadn’t fully committed to stepping inside.

    For a fraction of a second, their eyes met.

    Recognition softened her expression almost immediately.

    “Oh,” Carol said quietly, almost as if she hadn’t expected the sight of Zosia to feel quite like that. “Hi.”

    Zosia’s brain emptied.

    “Hi,” she managed.

    Another woman stepped in behind her. Tall, composed, brown hair pulled back neatly, her gaze sharp and assessing in a way that felt focussed rather than curious.

    “This is it?” the woman asked.

    “Yes,” Carol said, though her eyes remained on Zosia.

    Koumba leaned closer, without taking his eyes off the scene.

    “Okay,” he murmured under his breath. “Plot twist.”

    Zosia elbowed him lightly.

    “You found it,” she said to Carol, instantly regretting how unnecessary the statement sounded.

    Carol’s mouth curved faintly. “Helen knows her way around New York.”

    Helen.

    Helen offered a polite nod before drifting further into the store, her attention moving over shelves with quiet efficiency.

    “How are you?” Carol asked.

    “Good,” Zosia replied. “You?”

    She thought for a second. “I’ve been explaining fictional men to strangers all afternoon,” Carol said dryly. “So I’m feeling deeply fulfilled.”

    “You know,” Koumba said thoughtfully, “I’ve always felt underrepresented in pirate literature.”

    Carol’s gaze shifted to him, amused.

    “And you are?”

    “Koumba,” he said smoothly. “Friend. Occasional employee. Head of H.R.”

    Zosia rolled her eyes. “Don’t mind him. He was just about to sort the sale section.”

    He lifted his hands in surrender and wandered off, though not far.

    Helen reappeared near the counter.

    “We don’t have long,” Helen said mildly, glancing at her watch as she turned slightly toward Carol.

    Zosia retrieved the record she had already set aside and placed it carefully between them.

    Carol’s fingers brushed the sleeve, lingering for a fraction longer than necessary.

    “Perfect,” she said softly.

    Helen reached for her wallet before Carol could. “I’ve got it.”

    Carol didn’t argue.

    That detail lodged somewhere uncomfortable inside Zosia.

    “Do you want it wrapped?” she asked, because standing still suddenly felt dangerous.

    “Please,” Carol replied. At that moment, Zosia caught the faintest tightening around Helen’s eyes.

    Zosia folded the paper slowly, aware of being watched. The air between them felt thinner now, as though each small movement carried more meaning than it logically should.

    At one point, Helen stepped closer and placed her hand lightly at the small of Carol’s back while leaning in to murmur something about traffic, and although the gesture was subtle enough to pass as casual to anyone not paying attention, it carried the unmistakable ease of familiarity. 

    Zosia noticed.

    Koumba noticed.

    Carol did not move away from the touch, nor did she lean into it, but in that exact moment her gaze flickered toward Zosia. Quick, searching, as though checking something, before returning to neutral composure.

    It lasted no more than a second. It was more than enough.

    Zosia finished tying the twine with hands that were steadier than she felt and slid the wrapped record across the counter, placing it between them like a fragile offering.

    “There,” she said, striving for normalcy.

    “Thank you,” Carol replied, and when their fingers brushed as the gift changed hands, the contact registered as a small, electric spark that logically meant nothing and yet lingered far longer than it should have.

    Helen cleared her throat gently, not impatient but decisive. “We should go.”

    Carol exhaled, almost reluctant. “Right.”

    Before turning, she looked at Zosia once more. Not the polite glance of someone finishing a transaction, but something slower, as though she didn’t quite want to let it end.

    “Thanks for this,” she said softly.

    “You’re welcome,” Zosia replied, hoping her voice remained steady enough to disguise the tightness in her chest.

    The bell chimed as they stepped outside. The door swung shut behind them.

    Silence settled over the store in their wake, thin and suspended.

    Koumba turned slowly toward her, reading her face with professional concern.

    “Well,” he said at last.

    Zosia did not answer immediately.

    “I have notes,” he added gravely.

    She covered her face with both hands. “Please don’t.”

    “First of all,” he began, lifting a finger in formal presentation mode, “that was not casual friend energy.”

    “I know,” she admitted with a groan.

    “And second,” he went on. “If that brunette is her girlfriend, then Carol just walked into this store playing a very dangerous game.”

    Zosia stared at the door long after it had closed.

    For the first time since meeting Carol, she understood that whatever this was, it was no longer harmless.

     

    ----

     

    That evening, Zosia did what any emotionally stable adult would do and conducted a completely normal social media deep dive on Helen.

    She began with Carol’s following list, scrolling slowly, pretending to herself that she was simply curious about publishing industry connections and not, in fact, investigating a potential romantic rival.

    Helen Umstead.

    Well. That was easy.

    There were two accounts. One private. One verified.

    The verified one told her what she needed to know. At least on paper.

     

    Literary manager @ Wycaro series

    Professional problem-solver

     

    Zosia exhaled.

    Manager. Of course.

    That made sense. Tour logistics. Schedules. Airports. Lower-back guidance.

    It fit the picture she had witnessed in the store. 

    And yet, despite the neat clarity of the bio and the professional tone of everything Helen chose to show the world, something in Zosia refused to settle.

    She clicked into the tagged photos.

    Event after event unfolded beneath her thumb. Bookstores, curated tables, crowded readings, Carol smiling for readers with that controlled ease she carried like armor. And in the margins of nearly every image, Helen.

    Never posing. Just there.

    Zosia zoomed in on one photo from three months ago, a candid shot taken from the side. Carol was laughing at something off-camera, her head tipped back, eyes crinkled in a way that didn’t belong to her public smile. Helen stood beside her, head tilted subtly, not looking at the audience or the photographer, but at Carol.

    That look.

    Was that strictly business? Was that the attentive posture of a competent manager making sure her client didn’t trip over a cable or miss a cue? Or was it something softer? Something that existed outside contracts and calendars?

    Zosia never had a manager. Maybe this was normal. Maybe managers hovered, adjusted sleeves, placed steadying hands at lower backs while murmuring about traffic.

    She returned to Helen’s profile.

    Airports. Hotel lobbies. Wine glasses. Event captions.

    Nothing romantic. Nothing incriminating. Just logistics.

    Her mind replayed the gesture again. Helen’s hand resting lightly at the small of Carol’s back.

    The kind of touch that suggested repetition rather than impulse.

    Zosia sat back on her couch, phone still in her hand, and pressed her thumb against her lip thoughtfully.

    “She could just be hands-on,” she muttered to the empty apartment, the words sounding unconvincing even to her own ears.

    After a moment she opened Carol’s profile again. She scrolled until she reached the most recent post from earlier that day. A photo from the airport, Carol with sunglasses on, coat slung casually over one shoulder, the caption brief and characteristically restrained:

    Onward.

    Helen had liked it. Of course she had.

    She dropped the phone onto her chest and exhaled sharply into the quiet room, the sound louder than she intended.

    This was ridiculous.

    She was not new to this. She had loved before. She knew better.

    Then why was she so affected by a woman she had only met twice?

    The absurdity of it should have snapped her out of it, but it didn’t.

    Zosia rolled onto her side and pulled a pillow beneath her head, staring at the faint glow of city lights through the window while her thoughts refused to quiet.

    If Helen was only a manager, then today meant something.

    If she wasn’t…

    Zosia closed her eyes.

    And for the first time, she stopped pretending she didn’t know why it mattered.

    She wanted Carol.

    Not as a flirtation. Not as a distraction.

    She wanted her in a way that was inconvenient and very real.

    Chapter 4: The Club

    It had been two days since Carol visited the store, and Zosia had finally imposed a strict time limit on her social media. She was constantly refreshing Carol’s profile, analyzing every like, every post. It had become borderline obsessive.

    Koumba noticed immediately.

    “Zos,” he said, pointing the pizza at her for emphasis, “you have two options. Ask her out. Or ignore her completely.” He took a bite and mumbled on. “Either way, it would be nice if you joined me for this dinner.”

    Zosia froze mid-scroll, tomato sauce threatening to drip onto her blouse. In her defense, it was not entirely her fault. Carol was captivating. Every post, every picture, that sly little smirk. It was maddeningly magnetic.

    She sighed and placed her phone face-down on the table, trying to convince herself she could enjoy the evening without obsessing over every tiny thing Carol might be doing on the other side of the country. But it wasn’t easy.

    She hadn’t heard from Carol since the store visit. Not even a polite “my friend loved the gift.”

    The silence was louder than it should have been.

    Koumba leaned back with his arms crossed. “You’re pouting at your phone like a child. You know, you could just…not.”

    “I can’t just not,” Zosia muttered, cheeks heating. “It’s like there’s this tiny little part of my brain that’s just screaming out for her.”

    “Yeah,” Koumba said, “and another part screaming ‘why am I letting a straight woman ruin my life?’”

    Zosia groaned, dragging her hands down her face before pushing back from the table. She stood, paced two steps, then turned back to him.

    “You said she was gay! That was the one thing we had agreed on!”

    Koumba leaned back, one eyebrow lifting slowly. “Then ask her out.”

    She took a deep breath and let it out slowly.

    “How would I even do that?” Her jaw tightened. “What if she and Helen are… something?”

    Koumba shrugged, chewing the last bite of pizza. “Then she says no. You survive. And you’ll move on to the next.”

    “I’m not like you.”

    “Maybe you should be. Just a little bit.” He pinched his fingers together to demonstrate.

    Zosia peeked up at him, skeptical. “Do you think I should message her?”

    Koumba shrugged. “Send her something smart. Something with just enough subtext to make her wonder.” A slow grin. “Worst-case scenario? She ignores it. Best-case?” He waggled his eyebrows. “Scissor emoji.”

    She stared at him. “One, you’re terrible. And two, you are absolutely not helping.”

    She decided to put her phone in a drawer for the night, and focus on her friend right now. It was the decent thing to do since he brought the pizza. And also the healthiest for her sanity.

     

    ——

     

    The second Koumba closed the door behind him, Zosia bolted.

    Not a jog. But a full, barefoot sprint down the hallway like her apartment had caught fire and the only thing worth saving was her phone.

    “Okay, okay, okay,” she muttered, skidding to a stop at the dresser and yanking open the drawer where she had hidden her phone earlier.

    She grabbed it, thumb already flying, heart already racing. Instagram. Open.

    Then…

    Time limit reached.

    Zosia groaned. “Oh, come on!”

    She tried again. Same message. She stared at it, betrayed not just by technology but by Past Zosia, who had clearly believed she possessed self-control.

    She paced the bedroom, phone clenched in her hand.

    Okay. Options.

    She stopped and held up one finger.

    “Option one,” she told the room, “I wait until tomorrow morning. I wake up calm. Rational. Like a mentally stable adult.”

    She immediately snorted. “Unlikely.”

    She held up a second finger.

    “Option two. I override the limit, message Carol right now, and then spend the entire night dissecting every word until my brain melts.”

    She collapsed onto the edge of the bed, rubbing her face with both hands.

    “Both options are terrible,” she muttered. “One is just faster.”

    She glanced at the phone again.

    “I could just… check,” she murmured, already going into Settings. “Just temporarily. Five minutes. That’s not a moral failing.”

    She paused.

    Decision made, she jabbed at the screen, overriding the limit with far more drama than necessary. 

    Instagram opened.

     

    New friend request.

     

    Zosia blinked.

    She leaned closer, as if proximity might change reality.

    The notification sat there calmly, unbothered by her emotional collapse.

    Carol Sturka sent you a friend request.

    “Oh my god.”

    She tapped into her own profile in a blind panic.

    Okay. Breathe. Profile damage check.

     

    Profile picture: Decent

    Bio: Harmless

    Record store shots: Respectable

     

    No red flags.

     

    She exhaled, shoulders dropping.

    Okay, she could work with this.

    She peeked at the phone again.

    The friend request was still there. Waiting. Patient. Like Carol wasn’t in any rush at all.

    Which somehow made it worse.

    Zosia picked the phone back up. “Don’t be a coward,” she told herself. Then she tapped Accept.

    Her opening message typed itself before she could interfere.

    Zosia: Hi… guess we’re Insta-friends now! How’s your tour going?

    Wow. Truly revolutionary, she thought.

    She didn’t have time to regret sending it.

    Carol: I’m in Florida, enjoying some alone time in my hotel room. But I won’t lie, I’m looking forward to going home.

    Zosia read that twice.

    My hotel room. Not ours. Not we. My.

    It was a small thing. But small things mattered.

    Zosia: When does the tour end?

    Carol: Three more days.

    No follow-up, no return question, nothing to pull her further in.

    The silence stretched, and she didn’t know if that meant distance, or discipline.

    Carol: You know, you really do look like Raban in some of your photos. 

    Zosia blinked at the screen.

    So that’s what you were doing.

    She suddenly had a vivid image of Carol: shoes kicked off, jacket draped over a chair, propped against hotel pillows, Florida humidity still clinging to her hair. Lazily scrolling through her profile instead of watching whatever mindless television flickered in the background.

    The thought did something inconvenient to her pulse.

    Zosia: I still don’t see it.

    Carol: I do. But then again, I have Raban in my head. I know what Raban should look like.

    Should look like? Okay.

    Zosia: I have a strong opinion about this.

    Carol: So do I, Pirate-Lady.

    There was a pause.

    Zosia: What happened to music-girl?

    Carol: I like Pirate-Lady better. It suits you.

    Zosia hesitated, then typed,

    Zosia: I’m not sure that’s settled yet.

    A beat.

    Carol: Good night, Pirate-Lady.

    Zosia: Good night, Miss Sturka.

    Carol: Miss Sturka?

    Carol: Noted.

    Zosia bit her bottom lip. There was something there.

     

    ——

     

    Carol didn’t message again that night. Or the next day.

    Which meant Zosia had almost twenty-four hours to imagine what “Noted” meant.

    Maybe it meant nothing.

    Maybe it meant everything.

    By Friday, the not knowing felt intentional. So she stopped waiting.

    Which was how she ended up standing in front of her closet, debating heels like it was a tactical decision.

    She hadn’t been inside a club in over three years. She preferred vinyl and the soft scratch of a needle to neon lights and sweat-slick floors. And yet… here she was.

    She had tried on three different outfits, rejected two, and settled on a black blouse with a subtle shimmer and comfortable jeans. Simple, yet functional enough to survive dancing or, worse, social interaction.

    Her phone buzzed on the counter. She froze.

    An Instagram notification. Not Carol. She opened it anyway. Iinevitably ending up on Carol’s feed.

    The latest post: a sun-bleached Florida beach.

    She checked the comments. Helen had already reshared it.

    So much for alone time.

     

    ——

     

    By ten, the club was in full swing. The music hit her the second she stepped inside, bass rattling through her ribs. Koumba was already there, waving wildly, an unrestrained grin plastered across his face.

    “Zos! My hero! You made it!” he shouted over the music, practically dancing in place. “You look amazing. I honestly thought you’d chickened out.”

    Zosia laughed nervously, holding her clutch close. “I almost did. Twice.”

    He grabbed her hand, spinning her toward the crowded dance floor. “No backing out. Tonight we dance, we mingle, and someone might buy you a drink that tastes like regret.”

    “Regret is expensive,” she shot back.

    “Good,” he grinned. “You look like you need the top shelf kind.”

    At the bar, neon light cut everyone into sharp pink and blue angles. He ordered two tequila shots and a round of something fluorescent that came in shallow glasses rimmed with sugar.

    “What is that?” Zosia asked.

    “Probably a very bad decision,” he said. “Drink.”

    She did.

    It burned bright and sweet and dangerous. Tequila loosened its fingers in her chest almost instantly. The second sip went down easier. By the time she finished it, her shoulders had dropped an inch. The world felt less sharp.

    He grabbed her hand again and dragged her toward the dance floor.

    People were sweating, flashing lights, neon strobes. Someone bumped into her and sloshed beer across her wrist. She didn’t even flinch. She clung to the rhythm, swaying her hips awkwardly at first, then letting herself be pulled in by Koumba’s reckless energy.

    Another drink appeared in her hand. Maybe vodka? She didn’t remember accepting it.

    A girl with glitter streaked across her cheek leaned close, shouting over the music,

    “You look like you’re holding something back!”

    Zosia blinked, then laughed, breath warm with vodka. “I probably am!”

    The girl shook her head, smiling like she’d caught her in the act.

    “Don’t. No one here knows you. Let it go.”

    The girl grabbed her hand and pulled her closer to the center, bodies pressing in from all sides. Zosia didn’t pull away. Someone’s hands landed at her waist, respectful but warm. She took another swallow, tipped her head back, let the bass thud through her ribs.

    It was loud.

    It was warm.

    It was easy.

    And then her phone buzzed.

    Her stomach sank. Please, not now. She glanced at the screen. Then looked properly.

    Carol: I see we’re embracing the club scene. That doesn’t look like your natural habitat. But you look… comfortable.

    Zosia froze. She scrolled back. 

    Tagged.

    Of course. Koumba uploaded in real time like it was a competitive sport.

    “Koumba!” she shouted, grabbing his sleeve. “Stop tagging me!”

    Koumba shot her a wide can’t hear you, don’t care grin, then immediately redirected his attention to a woman beside him, already leaning in.

    Another notification.

    A comment on a different photo. This one closer. Glitter-girl’s hand on her waist. Zosia laughing, head tipped back.

    Carol: That one especially.

    Zosia’s fingers hovered over the keyboard. Do I act casual? Pretend I’m not mortified?

    Finally, she typed.

    Zosia: In my defense, I was being lured with tequila. And I have no idea who that is. 

    Almost immediately, a reply came.

    Carol: Hmm. She seems very sure of you, Pirate-Lady.

    Zosia snorted despite herself, shaking her head.

    “Unbelievable,” she muttered under her breath. Pirate-Lady. 

    Zosia: You don’t think she’s a good match for me?

    She leaned against the bar as she sent it, lifting her drink to her mouth but not actually sipping.

    Carol: Tough one. What do you think?

    She glanced up.

    Glitter-girl was dancing with someone else now.

    Zosia: If she were a good match… I’d be paying attention to her right now.

    She stared at the screen after sending it, her pulse beating a little faster than the music.

    Within seconds, the three little dots appeared.

    Carol: Careful, Pirate-Lady. You’re in dangerous territory.

    Zosia’s jaw tightened, but she smiled anyway.

    Dangerous territory. Good.

    Zosia: Just testing the waters.

    She pushed off the bar, turning slightly away from the crowd like she needed physical space to hold the conversation steady.

    The dots appeared again.

    Carol: Pirates don’t test, they conquer.

    She looked at her phone with the biggest grin on her face. Which got instantly noticed by Koumba.

    He leaned in, eyes narrowing with delight.

    “Let me guess,” he shouted over the music. “Carol?”

    “You posted that on purpose, didn’t you?” Zosia hissed, clutching her phone like it was a ticking bomb.

    “It may have been an accidental slip of the thumb,” he said innocently, already stretching to read her screen. “Did it work?”

    He scanned the message and let out a delighted noise.

    “Ohhh. Okay.” He beamed. “We did it.”

    He lifted his hand for a high five.

    “I am not high-fiving you in a club over this,” Zosia said flatly.

    She looked back at her phone, thumbs hovering, heat creeping up her neck.

    Zosia quickly put her phone away before she said something reckless. Assuming she hadn’t already.

    Chapter 5: Dangerously gay

     

    The next morning, Zosia regretted what she suspected had been at least a fifth cocktail. The first few drinks were still clear in her memory. After that, everything blurred together, her confidence rising as her judgment steadily declined.

    Her head ached as she stared at the ceiling, going over last night’s messages one by one. In the dim light of the club, they had felt witty and controlled. Now they felt reckless.

    She groaned and rolled onto her side, burying her face in the pillow, until her eyes drifted to the clock.

    09:12

    “Shit.”

    She shot upright, heart racing.

    Within minutes she was under lukewarm water, trying to wake herself into coherence while mentally calculating just how late she already was. She dressed without thinking, grabbed the first vaguely clean clothes she could find, brushed her teeth at alarming speed, and ran out the door with damp hair and mismatched socks.

    If she moved fast enough, she could still make it before 10:00.

    She stopped at the bakery on the corner for coffee and a croissant, then reached the record store slightly breathless and powered more by adrenaline than caffeine. She unlocked the door and flipped the sign to ‘Open’, letting the familiar motion calm her down.

    Her phone buzzed.

    She went still, breath catching.

    For half a heartbeat, she let herself believe it was Carol.

    But it was only an inventory reminder.

    The least romantic notification imaginable.

    Zosia rubbed her temples. “Okay,” she muttered. “Focus.”

    She set her bag down and went straight for the newest shipment, thumbing through vinyl sleeves with careful precision. The routine helped. Sorting was predictable. Cataloging was controllable. Records didn’t flirt.

    Even as she scanned barcodes and straightened displays, Carol’s words lingered at the edges of her thoughts.

    Measured. Guarded. Weighted with subtext.

    Her phone rested in her pocket, suddenly too noticeable to ignore.

    She told herself she wouldn’t check it. But she did.

    No notifications.

    Her thumb hovered for a moment anyway before she opened Instagram.

    Her eyes went straight to Carol’s profile without hesitation, muscle memory already stronger than her restraint. She didn’t even pretend she was browsing.

    No new message, post or story.

    The quiet of it pressed in more than it should have.

    She dropped into the chair and pressed her lips together, drumming her fingers once against the counter before going still.

    “Idiot,” she muttered under her breath.

    The night replayed with uncomfortable clarity. Club lights flashing gold and blue, drinks she barely remembered ordering, glitter inexplicably clinging to her cheekbone. And Carol. Her dry timing. The way a single sentence from her could shift the air in a room, tilt the ground just slightly off center.

    How did someone manage to be that casually devastating?

    Zosia pushed herself upright and headed for the jazz section, sliding records out and back in with unnecessary care, aligning the edges until they were perfectly flush.

    Precision, she told herself. Control.

    It didn’t help.

    Carol’s smirk surfaced anyway. The faint lift at the corner of her mouth, the way she could make something sound entirely neutral and somehow turn it into a confession.

    The bell above the door chimed, jolting her out of the spiral.

    “Excuse me,” a customer said gently, careful not to disturb the hush of the shop. She held up an empty space in the J section. “Do you happen to have Blue by Joni Mitchell?”

    Zosia blinked, heart leaping for reasons that had nothing to do with folk music. “Yes. Yes, of course,” she said quickly, almost too quickly, guiding the woman to the right shelf with rehearsed calm.

    She smiled. Made small talk. Recommended a pressing. Nodded at the appropriate intervals.

    All while her thoughts pulsed with a single name.

    By the time the customer left, she felt strangely exposed, as if everyone in the store could see exactly where her mind had been wandering.

    She drifted back behind the counter and leaned against it, phone resting just within reach. She didn’t pick it up immediately, that would have been admitting something. But she could feel its presence, quiet and charged.

    Not yet, she told herself.

    Still, every instinct in her body urged her to reach out, to send something light and clever, to recreate the current that had hummed between them the night before.

    And maybe it was the lingering alcohol still softening her edges, or maybe it was simply the silence stretching too long, but eventually she gave in.

    She opened the chat before she could overthink it.

    Zosia: Sorry about yesterday. I had a few drinks and didn’t want to overstep. Putting my phone away felt like the responsible choice.

    She read it twice, considered deleting it, then hit send anyway.

    And waited.

    The screen remained still. No typing dots. No immediate reassurance.

    Annoyed with herself for caring so visibly, she slipped the phone back into her pocket and forced her attention onto work.

    Saturdays were merciless, which helped. Customers filtered in and out. Questions about pressings, collector’s editions, reissues. It was something she could answer. Something she could manage.

    After almost 45 long minutes, her phone buzzed.

    Carol: Good morning. You weren’t overstepping. Yet.

    Zosia’s pulse ticked upward. Yet?

    She typed before she could edit herself into safety.

    Zosia: That sounds less like reassurance and more like a challenge.

    She hit send, and instantly questioned every word.

    Too revealing?

    Too coy?

    Too much?

    The reply came quicker this time.

    Carol: Only if you’re thinking of accepting it. Or would that be you overstepping?

    Zosia stared at the message, warmth creeping slowly up her neck.

    Oh, come on. What more evidence do you need?

    Her inner voice had taken on Koumba’s tone now. Dry, unimpressed, impatient. 

    Zosia: I don’t know. Am I?

    Carol: You’re still in the safe zone.

    That wasn’t caution.

    That was encouragement.

    Zosia’s heart kicked hard against her ribs, heat rushing up her neck before she could stop it. She looked up from her phone, and nearly collided with a customer standing directly in front of the counter.

    “I’m so sorry,” she blurted, dropping the phone toward the counter as if she’d been caught doing something she shouldn’t. “I was just…”

    The woman smiled kindly, holding up a Frank Sinatra record between them like evidence.

    “Don’t worry, dear. We’ve all been there,” she said, tapping the sleeve lightly. “Trust me. Ol’ Blue Eyes here was my first love.” She gave Zosia a conspiratorial wink.

    Zosia let out a breath that turned into a reluctant laugh. “I’m not…” She stopped herself. Who was she kidding? “Yeah,” she admitted. “It makes us act like idiots, doesn’t it?”

    The woman’s smile softened with the confidence of experience. “It does, dear,” she said gently. “And somehow we keep signing up for it anyway.”

    With that, she carried the record to the register, leaving Zosia standing there with her pulse still uneven and her phone warm in her hand.

    The air left her lungs in a quiet rush.

    Back to work.

    The next hour passed in a blur of customers and questions. Pressings, reissues, collectors’ editions. The steady rhythm of transactions grounding her in something tangible. She answered confidently, recommended albums, wrapped purchases in brown paper with practiced ease.

    This was the part she understood.

    This was the life she had built.

    And yet, beneath every transaction, there was the echo of her. Carol had been here. Had stood at that counter. Had crossed this floor. The awareness lingered like something unfinished.

     

    ————-

     

    By the end of the day, Zosia let herself collapse on the couch into the quiet of her apartment, trading denim for soft cotton and pouring a cup of tea she barely tasted before reaching for Carol’s newest book. She was already halfway through.

    Outside, the city blurred into a distant hum. Inside, pages turned steadily beneath her fingers. Lucasia made yet another catastrophically questionable decision, and Zosia groaned into her mug before smiling despite herself.

    She held out for a few more chapters, pretending discipline, but the pull was inevitable. Eventually she set the book face-down, reached for her phone, and hovered over the chat long enough to feel that small, electric flutter beneath her ribs before opening it.

    Zosia: Okay. Honest question. Why is Lucasia so wildly incompetent? Why does she need rescuing every single time?

    The typing dots appeared almost immediately.

    Too fast.

    Suspiciously fast.

    Carol: Maybe she just wants to see Raban?

    Zosia snorted, the sound sharp in the quiet room.

    Zosia: Couldn’t she just send him a message like, “Hi, I want you. Please stop letting me almost die.” This behavior is getting dangerous.

    The dots reappeared.

    Paused.

    Vanished.

    Nothing.

    Her stomach tightened.

    This wasn’t a book club forum. This was the actual author. A real woman. With an ego. With boundaries. And she might have just crossed one.

    Zosia lifted her tea as if she were completely unbothered, staring resolutely at the opposite wall while watching the screen from the corner of her eye.

    It lit up.

    Carol: Sometimes, you can’t say the things you really want to say.

    Zosia went still.

    That didn’t feel literary.

    That felt personal.

    Zosia: So this is more of an autobiography disguised as fiction?

    The reply came without delay.

    Carol: That’s one way to put it.

    Heat climbed up Zosia’s neck.

    Zosia: Hypothetically… would you be Lucasia or Raban in this case?

    Carol: Lucasia, obviously.

    Is it?

    Zosia: So what would you do to get Raban’s attention?

    This time the dots lingered, blinking thoughtfully, and for a fleeting second she imagined Carol hesitating, which felt unfair. Writers weren’t supposed to struggle with words.

    Carol: I’d do something indirect. Convince myself I needed an excuse. Invent a reason to be where they were. Something harmless. Something that didn’t give too much away. Like showing up at work.

    A beat. 

    Her pulse thudded loudly enough to feel audible.

    That wasn’t subtle.

    The conversation had shifted, almost imperceptibly, from playful to pointed.

    This was the part where things stopped being fun and started having consequences.

    And she knew exactly what consequences felt like.

    The memory rose before she could stop it.

    Her first girlfriend, the breakup, the crying at her grandmother’s kitchen table. The quiet certainty in her grandma’s voice: Someday you’ll find a woman who stays. And hopefully one day you will be able to marry her. 

    She had believed her then. Or at least, she wanted to.

    Now, years later, the thought of that kind of heartbreak still felt sharp, like pressing on a bruise to see if it hurt. It did. Enough that she knew she wasn’t eager to test her resilience again.

    Her fingers moved before she could stop them.

    Zosia: Carol. What are we doing?

    Instant regret, but necessary.

    Carol: You know the answer to that.

    Zosia groaned, half-laughing, half-frustrated.

    Zosia: Be honest. Are you gay? Like. Even a little?

    Almost immediately, the three little dots appeared. Zosia’s stomach twisted, she was sure this was going to be the end.

    Then Carol’s reply popped up.

    Carol: Full disclosure: I’m very, very gay. Dangerously so.

    Zosia stared at the screen, heart sprinting. 

    For a split second she just sat there. Then she was on her feet, pacing once, twice, before dropping back onto the couch with a disbelieving laugh that sounded nothing like her.

    The phone rested in her hands again, suddenly heavier. Important.

    This time, she didn’t rush.

    Her thumbs moved slowly, drawing out the pleasure of it, savoring the fact that she could.

    Zosia: Dangerously gay… hmm. Then I have to ask, how does that work with the Wycaro storyline?

    Carol: That’s a story for another time.

    Zosia: Fair.

    Carol: Is there anything else you’d like to know?

    She hesitated. 

    Before this went any further, she needed one thing clarified.

    Her fingers stilled above the screen. Helen’s name pressed at the edges of her thoughts. Unspoken. Simply waiting.

    She could ignore it. Pretend it didn’t matter. Keep the flirting light and clever, as if the only thing at stake were wordplay and nautical metaphors.

    But it wasn’t harmless anymore.

    And she had no intention of stepping into something unfinished while convincing herself she hadn’t seen the warning signs. She had done that once before, believed chemistry could outrun history. It couldn’t.

    If there was still someone else, she needed to know before she let herself move forward.

    Her thumb brushed the edge of her phone as she considered asking directly.

    Are you single?

    Too blunt.

    Are you available?

    Too exposed.

    She inhaled slowly, steadying herself, and let her mind circle back to the one word that had threaded its way through their conversation since the beginning. Overstep. A shared language, a door she could open without sounding desperate.

    Zosia: If I were to… overstep, would that create problems for you?

    It wasn’t possessive, it was a boundary check disguised as flirtation.

    The typing dots appeared almost at once, and her stomach tightened in response, only to drop again when they vanished just as quickly.

    They reappeared.

    Paused.

    Disappeared again.

    She imagined Carol pausing over the question, choosing her words carefully.

    When the screen finally lit up, she braced herself before even reading it.

    Carol: Privately? No.

    The relief was instant. She hadn’t realized how tense she’d been until she felt herself loosen.

    But the message wasn’t finished.

    Carol: Professionally… I’d have to be careful.

    She read it again, considering the line between private and professional. 

    Her mind drifted back to that first night. The reading, the street, the door closing behind them.

    Zosia: You weren’t being careful when you followed me into that bar.

    Carol: No. I wasn’t.

     

    Chapter 6: Carol's POV

    Chapter Notes

    Thank you all so much for the amazing feedback! I love reading your comments. They mean a lot.

    I saw some requests for Carol’s POV, and since I had already written parts of it in earlier drafts, I thought… let’s just go for it! :)
    Her POV will probably pop up again later on.

    I’m not sure I’ll manage daily chapters this week, but I’ll do my best.

    Enjoy!

     

    ** Two Months Earlier **

     

    Carol’s POV

     

    Airports had a way of erasing time.

    Carol felt it as soon as the tour started. Everything looked the same. Bright airport gates, the sound of rolling suitcases, one city blending into the next. Cities became hotel keys, mic checks, and polite applause.

    The repetition did something strange to her memory. It blurred the present with the past. Made it harder to tell what had actually changed.

    It had been two years since she signed the divorce papers.

    Two years since she and Helen stopped being wives.

    And yet here they were.

    Side by side in business class, knees almost brushing. Sharing car rides. Sharing late dinners after readings while journalists asked about deadlines and inspiration. Never once suspecting that the woman managing her schedule had once shared her bed.

    They had separate hotel rooms. That had been agreed upon in advance.

    It was supposed to be easy.

    It wasn’t.

    The first week of the tour passed in a blur of interviews and packed auditoriums. Helen moved through it with the same practiced ease she always had. Sharp, articulate, charming when required. Carol knew her rhythms too well. The slight shift in posture before she disagreed. The way she pressed her lips together to hold back a smile. The subtle glance before answering a difficult question, a silent ‘are we aligned'?

    They still were.

    That was the problem.

    At night, Carol would return to her room and sit on the edge of the bed, staring at the quiet city beyond the glass. The divorce had been rational. Necessary. The love had thinned into something functional. They had stayed longer than they should have. Not out of passion, but held in place by history and the weight of everything they had built together.

    It had not been explosive. It had been erosion.

    By the third week, the exhaustion set in. A smaller city. A modest but enthusiastic crowd. A long Q&A that drifted into nostalgia.

    After the reading, they went out with the local organizers. Wine flowed too freely. Laughter came too easily. It felt like the early days, when success was new, when everything between them had spark.

    Carol watched Helen across the table as she leaned in to tell a story, her hand brushing Carol’s forearm without thinking. Habit.

    Back at the hotel, the elevator ride stretched longer than it should have, the small space holding too much silence between them.

    They stood side by side, close enough to feel the presence of each other’s warmth, but not touching.

    Carol’s floor came first, the soft chime breaking through the silence.

    She didn’t step out.

    Helen turned her head slightly. “You missed your...”

    “I know,” Carol said, too quickly, the answer landing before the sentence was finished.

    For a moment, neither of them moved.

    The doors slid closed.

    They didn’t speak in the hallway.

    It happened without ceremony, without dramatic confession or grand declaration.

    The hotel door shut with a soft click, and the world narrowed instantly to the dim glow of the room and the heat between them. Helen didn’t hesitate. She stepped forward and pushed Carol gently but firmly back against the door, kissing her with a certainty that left no room for second thoughts.

    “I’ve missed you,” Helen whispered, her voice low and unguarded.

    For a split second, something steadier inside Carol tried to surface. The rational voice that remembered late-night discussions and the private grief of dividing a life into two smaller ones. But the wine and the familiar weight of Helen’s hands at her waist pressed that voice down until it became little more than background noise.

    The kiss unfolded with practiced ease, their bodies adjusting without instruction, guided by repetition rather than curiosity. Their hands moved confidently, and they found the bed as if by instinct. A natural descent into intimacy that required no introduction.

    Helen settled against her, moving with the kind of assurance that comes from years of knowing someone’s body by heart. Carol responded, muscle memory guiding her more than intention, her breath adjusting to match Helen’s as the rhythm between them formed with unspoken certainty. It required no effort to surrender to it.

    It felt right in the moment.

    When the intensity finally broke and left her breathless beneath Helen’s weight, Carol closed her eyes and let the warmth wash over her. It had been good. It had been easy. It had even been tender.

    But as their breathing slowed and the room grew quiet again, clarity returned with it.

    Her body was satisfied.

    Her heart was not.

     

    ---

     

    Morning came slowly, pale light slipping through the curtains and stretching across the bed in thin, delicate lines. The room still held the faint scent of linen and last night’s perfume, but the urgency was gone, replaced by a softer, heavier stillness.

    Carol woke first.

    For a moment she didn’t move. Helen was curled against her side, one arm draped across her waist, their legs tangled in a way that felt instinctive rather than intentional. The comfort was real. The closeness was real.

    But so was the finality.

    Helen stirred, pressing closer before her eyes even opened. “Morning,” she murmured, voice still thick with sleep.

    Carol’s throat tightened. “Morning.”

    Helen shifted up onto one elbow, studying her face with an expression that was softer than the night before. Hope lived there now, subtle but unmistakable.

    “That felt…” Helen began, searching. “Right.”

    Carol held her gaze. “It did,” she said honestly.

    Because it had.

    Helen didn’t miss it. The slight pause, the careful tone.

    “You regret it,” she said.

    Carol’s head lifted. “No.” The word came too fast.

    “But?” Helen pressed gently.

    “But,” Carol finished. “It can’t happen again.”

    Helen didn’t respond right away. Something tightened in her expression. It was small, yet unmistakable.

    Carol saw it then. The hurt Helen wasn’t going to say out loud.

    “It’s not because I regret it,” Carol said. “I don’t.”

    Helen nodded once.

    “It’s just…” Carol exhaled softly. “We closed this chapter a long time ago.”

    Helen let out a slow breath, steadying herself.

    “Then we leave it there,” she said.

    Carol nodded. “We leave it there.”

    Silence settled between them.

    The same ending, chosen twice.

     

    ---

     

    The next month passed in measured civility.

    They traveled. They performed. They collaborated with professional precision.

    But the dynamic had shifted.

    Helen was composed. Polite. A degree more distant.

    Carol noticed the absence of unconscious touches. The lack of lingering glances.

    It was right, because it was what she had insisted on.

    And yet some small, restless part of her refused to settle. Not because she missed Helen in the way she once had, or because she regretted the boundary. But because the stillness felt revealing.

    She began to understand something she hadn’t allowed herself to articulate.

    She hadn’t felt anything new in years.

    No spark sharp enough to interrupt her thoughts. No attraction that destabilized her. No quickened pulse that arrived without history attached to it.

    She had assumed that part of her life was simply… over.

    Then she met Zosia.

     

    ---

     

    It was an unremarkable venue in the way most independent bookstores are. Mid-sized, softly lit, tastefully curated to feel intimate without trying too hard. The air carried the soft blend of paper and dust, with coffee drifting in from the small café at the back, the low murmur of conversation folding easily into the warm light.

    Carol had done dozens of events like this. She could move through them with practiced ease now. Read a passage, pause in the right places, answer questions with measured charm. Her name written again and again until it felt detached from the person holding the pen.

    She was signing a copy when she noticed her.

    Not because she was loud. Not because she tried to stand out.

    But because she didn’t.

    Zosia stood slightly apart from the line, hands loosely wrapped around the copy of the book pressed against her ribs, watching with a kind of intent stillness that felt different from admiration. There was no breathless awe in her expression. Just focus.

    When their eyes met, something shifted.

    Zosia froze.

    For a brief moment, she looked startled, as if she hadn’t expected to be noticed at all, and then her gaze fell quickly to the book in her hands.

    That was the first thing Carol registered.

    The second was the sensation in her own chest. Sudden and undeniable enough that she nearly lost her place on the page she was signing.

    There it is.

    Not history.

    Not rehearsal.

    A pull.

    Something undeniably alive.

    By the time Zosia stood in front of her, Carol had the uneasy sense that she had already made a choice.

    Zosia’s composure faltered almost immediately. Her voice caught on the first word, and she laughed softly at herself, color rising along her cheekbones. The nervousness wasn’t polished or charming. It was real. And that rawness made the pull inside Carol sharpen with startling clarity.

    She felt the difference. 

    This is what it’s supposed to feel like.

    Their exchange was brief and entirely polite. Names. A quiet thank you. The soft glide of pen across paper.

    But beneath it ran something charged and unmistakable.

    Suddenly, the past month rearranged itself.

    The night with Helen. The morning after. The guarded, practiced boundary they had both pretended was enough.

    It hadn’t felt wrong because Helen was wrong.

    It had felt wrong because it was finished.

    Completely.

    And Carol had been circling that truth without naming it, mistaking habit for meaning, mistaking comfort for connection.

    Zosia thanked her and stepped away.

    Carol watched her go. Watched her push open the bookstore door. 

    She saw her pause outside, just long enough to suggest consideration, before turning toward the bar across the street.

    Helen stood a few feet away, mid-conversation with someone from the event, laughing at something Carol didn’t catch.

    Carol barely noticed.

    Two realizations became clear in an instant.

    First, she needed to prove, to herself as much as to Helen, that what had happened weeks ago had been nostalgia. Not revival. She needed to feel something that wasn’t borrowed from memory.

    And second, she was already drawn to Zosia with an intensity that startled her.

    It was immediate.

    Carol excused herself before she could reconsider.

    Helen noticed. Of course she did.

    “Where are you going?” she asked, lightly, but not casually.

    “Out,” Carol replied.

    She didn’t offer an explanation. She just crossed the street without hesitation.
