    The bar was dimmer than the bookstore, softer in a different way. Shadows pooling along the walls, low music blending with the muted clink of glass against wood.

    Zosia sat alone with a cup of coffee and her phone, her gaze lowered, shoulders slightly drawn in as though she were collecting herself.

    Carol felt the difference the moment she stepped inside.

    This wasn’t careful. It wasn’t strategic. It certainly wasn’t professional.

    But it was honest.

    It felt impulsive in a way she hadn’t allowed herself in years.

    She crossed the room before she could talk herself out of it.

    “Is this seat taken?” she finally asked.

    Zosia glanced up, startled, stumbling over her first word before answering, “It’s free.”

    And in that small exchange, something took hold.

    Something new.

    She wasn’t rehearsing explanations. She wasn’t thinking about Helen.

    She was thinking about how alive she felt walking toward her, and how unwilling she suddenly was to step back.

     

     

    ** Present day **

     

    Zosia: You weren’t being careful when you followed me into that bar.

    Carol: No. I wasn’t.

    She hadn’t been careful that day. She had no idea what she was walking into. All she knew was she had to chase that feeling. The instant attraction. The soft flutter in her chest. The simple, almost innocent pull of wanting to stay close a little longer.

    And beneath that, nothing planned or calculated. Just the steady certainty that if she let Zosia walk away without following, she wouldn’t forgive herself for it.

    She read the message again and reached for her glass. 

    She took a measured sip of whiskey and set it down carefully, as if precision in that small movement could make up for the risk she had just taken.

    This was the point where Zosia could retreat. Laugh it off. Decide it had crossed into something real.

    Carol felt the vulnerability move through her like a slow electric pulse. She hadn’t done this in years. Not without hiding behind irony, not without leaving herself a way out. Not since before the interviews and the calculated control, before she learned to keep her personal life neatly contained.

    More than a decade of control.

    And now she had admitted that following Zosia into that bar had meant enough to risk the career she’d spent ten years building, for someone she had known all of five minutes.

    Carol exhaled slowly, her thumb hovering over her phone as if she could somehow brace herself for whatever came next.

    Zosia: Why?

    Her thumbs hovered for a moment before she let herself answer without pretense.

    Carol: Because you walked out.

    She paused.

    Carol: And I didn’t want you to.

    She swallowed, feeling the unfamiliar weight of it settle in her chest.

    Zosia: I’m glad you followed me.

    Relief moved through her, soft but steady. Not victory. Just the sense that she hadn’t misread everything.

    Carol: Me too.

    The warmth of the whiskey began to spread, loosening the edges of her restraint. It would be easy to keep going now, to let the conversation deepen under the cover of late-night courage.

    But she knew better.

    Carol: Listen. I know this is the worst timing. But I need to get some sleep. I have a photoshoot and the final reading tomorrow. It’s going to be a long day.

    Zosia: That deserves your full attention.

    Carol smiled at her screen.

    Zosia: And Carol, let me know how it went.

    A phone number appeared in her DMs. 

    Carol: Have we outgrown Instagram?

    Zosia: That’s entirely up to you.

    She saved the number and watched it settle into her contacts.

    Zosia.

    It looked unexpectedly intimate there. Stripped of usernames and profile pictures and public space. Just a name. Direct and personal.

    She switched apps.

    Her thumb hovered once.

    Then she typed the first text message.

    Good night.

    Zosia: Good night, Carol.

    Chapter 7: Juliette

     

    Zosia couldn’t sleep.

    At 2:20AM, the world outside her windows had gone completely still.

    She turned onto her side, then onto her back again, but the movement didn’t quiet anything. Carol’s confession kept replaying in her head.

    Because you walked out.

    And I didn’t want you to.

    She reached for her phone before she could stop herself. The screen lit the room in pale blue as she opened Carol’s last message again.

    Good night.

    That was all it said.

    No nickname. No emotional warranty attached.

    Then, just as she convinced herself she didn’t care:

     

    Carol is typing…

     

    Her pulse spiked before she could steady it.

    She watched the typing indicator. Waiting.

    The typing stopped.

    Started again.

    Stopped.

    Then… nothing.

    The blank space where a message should have appeared felt louder than any notification sound.

    Curiosity won.

    Zosia: Can’t sleep?

    Which, unfortunately, also revealed that she was very much awake and making questionable decisions.

    Carol: You saw that, didn’t you?

    She smiled despite herself.

    So it wasn’t accidental. Good. She preferred mutual instability.

    Zosia: Yes.

    Zosia: What were you going to say?

    She watched the typing bubble return.

    Carol: There’s a reason why I didn’t send it.

    That meant there was something worth sending.

    Zosia: I figured.

    Zosia: Can I change your mind?

    Her pulse kicked up. This was the part where things either deepened or pulled back.

    There was a pause.

    Carol: I don’t negotiate past midnight.

    Carol: Why are you still awake?

    Zosia shifted onto her side, cocooning herself in the blanket.

    Zosia: I was thinking about our conversation.

    Carol: Which part?

    Zosia: The part where you said you didn’t want me to walk out.

    Zosia: Why was that?

    Carol didn’t answer immediately.

    Carol: That’s not a fair question at 2:30.

    Carol: Before I say something I can’t take back, I should sleep. You should too.

    Of course.

    Zosia let the silence sit for a moment longer than necessary.

    Zosia: You’re probably right.

    Zosia: Good night, Carol.

    This time the response came quickly.

    Carol: Good night.

    No softness. Just a period.

    She set the phone down and left it there, determined not to dissect the silence between the words.

    What kept replaying in her mind wasn’t what Carol had said. It was what she hadn’t.

    And that absence felt heavier than the message itself.

     

    ——-

     

    When she opened the store the next morning, the sunlight felt almost too bright. Like it hadn’t been informed about the night before.

    Still, Sundays were the best.

    She liked the slowness of them. Getting up late. Unlocking the door without hurry. Letting the quiet settle into the shelves before the city decided to be loud again.

    She put on a soft record and let it spin. Sunlight stretched across the wooden floor, dust drifting in its path. For a while, things felt easy.

    Manageable.

    The bell above the door chimed behind her.

    Footsteps crossed the floor, slow and steady.

    “Hi.”

    Something inside her went still before her mind caught up.

    It was a voice that had followed her into sleep for months after everything ended.

    Zosia turned.

    “Juliette.”

    For a second, her mind refused to process it. “What are you doing here?”

    Juliette’s hair was longer now, braided loosely over her shoulder, the blonde brighter than it used to be. It caught the morning light. Her hazel eyes held the same quiet intensity.

    That hadn’t changed.

    “I moved back.”

    The words landed strangely.

    “Moved back when?”

    “Six months ago.”

    Zosia blinked. “Of course you did.”

    France. The place she moved to with the same ex she had sworn was 'just history'.

    “I just thought I’d let you know.”

    “After six months?”

    Juliette hesitated. “I wasn’t sure if you’d want to see me.”

    Zosia let out a short, humorless scoff. “Oh, I can assure you… I don’t.”

    The record continued playing softly behind them, absurdly calm.

    “Look,” Juliette said, lowering her voice. “I’m sorry for how things went between us.”

    Apologies always came after the damage.

    “Really? After two years together, I still don’t know if I was your girlfriend or your rebound.”

    Juliette flinched.

    Back then, before everything collapsed, Juliette had stood behind the counter on busy December afternoons. Sleeves rolled up. Calling Zosia “boss” like it meant something private.

    There had been late nights too. The store dim. Juliette’s arms around her waist, chin resting against her shoulder as if the future were settled.

    And then she’d go home and text someone else goodnight.

    “It went on for months,” Zosia snapped. “You stood behind this counter with me. You closed up with me. And then you went home and texted her.”

    Juliette’s composure slipped for a second.

    “It wasn’t that simple,” she said.

    Zosia held her gaze.

    “It actually was.”

    Silence pressed in between them.

    “I didn’t plan for it to happen like that,” Juliette added quietly.

    “That’s the problem,” Zosia replied. “You didn’t plan. You just let it happen. For almost a year.”

    Zosia felt the old humiliation stir. The quiet awareness that she had been loving someone who had one foot out the door.

    Juliette looked around the store again, and for a second Zosia couldn’t tell if she was nostalgic or assessing.

    “I didn’t come here to argue,” she said.

    Zosia tilted her head slightly. “Then what did you come for?”

    Juliette didn’t answer that.

    The record crackled softly in the background.

    For a moment, they just stood there.

    “You should go,” Zosia said finally.

    Juliette hesitated, her hand lingering near the counter, like she might say something else. Something that would complicate things further. Instead, she walked to the door before speaking.

    “I did love you,” She said quietly before leaving. “I just didn’t handle it well."

    The bell chimed again as the door closed behind her.

    Zosia watched her go.

    “Pizda pierdolona,” she said under her breath.

    The softness of Sunday had evaporated.

    She stood there longer than she meant to before finally reaching for the counter, straightening a stack of receipts that didn’t need straightening.

    When she got home, she resented how much of the day had belonged to Juliette instead of Carol. Even gone, Juliette had a way of pulling gravity toward herself, making everything else feel secondary.

    After months of being lied to, Zosia trusted patterns more than promises, and this felt familiar. Not the words, but the uncertainty beneath them.

    That was what unsettled her about Helen.

    Carol hadn’t given her a reason to doubt her. But neither had Juliette.

    Zosia rubbed her thumb against the edge of her phone before opening Instagram.

    #carolsturka

    She scrolled past a few stories.

    Wycaro. Pirates. Books.

    Then she saw it.

    Helen had posted something only a few hours ago.

    Zosia’s thumb hesitated for the briefest second before tapping.

    The photo filled the screen.

    Carol stood at the front of the room, book open mid-laugh, the audience blurred behind her.

    It was a good photo.

    Helen had captioned it simply:

    Last stop of the tour. Always nice to see old friends.

    Someone else was tagged. She tapped the name.

    The profile belonged to the bookstore owner. At first, it was all event documentation. Shelves, crowds, signings arranged under warm light.

    Further back, the tone shifted. Dinners. Birthdays. Holiday tables. Public moments giving way to something more personal.

    And then a photo from seven years ago.

    Barcelona.

    “2 weeks in Barcelona with these good people.” The caption read.

    Wine glasses raised. Helen’s arm along Carol’s shoulders with practiced ease. Carol’s hand resting over Helen’s wrist.

    Carol was smiling.

    Not the author smile.

    The private one.

    She could remember exactly how Carol had looked at her at the bar. And she had convinced herself that the intensity meant something singular. Seeing that same softness in a photograph taken years ago unsettled her more than the arm around the shoulder ever could.

    Of course Carol had a past.

    She just hadn’t expected it to look so… real.

    The phone went dark in her hand.

    “And that’s why we don’t stalk,” she muttered.

    A breath.

    Then the screen lit up again.

    Her thumb hovered over the keyboard on their latest text message, waiting for the right phrasing to appear. Something casual, something neutral, something that wouldn’t betray how long she’d been spiraling about this.

    She typed:

    Were you and Helen ever together?

    She read it once, then again, and felt her entire body tense at how direct it sounded.

    Delete.

    She tried again, attempting something looser, something that might pass as casual.

    So what’s the deal with you and Helen?

    That was worse. That sounded suspicious. Like she had been studying archived evidence for hours, which unfortunately, she had.

    Delete.

    She let out a long breath, dropped the phone back onto the table, and pressed her palms briefly over her eyes, as if she could physically push the insecurity back into something manageable.

    “This is ridiculous,” she exhaled.

    Then she did what she always did when she ran out of internal arguments, she called Koumba.

    He answered almost immediately.

    “If this is about her, I’m charging you.”

    Zosia shook her head, a smile breaking through. “I will pay for dinner the next two rounds.”

    “Three.”

    She rolled her eyes. “Fine. Three. But no expensive sushi.”

    “Acceptable. So. Talk.”

    She stood up and began pacing, because sitting still felt impossible when her thoughts were racing in overlapping circles.

    “Carol and Helen were a couple,” she said, the words feeling strange and heavier once spoken out loud.

    There was a brief pause on the other end.

    “She told you?” Koumba asked.

    “No,” Zosia said quickly. “I saw a photo. From seven years ago.”

    “You scrolled back seven years ago?”

    A beat.

    “…That’s not the point.”

    She exhaled sharply and ran a hand through her hair.

    “Their pose.” She continued. ”There’s a difference between friendly and… that.”

    Koumba hummed.

    “And of course,” he said carefully, “you asked her about it before calling me.”

    Zosia stopped pacing.

    “This is going to cost me four dinners, isn’t it?”

    “Definitely.”

    She sank down onto the couch.

    “I don’t know what to do,” she admitted, quieter now. 

    She closed her eyes. Considered stopping there.

    “And," she hesitated. "Juliette walked into the store today.”

    “Wait. What?”

    “She just… showed up. Said she moved back. Six months ago.”

    “And you’re telling me this now?”

    Zosia stared at the wall.

    “I handled it,” she said quickly. Too quickly. “It was fine.”

    “Uh-huh.”

    “She stood behind that counter for almost a year while she was still texting someone else,” Zosia said. “I’m not doing that again.”

    There it was. The real fear.

    Not Helen.

    Pattern.

    "I see," Koumba exhaled. “Has Carol done anything that suggests she’s still with Helen?”

    “No.”

    “Then maybe don’t convict her yet. And just… talk to her.”

    “I don’t want to sound insecure.”

    “You won’t,” he said. “You’ll sound like someone who knows her limits.”

    She leaned back, eyes settling somewhere neutral on the opposite wall.

    “Why are you reasonable?” she asked.

    “It’s a burden I carry for the both of us.”

    She smiled faintly.

    “I’m still getting four dinners,” Koumba reminded her.

    He informed her that appetizers were included in the four-dinner agreement and hung up before she could object.

    The apartment felt quieter after that.

     

    Zosia stayed on the couch, replaying what he’d said. He always managed to strip her dramatic version down to something practical. It helped.

    She got up. Folded the laundry that had been waiting on the chair. Reorganized a shelf that didn’t need reorganizing. Then poured herself a cup of tea.

    Progress.

    Outside, the light thinned into evening. The quiet almost felt earned.

    Her phone buzzed.

    Carol: As promised, reporting back.

    Her shoulders loosened, just slightly.

    Carol: Last day done. I’m exhausted, but I made it.

    Zosia: You must be relieved you’re flying home tomorrow.

    Carol: You have no idea.

    Zosia wanted to ask about Helen. She really did.

    But the question felt heavier than it should have. Like something that belonged to someone with more claim.

    She let it sit.

    Zosia: What time is your flight?

    Carol: 6 AM. Trust me, I tried to change it.

    She hesitated, then forced her fingers to move again.

    Zosia: You should have used your charm.

    Carol: As shocking as it may sound, it doesn’t work on everyone.

    Zosia: I’d have folded immediately.

    A pause, long enough for her to regret how honest that sounded.

    Carol: Are you trying to give me another sleepless night?

    Zosia: Maybe. I still want to know what you were going to say last night.

    There was a long pause.

    Carol: Good night, Pirate-Lady.

    And there it was.

    Softness reinstated.

    Zosia stared at the nickname for a full five seconds longer than necessary.

    Great. Now she was smiling at her phone like an idiot.

     

    Chapter 8: Couch-Carol

     

    Zosia was halfway through her second cup of coffee when the doorbell rang.

    She paused mid-sip.

    Unscheduled human interaction was not part of her Monday morning plan.

    The bell rang again, unnecessarily optimistic.

    Zosia set her mug down and padded over in socks to open the door.

    Koumba stood there, grinning like a man who had already won.

    He was holding two things:

    1. A paper bag from her favorite bakery.

    2. His phone, screen facing her, like evidence.

    “Oh no,” Zosia said immediately. “Whatever this is, I don’t consent.”

    “Good morning to you too,” Koumba replied brightly, stepping past her and into the apartment without waiting for permission.

    “I brought croissants. The good ones. Coffee. And something that’s about to dramatically improve your life.”

    She closed the door slowly. “That sentence makes me regret opening the door.”

    He set the bag on the counter, inhaled deeply. “Aaah. The smell of queer longing and poor decision-making.”

    Zosia groaned. “Why are you here?”

    “First of all,” he said, holding up a finger, “I sensed you were still spiraling.”

    “I am not spiraling.”

    He looked around the apartment. “Yesterday, you reorganized a shelf that didn’t need reorganizing.”

    “…Okay, continue.”

    “Second,” he said, waving the phone, “I did a thing.”

    Zosia eyed him suspiciously. “Koumba. The last time you ‘did a thing,’ I had to talk a bartender out of calling the police.”

    “I’ve learned from my mistakes, and I’ve grown as a human being,” he said. “Besides, this is a much better thing.”

    He turned the phone toward her.

    On the screen: a confirmation email.

    Flight booked. JFK → ABQ.

    Fully refundable.

    Zosia stared at the screen. Blinked once. Twice.

    Then she looked up at him, eyes wide. “What did you do? I did not ask for this.”

    “No,” he agreed cheerfully. “You didn’t need to. I acted in your emotional best interest.”

    Zosia pressed her palms to the counter, silently vowing never to tell him about her romantic life again. “Koumba. I have a store. A life. I cannot impulsively fly to New Mexico because of a woman I barely know. I’ve met her twice!”

    “You absolutely can,” he said. “And relax. I didn’t fully book it. It’s just a hold. You’re not flying yet. You’re considering flying.”

    She glared at him. “This is insane.”

    “And yet,” he said, sliding a croissant toward her, “you haven’t thrown me out.”

    She took it. Bit into it. Chewed angrily. “… not yet.”

    “Now, before you panic, this doesn’t mean you have to go. It just means the possibility exists.”

    She leaned back against the counter, exhaling.

    “I don’t even know what she wants,” Zosia said quietly.

    Koumba softened, just a little. “No. But you know what you want.”

    She thought of Carol’s texts. The unexpected messages. The obvious flirting. The endless refreshing of her profile, hoping for something new.

    “Fine,” she said quietly. “I want to get to know her. Beyond texts.”

    “There it is.” Koumba said gently. “That’s all this is for.”

    She looked at the phone again.

    The date was in a week. She knew she could find someone to watch the store for a few days. Heck, this might be important enough to close it.

    “Okay,” she said finally. “But if I do this and it explodes, I’m blaming you forever.”

    He grinned. “I accept full responsibility.”

    Her phone buzzed on the counter.

    Both of them looked at it. Zosia with a flicker of fear, Koumba with open anticipation.

    Zosia picked it up and turned her back to Koumba to enforce a little privacy.

    Carol: Home. I forgot how quiet Albuquerque is. It feels like the world finally stopped asking things from me.

    She felt it before she could suppress it. That small, traitorous warmth in her chest.

    Koumba clocked it instantly. He leaned in, voice lowered to a conspiratorial whisper, as if Carol might somehow hear him through the phone. “Tell her.”

    Zosia waved him off. “Go away.”

    Zosia: That sounds really good. I’m glad you’re home safe.

    She felt herself smiling, helpless against it.

    Koumba studied her for a second. “You didn’t even try to hide that.”

    “Don’t.” Zosia warned.

    “She’s texting you from her home,” he said reverently. “That’s sacred territory.”

    Zosia shot him a look but didn’t deny it.

    Her phone buzzed again.

    Carol: What are you up to today?

    Zosia glanced at Koumba. At the croissants. At the phone with Albuquerque still glowing on the screen.

    She stared at the question longer than necessary.

    What are you up to today?

    She glanced around her apartment, half-folded laundry on the chair, a mug gone cold, Koumba pacing near the window like a proud menace who knew exactly what he’d done.

    She typed.

    Zosia: Very thrilling Monday activities. Coffee. Laundry. Arguing with Koumba about boundaries. Which he does not seem to have.

    A pause.

    Carol: And how are you holding up in that battle?

    Zosia: Not sure yet.

    Zosia: What are your plans?

    Carol: I’m doing absolutely nothing. I unpacked. Threw away some dead plants that I forgot about. And now I’m sitting on my couch pretending emails don’t exist.

    Zosia: That sounds deserved.

    She hesitated, then decided to be honest. That seems to be happening more and more lately.

    Zosia: So. Slight complication.

    The dots appeared almost immediately.

    Carol: Go on.

    Zosia: Koumba may or may not have taken it upon himself to buy me a plane ticket.

    A beat.

    Carol: I’m sorry, ‘may or may not’?

    Zosia: He claims it was an “act of friendship.”

    Carol: Okay.

    Zosia: It’s for next week. Fully refundable. He said, and I quote, “I acted in your emotional best interest.”

    A longer pause this time. The dots lingered, vanished, came back.

    Carol: And where exactly does this extremely supportive friend think you are flying?

    Zosia swallowed, then typed it anyway.

    Zosia: Albuquerque.

    There it was. Not a declaration. Just a fact, set carefully between them.

    Another pause, quiet, thoughtful.

    Carol: You don’t say.

    Zosia waited, heart doing that slow, steady rhythm again.

    Zosia: I panicked for a full ten minutes after he told me.

    Carol: That honestly seems like a very reasonable amount of panic for something like this.

    Zosia snorted softly.

    Zosia: I should be clear. I didn’t ask him to do it. He just showed up with a confirmation email and a very smug face.

    Carol: I’m not sure whether to be impressed or concerned.

    Another beat.

    Carol: But as crazy as it may sound, I don’t completely hate this development.

    Zosia exhaled, relief soft and warm.

    Carol: How do you feel about it?

    She tried to pin it down. Excitement, yes. But also nerves. And something dangerously close to panic. She honestly didn’t know. They barely knew each other. And ‘barely’ was very generous.

    Zosia: I guess… it’s not the worst idea in the world.

    Carol: So, in one week you say? And for how long?

    Zosia: Sunday until Wednesday.

    Carol: Okay. Albuquerque has lovely scenery and several notable attractions.

    Silence.

    Carol: Though I’m obviously the highlight.

    Zosia laughed out loud. Carol didn’t just flirt, she committed to it.

    Now she just had to convince herself.

    Zosia: Well I’m sold.

    Carol: Is Koumba still there?

    She eyed Koumba who was still pacing back and forth between the window and the couch. Waiting for a verdict on his idiotic, but appreciated, move.

    Zosia: He is.

    Carol: Maybe we should talk about this properly. I could call you this evening?

    Her heart jumped.

    Zosia: Yes. I think we should.

    Carol: Oh and pirate-lady?

    Carol: Thank him for me.

    Zosia stared at the screen, a little stunned. Carol wasn’t annoyed. She wasn’t wary. She was, if anything, open.

    Koumba waited, barely containing himself. “Well?”

    “She didn’t hate the idea.” She laughed.

    He let out a triumphant shout and spun her around. “I knew it! I was starting to doubt myself for a second.”

    “You?” Zosia laughed as he set her down. “Doubt yourself? That’s new.”

    “Hey, this was bold. Even for me.” He grinned. “But clearly, I’m a visionary.”

    “Oh, please.”

    He waggled his eyebrows. “So. Should I start preparing my speech? Best man duties and all?”

    “Shut up,” Zosia said, smacking his arm, smiling the entire time.

     

    ——

     

    That evening, Zosia cleaned her apartment like she was expecting an inspection.

    The phone call felt like inviting Carol into her home. Which, in a way, she was.

    She folded the laundry she’d abandoned earlier. Watered the plants. Fluffed the cushions on the couch. At one point, she stood in the middle of the living room and said, out loud, “This is ridiculous.”

    Koumba, finally on his way out after spending the entire day on her couch and systematically emptying her fridge, grinned from the doorway.

    “You’re about to get a call from a woman you’re flying across the country to see,” he said. “This isn’t ridiculous. This is pre–U-Hauling.”

    “Leave,” Zosia said, pushing him toward the door.

    He kissed her cheek. “Don’t overthink it.”

    She waited until the door clicked shut before muttering, “Too late.”

    The apartment settled into quiet. Evening light slanted through the windows. Her phone lay face-up on the coffee table, patiently waiting.

    9:22 p.m.

    She told herself she wasn’t staring at the clock. She didn’t calculate the different time zones about a dozen times.

    At 9:29, her phone rang.

    Zosia inhaled once. Deep. Then again, slower this time. Useless, but she tried. Her heart was already sprinting, with no intention of pacing itself. She answered before she could overthink it.

    “Hi,” she said, voice steadier than she felt.

    Carol’s voice came through warm and unhurried, familiar already in a way that startled her.

    “Hi. I hope this is a good time. I realized I didn’t actually ask. I just assumed.”

    “It’s okay,” Zosia said quickly. “I mean, yes. It’s good. I’m glad you called.”

    A small pause. The faint sound of movement on the other end. Fabric shifting, someone settling in. Then a quiet clink from a glass against wood maybe?

    “Good,” Carol said. “Because I don’t think this is a conversation I wanted to have in tiny gray bubbles.”

    Zosia shifted on the couch, tucking her feet under herself. The apartment felt suddenly too quiet.

    “Me neither,” she said, aiming for casual and landing somewhere honest. “You sound… different. Not stage-Carol. Not text-Carol.”

    Carol hummed softly. “Yeah. This is couch-Carol. Less impressive. Drinks whiskey and talks to her houseplants.”

    Zosia smiled, sinking onto the couch. “Plants are good listeners.”

    Carol chuckled, low, easy. Zosia’s heart skipped, annoyingly pleased with itself.

    “I was a little nervous to call.” She admitted. “Which feels ridiculous, considering I talk in front of rooms full of strangers for a living.”

    There was a soft clink on the other end. Definitely a glass this time.

     “Whiskey?” Zosia asked, smiling.

    “Liquid courage.”

    “Same,” Zosia admitted, lifting her glass of wine. “This is the second most nerve-wracking thing I’ve done in a while.”

    A pause.

    “Do I want to know the first?” Carol asked.

    Zosia laughed under her breath. “Opening the door to your book reading last time.”

    Carol laughed, genuinely surprised. “Really? Why?” 

    Zosia hesitated. The truth pressed against her ribs, impatient.

    “Because I was…” She stopped herself. “Never mind.”

    “What?” Carol prompted, smiling in her voice.

    Zosia took a deep breath.

    “Because I was curious about you… sorry, that sounds weird.”

    On the other end, Carol shifted, maybe smiling. 

    “No,” she said gently. “That’s actually kind of sweet.”

    There was a brief, charged silence after that.

    The word lingered.

    “So,” Carol asked casually, “have you confirmed your ticket yet?”

    “Do you really want me to?”

    “I do. And I hope you will.” She paused. “There’s just one thing. I have a reading on Tuesday followed by a dinner.”

    Zosia smiled, slow and real. “That’s ok. You don’t have to entertain me.”

    “No I mean,” she continued. “You can join me if you’d like. To the reading and the dinner.”

    Another pause slipped in. 

    “Oh.” Zosia blinked. “Yeah. If it’s not a bother, I’d like that.”

    “Okay then.”

    This one stretched. Long enough for Zosia to become acutely aware of the phone in her hand, of her own breathing, of how far away Albuquerque actually was. Long enough to feel the shift from planning, to possibility.

    “Where will you be staying?” Carol asked.

    There it was. 

    The question that had been hovering all day. Somehow more intimidating than crossing the country.

    Zosia didn’t answer immediately. She pulled her knees to her chest, curling her toes into the couch as if it could hold her in place.

    She needed proof she was still here. Still in New York. Not already halfway across the country, about to do something wildly out of character.

    “I am going to book something downtown.” she said finally. “Do you have recommendations?”

    Carol didn’t answer right away. Zosia imagined she was choosing her words carefully.

    “Well,” she said finally, “downtown is fine. Good coffee. Decent bars. Very… neutral.”

    Zosia smiled at the word. “Neutral sounds good.”

    “It is.” Carol agreed. “It’s also what you book when you’re trying to keep your distance.”

    Zosia shifted on the couch, tucking one foot under the other. 

    “And what’s the alternative?” She asked.

    There was a quiet exhale on the other end of the line, warm and human.

    “I have a guest room,” Carol said, her tone stayed casual. “It’s small. Clean. Just… an option.”

    Zosia went very still.

    “That’s generous,” she said carefully.

    “Hmm,” Carol replied. “It’s also practical. Cheaper. And you’ll have better coffee in the mornings.”

    Zosia shifted again, tucking the phone closer to her ear like that might shorten the distance.

    “I wasn’t fishing for an invitation,” she said, lightly but honestly.

    Carol laughed under her breath, not teasing, just real.

    “I know,” she said. “You don’t have to decide now. Just promise me you’ll think about it.”

    Zosia closed her eyes, letting the sound of Carol’s voice settle somewhere warm in her chest.

    “I promise.”

    Carol hummed, satisfied.

    Another quiet moment stretched between them. Zosia could hear the faint sounds of Carol’s apartment. Something shifting, the clink of a glass being set down. Ordinary noises. Intimate ones.

    “You sound tired.” Zosia said gently.

    “Jet lag. I should probably call it a night. Boring, I know.”

    “I don’t mind boring.” Zosia replied.

    Carol let out a quiet breath, the kind that sounded like a smile.

    “Get some sleep,” Zosia added. “We’ll talk soon.”

    There was a pause. Long enough for Zosia to picture Carol hesitating, weighing stubbornness against being cared for.

    “Okay,” she said finally. “On one condition.”

    Zosia smiled, already bracing herself. “I’m listening.”

    “Think about my offer.”

    And long after the call ended, Zosia lay awake, already rearranging her life around a guest room in Albuquerque.

     

    Chapter 9: No expectations

     

    “You’re doing what?” Joanna yelled in disbelief.

    Zosia sat there, waiting for her sister to calm down.

    “You cannot just step onto a plane and fly across the country to meet someone you have met twice!” She stood up, pacing the living room. “And this is the woman from the Wycaro books?”

    Zosia could only nod.

    “Are you out of your mind?” she snapped, throwing her hands up. “What the hell are you thinking? You barely know her! She could be anyone. She could be dangerous!”

    Zosia let out an involuntary snort.

    “Don’t laugh,” Joanna snapped. “I’m serious.” She started pacing again, faster now. “You are absolutely not telling Mom and Dad about this.”

    “I won’t,” Zosia said quietly, the words small but steady.

    “I’m serious, Zos,” Joanna said, her voice cracking between anger and something that sounded a lot like fear. “Why are you doing this? What’s the rush?”

    It was the same question Zosia had been turning over in her own head for days now. In the quiet moments, in the middle of the night, in every pause between Carol’s messages.

    “Because… she lives too far away,” Zosia said, struggling to put something instinctive into words. “There’s no chance of just bumping into her. No accidental coffee. No slow, normal build-up. It’s either this or nothing. And if I don’t go now…” Her voice thinned. “I’ll talk myself out of it. And then I’ll never go.”

    She inhaled slowly, steadying herself.

    “I just have this feeling,” she said, quieter now but no less certain. “Like I need to do this.”

    “A feeling?” Joanna exploded, staring at her as if she’d just confessed to something criminal. “You’re putting your life on the line for a feeling?” Her hands flew up again in disbelief. “Do you even hear yourself?”

    “Don’t be so dramatic,” Zosia muttered.

    “I am not being dramatic!” Joanna shot back instantly. “Have you not watched a single one of those serial killer documentaries I keep sending you? This is exactly how they start!”

    Zosia hadn’t. Not one of them.

    “I am not letting you get on that plane,” Joanna said, pressing her fingers to the bridge of her nose like she was fighting off a migraine. The anger drained from her face, leaving something rawer behind. “Please, Zos. Just… don’t do this.”

    Zosia gave a small, helpless shrug, but her eyes stayed steady.

    “But I want to.”

    Joanna exhaled sharply, the fight draining out of her posture. She looked at Zosia like she was a stranger wearing her sister’s face.

    “And where exactly are you staying?”

    “I don’t know yet,” Zosia said. She could have mentioned the two options she’d been carefully comparing in her head for days, but she knew it wouldn’t help.

    “Just book a hotel. Downtown. Somewhere safe. Not some creepy roadside motel with flickering lights and stained carpets.”

    Zosia didn’t answer.

    Joanna’s eyes narrowed. “You’re staying at her place, aren’t you?”

    Silence.

    “You are unbelievable.” Joanna let her arms fall to her sides. “You are the least impulsive person I know. You make pro and con lists for buying shampoo. What is happening to you?”

    “Listen,” Zosia said, forcing calm into her voice. “I haven’t decided anything yet. She offered me the guest room. I told her I’d think about it. That’s all.”

    She tried to sound calm and rational, like this was all under control. Joanna’s face told her she hadn’t succeeded.

    “Anyway,” Zosia said, pushing ahead before doubt could catch up with her. “Back to my original question.” She hesitated, then added carefully, “Would you be able to watch the store for a day or two?” A beat. “Koumba said he’d help as well.”

    “I cannot believe I’m agreeing to this,” she muttered. “But yes. I’ll do it.”

    Zosia couldn’t help the small smile that tugged at her mouth.

    “Thank you,” she said softly.

     

    ——

     

    The days unfolded normally. Too normally, considering how much was changing beneath the surface.

    At first, nothing dramatic happened at all.

    They texted.

    Not constantly, or obsessively. Just regularly. Casual morning check-ins. A photo of Zosia’s coffee gone wrong. A dry comment from Carol about something she saw on TV.  Small observations. Tiny jokes. Growing familiarity.

    She asked about the flight once, lightly, as if it were nothing more than a practical detail. Then about food. What did Zosia like? Did she prefer museums or long walks? Restaurants with dim lighting and too many courses, or quiet evenings cooking at home?

    Carol wasn’t just chatting. She was shaping the visit. Adjusting things quietly, already expecting Zosia to be there.

    Zosia, meanwhile, booked a hotel.

    Downtown. Sensible. Refundable.

    The kind of booking you make to prove to yourself, more than anyone, that you are still a responsible adult who does not fly across the country and move into a near-stranger’s home on instinct alone.

    She left the confirmation untouched, a small insurance policy she wasn’t sure she wanted to cash in. As long as she didn’t click it, the decision remained theoretical.

    Two nights before the flight, Zosia finally cracked. She was sitting cross-legged on her bed, suitcase open, containing nothing but socks.

    Zosia: I’m still torn. I booked a hotel. It feels smart. But I hate the idea of staying there.

    The dots appeared. Stopped. Appeared again.

    Carol: Okay. And how do you feel about staying here? In the guest room.

    Zosia: Better. And slightly terrifying.

    Carol: Want to talk me through it? Maybe I can help.

    She read the last message again, then started answering.

    Zosia: Okay. I’m just going to be honest. I’ve been thinking about you more than I probably should.

    She stared at the message before sending it, thumb hovering like she might still take it back. She didn’t.

    Zosia: Part of me wants to get on a plane and stay at your place. Which, objectively, sounds reckless, considering I don’t actually know you. Not properly. So yes, the smart choice would be a hotel.

    She paused, reading it over, feeling the weight of her own logic.

    Zosia: The problem is… I don’t want the smart choice. But if this were anyone else, I’d tell them to book a hotel without hesitation.

    After a few seconds, her phone rang.

    Carol.

    Zosia didn’t hesitate. She answered on the first ring.

    “Hi.”

    “Hi.”

    “I’m sorry.” Zosia started.

    Carol exhaled softly on the other end.

    “Okay,” she said. “What do you want to know?”

    Zosia went quiet.

    “You said you don’t really know me,” Carol continued, her voice gentler now. “So ask.”

    It made Zosia laugh. The tension cracking just enough.

    “Alright,” she said. “Let’s do this properly.”

    She started asking her the most basic things she could think of. Favorite color. Favorite food. Favorite TV show. Did she like gardening? Was she any good at it? Simple things. The kind of information you’re supposed to know before you show up with a suitcase.

    Carol answered every question patiently, occasionally amused.

    She lingered in the silence, exhaling gently.

    “Okay,” she said softly. “Now that we’ve covered my gardening skills and television preferences… can I make the decision for you?”

    “You can try,” Zosia said, her voice coming out softer than she intended.

    “Stay in my guest room.”

    Zosia hesitated. “You still want me to?”

    “I do,” Carol replied. “Even more now.”

    She shook her head faintly, almost to herself. “How is that even possible?”

    “Because,” Carol said slowly, “I want to actually get to know you. And you’re only here for a few days. I don’t want those days to feel scheduled. Or careful. Or lonely.”

    Carol let out a quiet laugh, softer now.

    “I don’t want you going back to a hotel room at the end of the night because it seems appropriate. I don’t want us watching the clock. I want it to feel easy. If we wake up and don’t feel like leaving the house, we don’t. If we go somewhere, I want us to come home together. Not let the night end with us heading back to separate parts of the city.”

    Zosia didn’t say anything. She couldn’t. The sincerity in Carol’s voice hit somewhere deep and unguarded, and she had to blink hard to keep herself steady.

    “And,” Carol continued, quieter now, “I want to make one thing clear.”

    Zosia’s breath caught.

    “There are no expectations,” Carol said quietly. 

    “Expectations?” Zosia’s voice was barely audible.

    “You know what I mean.”

    Zosia made a small, helpless sound.

    “It would complicate things,” Carol went on. “I don’t want this trip to feel loaded. I don’t want it to be about anticipation, or pressure, or that quiet question hanging in the air the whole time.”

    She held the moment a second longer before continuing.

    “And I don’t want you to feel like you have to force something just because you flew across the country.”

    It sank in slowly.

    “And who knows, maybe we’ll spend a few days together and realize we don’t actually like each other as much as we thought we did,” she said, almost lightly. “Maybe we’re better at texting than sharing the same space. If that’s true… then at least we’ll know.”

    Zosia’s fingers tightened around her phone.

    The words should have unsettled her. Instead, they felt strangely grounding. Like Carol was clearing the air before anything fragile could form inside it.

    “So here’s the deal,” Carol said. “I pick you up from the airport. You stay in the guest room. We do whatever we want, whenever we want.”

    A pause.

    “Without expectations,” Carol said, more firmly this time.

    Zosia could hear the soft exhale on the other end.

    “You’re serious.”

    “Very.”

    The line went quiet. Her heartbeat did not.

    “I…” She swallowed. “Okay.”

    “Okay,” Carol echoed, gentle but certain. “Now,” she added, her voice softening, “go pack your bags. Get some sleep. I’ll see you in two days.”

    A smaller smile in her voice now. “I’m looking forward to it.”

    The call ended.

    Zosia sat there, stunned, staring at nothing.

    I can do this. She told herself, as she started packing the rest of her suitcase.

     

    ——

     

    The day of her flight arrived whether she felt ready or not, and her suitcase had already been packed twice over. 

    She was halfway through her coffee when her phone buzzed.

    Joanna: If you’re really doing this, at least read this first.

    She sent a link. 

    Zosia almost didn’t click it. Almost.

    Literary Weekly — Tour Spotlight: Carol Sturka on Writing Love Without Losing Yourself

    The first half is harmless.

    Questions about world-building. About Wycaro and the character of Raban.

    Zosia relaxes a little.

     

    Interviewer:

    Your readers are incredibly devoted. Some follow multiple tour stops. What’s it like meeting people who feel so connected to your work?

    Carol:

    It’s a gift. Stories mean something to people. I never take that lightly. But I try to remember that the connection is to the work first. The rest requires a bit more distance.

     

    Zosia’s stomach tightens, just slightly.

    She keeps reading.

     

    Interviewer:

    You’ve mentioned before that you’re protective of your private life. Has being a public figure changed how you approach relationships?

    Carol:

    It’s made me more aware. When people feel like they already know you, it can create an imbalance. I’ve learned not to confuse admiration with intimacy. They’re not the same thing.

     

    The words linger on the screen.

    She had fallen for the woman in the author photo before she’d met the woman in the room.

    Was she already guilty of the very thing Carol warned against?

    She scrolls.

     

    Interviewer:

    You write epic, consuming romances. Do you believe in that kind of love in real life? What does it look like to you?

    Carol:

    I believe in timing more than destiny. Intensity isn’t proof of permanence. The real thing, if it exists, feels calm. It unfolds gradually. Two people meeting in the present instead of rushing toward an idea of each other.

     

    Zosia’s throat tightens as she rereads the line.

    Calm?

    That was the word that stayed with her, even after the rest of the interview faded from memory. The answers weren’t harsh or distant. They were reasonable. And somehow that made them harder to shake.

    She thought of the author photo she had looked at for too long. Of standing in line at the reading with her heart racing. Of already knowing small details about Carol’s life before they had even exchanged a proper sentence.

    The imbalance hadn’t started now. It had been there from the beginning.

    Carol had simply been kind enough not to say it out loud.

     

    Chapter 10: Carol's POV

    Chapter Notes

    Thank you all so much for the nice comments!
    I didn’t expect this kind of feedback, and it’s honestly a bit overwhelming (in a good way).

    I hope you like the next chapter. It's another one from Carol's POV.

     

    ** 4 days earlier **

     

    Carol’s POV

     

    Carol doesn’t usually announce anything this early.

    In the past, things happened quickly and quietly. A blurred hotel room, a mistake she told herself didn’t matter, something that would dissolve by morning and never touch the rest of her life.

    This isn’t that.

    This requires intention.

    Before this becomes more than a passing visit, before letting her step anywhere near the professional parts of her world, Carol decides she has to say it out loud.

    Helen has seen the reckless version of her. The lonely version. The one who mistook flattery for connection and paid for it later.

    This feels different.

    And that difference is exactly why she’s nervous.

    She watches Helen’s back for a moment before deciding she can’t postpone it again.

    Helen is slicing citrus at the counter when Carol says it.

    “I’ve met someone.”

    She doesn’t soften it. She doesn’t apologize. She just says it and lets it sit between them.

    Helen keeps slicing for a second longer. Then she sets the knife down and looks over her shoulder.

    “Okay,” she says, in the steady tone of someone preparing to manage consequences.

    “She’s coming here next week,” Carol continues, forcing herself not to rush the words. “For a few days.”

    Helen turns fully now.

    “To Albuquerque?”

    “Yes.”

    “And you’re telling me because…?”

    “Because she’s joining us Tuesday at the publisher dinner.”

    Helen wipes her hands on a towel and faces Carol with the kind of stillness that used to mean damage control was about to begin.

    “Who is she?”

    Carol sighs.

    “Remember the record store in New York?”

    Helen’s mouth tightens. “Right. I was wondering why you dragged me halfway across New York for that.” 

    Her gaze sharpens.

    “Where did you two meet?”

    Carol hesitates just long enough for Helen to notice. “At the book-reading three days earlier.”

    “A Wycaro fan,” Helen repeats, a short, humorless laugh escaping her. “Well. That’s original.”

    Carol flinches at the tone. “Don’t.”

    Helen crosses her arms slowly.

    “Am I wrong?” She exhales, and there is frustration in it, but also something heavier.

    “Do you remember Boston? The woman who showed up outside your hotel crying because she said she was going to leave her husband for you?”

    Carol stiffens almost involuntarily.

    Helen’s voice is low but steady. “You were in a bad place, you were drinking too much, and every signing blurred into the next and you started sleeping with every woman who told you your words saved her.”

    Carol looks away, because she remembers too clearly.

    “You slept with two readers in the first three weeks of the tour,” Helen continues. “Both of them married.”

    “I didn’t know they were married,” Carol says, but there’s no edge to it now. Just fact.

    Helen studies her for a long moment, something tightening behind her eyes.

    “That’s not the point,” she says quietly. “The point is you didn’t ask. You didn’t look. You didn’t want to know.”

    She exhales slowly, the anger thinning into something more tired.

    “I was your manager,” Helen says, her voice softer now. “It was my job to clean it up. I handled the hotel situations, the awkward emails, the PR messes.”

    Her composure falters, just slightly.

    “All the while,” she continues. “I was going through the same divorce you were. Do you have any idea what that was like? Standing there, fixing it, smoothing it over, acting like I was just doing my job? Pretending it didn’t hurt?”

    She lets out a breath that isn’t quite steady.

    Carol closes her eyes briefly. “I’m sorry,” she says, barely above a whisper.

    She remembers the panic, the shame, the way Helen had stepped in with practiced calm, redirecting, de-escalating, absorbing.

    Helen continues. “You were lonely. You were flattered. And you confused being desired with being understood.”

    “That’s not what this is,” Carol says quietly, though her throat feels tight.

    “Isn’t it?”

    “No.”

    “Then tell me how this is different.”

    Carol searches for words that don’t make her sound naive.

    “She didn’t tell me she loved Wycaro,” she says finally. “She didn’t tell me I saved her. She didn’t look at me like I was a fantasy.”

    “She looked at you how?” Helen asks instead.

    Carol holds her gaze. “Like I was human.”

    The answer sits between them.

    Silence settles.

    “Did you sleep with her?” Helen asks.

    “No.”

    There’s no hesitation there.

    “That’s not what this is about,” she says. “I don’t want to rush into anything. I just want to get to know her.”

    Helen steps closer, studying her face with the quiet intensity of someone who has learned to recognize self-deception on sight.

    “She’s flying across the country for you,” Helen says carefully. “You’re taking her to a dinner where your sexuality is considered a liability,” she continues. “You’re placing her inside your world.”

    She pauses.

    “That sounds an awful lot like 'rushing' to me.”

    Carol exhales. “I know how it looks.”

    Helen doesn’t interrupt.

    “But you know me,” Carol continues, quieter now. “You know when I’m being reckless. This isn’t that.”

    Helen arches a brow slightly.

    “She’s not some starstruck Wycaro fan you’ll have to clean up after,” Carol adds. “She’s not looking for a story. She’s just… herself.”

    Helen studies her.

    “I do know you,” she says. “And that’s why I’m not worried about her.”

    Carol frowns slightly. “Then what?”

    Helen’s gaze doesn’t waver.

    “I’m worried about you.”

     

    ————

     

    The days looked ordinary from the outside.

    Carol answered emails. Took calls. Had lunch with her agent. Watered the plants. Signed contracts.

    Underneath it, something had shifted.

    She re-read Zosia’s messages more than once before replying, adjusting her tone, deleting anything that sounded too eager. She timed her responses, not to seem distant, but not immediate either. She refused to be the one accelerating this.

    Carol found herself asking practical questions and pretending they were casual.

    She wasn’t just being considerate. She was preparing.

    She adjusted her schedule that week without announcing it to anyone. Moved a meeting. Declined a dinner invitation. Restocked the fridge with things she didn’t usually buy because Zosia had once mentioned liking them. That’s how she ended up with a fridge full of mango ice cream.

    She caught herself imagining mornings, the sound of someone else in the kitchen, an extra mug beside the sink.

    Two nights before the flight, Zosia texted that she was torn. She said that a hotel would be the smart choice, but she didn’t want it.

    Carol stared at that message for a long time.

    This was the point where she could step back and encourage caution. 

    Instead, she didn’t like the idea of something important unfolding somewhere she wasn’t part of.

    She didn’t want Zosia in a neutral hotel room downtown. She didn’t want the evenings to end with her disappearing into a cab. She didn’t want this visit to feel like a conference. 

    She wanted her nearby.

    When Zosia admitted she’d been thinking about her more than she probably should, Carol’s restraint thinned. The intensity she’d been trying to manage sharpened instead of fading.

    “Can I make the decision for you?” she asked during the call. “Stay in my guest room.”

    She stood and walked slowly down the hallway as she spoke, stopping at the guest room door. She looked inside. Neutral bedding, clean surfaces, nothing intimate. It’s safe.

    “I want to actually get to know you,” she continued. “And you’re only here for a few days. I don’t want those days to feel scheduled. Or careful. Or lonely.”

    She leaned against the doorframe.

    If this was going to be real, it had to survive this. And if it didn’t, she needed to know quickly.

    The “no expectations” rule wasn’t noble. It wasn’t even that generous. It was self-protection. If she said it out loud, she could keep things under control. If she lowered the stakes, she could slow down what already felt like it was moving too fast.

    Relief hit Carol harder than she expected.

    After the call ended, Carol stayed in the doorway of the guest room for some time. Then she stepped inside and started making the bed properly.

     

    ————

     

    ** Sunday **

     

    The plane lands ten minutes early.

    Carol has been at the airport for twenty.

    She told herself she’d wait in the car. That it would look relaxed. Casual. But the closer it got to arrival time, the less she could sit still. So now she’s inside, standing near the sliding exit doors, one hand tucked into her jacket pocket, the other wrapped around her phone. She keeps glancing down at the screen as if it might update faster under pressure.

    The sliding doors part every few seconds. Strangers spill out. Families. Business travelers. College kids with backpacks. She scans each face automatically, pulse steady but elevated.

    This is ridiculous, she tells herself. It’s just an airport pickup.

    But it doesn’t feel like that.

    It feels like something that has existed in private, in texts and late-night calls, is about to become real.

    She thinks about the guest room. The careful way she said ‘no expectations’. The balance she tried to create between invitation and restraint.

    Was it clear enough?

    She flips the phone over in her hand, hesitates, then opens the message thread.

    Carol: I’m here. Waiting at the exit.

    Her phone buzzes almost instantly.

    Zosia: Waiting for my luggage.

    Carol exhales, slow and controlled, as if she had been holding that breath for days.

    Another set of doors opens. Another wave of strangers. Carol shifts her weight, forcing herself not to pace.

    Her chest tightens.

    The doors slide open again.

    And then…

    There.

    Zosia steps through with her suitcase, pausing just past the threshold, scanning the crowd. For a second she looks unsure.

    There’s a slight hesitation in Zosia’s step, and Carol feels it. That quiet beat before their eyes connect.

    The airport noise dulls, fading into something distant and unimportant, as if the space between them has narrowed to a single, quiet point.

    Carol doesn’t wave or call out. She doesn’t need to.

    She just stands there, rooted and suddenly aware of her own heartbeat, steady on the outside and completely undone underneath.

    She’s here.

    Not a voice in her ear. Not a name on her phone.

    Here.

    Chapter 11: Sunday

     

    At baggage claim, the waiting is unbearable.

    The carousel turns, slow and indifferent. And Zosia wonders why she didn’t pack lighter, why she didn’t make this easier on herself.

    If she’d brought only a carry-on, she would be outside already.

    Her phone buzzes in her hand, sharp enough to make her heart jump.

    Carol: I’m here. Waiting at the exit.

    Zosia lets out a quiet involuntary laugh. Somewhere in the darker corner of her mind she had imagined being stood up. Finding the arrivals hall empty.

    Now there’s no fantasy left. This is real.

    She types back carefully, her thumbs steadier than her pulse.

    Zosia: Waiting for my luggage.

    The reply feels minimal, almost ridiculous compared to the magnitude of it all.

    Her suitcase finally appears on the belt, rolling toward her.

    She steps forward, takes a breath, and grabs the handle before it passes. Lifts it down. Sets it upright.

    That’s it. No more waiting.

    Each step toward the exit feels heavier than the last. 

    She slows just before the sliding doors.

    This is the last anonymous moment she will have.

    Inside the terminal, she is just another traveler with a suitcase and a tired expression. Outside, she becomes someone expected.

    The doors slide open.

    Bright New Mexico sunlight floods her vision. It’s so sudden it washes everything white for a second, and she has to squint as her eyes adjust from the flat artificial glow of the terminal to the sharp, unfiltered light outside.

    For a moment she sees only movement, silhouettes, the blur of strangers waiting.

    And then the outside disappears.

    Carol lingers at the edge of the crowd, hands buried in her pockets, composure arranged to look easy.

    It takes Zosia a second to realize that Carol isn’t scanning the crowd at all.

    She has already found her.

    She has been looking at her.

    Waiting.

    Zosia studies her, searching for hesitation. For politeness. For doubt.

    She finds none of it.

    Just recognition.

    The world narrows. Her breath falters. There she is.

    Not a voice through a speaker. Not a name on her phone. Not a photo on the back of a book.

    Carol’s expression shifts. Subtle, but unmistakable.

    Zosia slows her pace without meaning to. Her steps faltering as though her body needs extra time to absorb this new reality.

    She had not prepared for how different this would be from memory.

    Their previous meetings had been simpler. Back then, they hadn’t known what this was, or if it was anything at all. There had been uncertainty, space to dismiss it as coincidence.

    This time there was no pretending. They both knew. Now it had to survive reality.

    They smile at each other, almost shy.

    “Hey,” Carol says, her voice soft and even, as if she practiced this and it still caught her off guard.

    “Hi,” Zosia answers, and it comes out thinner than she meant it to.

    Carol’s smile widens. Impossible to hide. If she meant to play this cool, she failed spectacularly.

    There’s a pause.

    “So,” Zosia says, before she can overthink it, “is it okay if I hug you?”

    She had rehearsed this moment the entire flight.

    In her head, she’d tested every possible version of it. A polite handshake, something casual like a high five, a reckless, cinematic kiss with a dramatic slow-motion run toward her.

    In the end, she’d settled on a hug.

    Carol’s expression shifts at once.

    Relief.

    As if she had felt it too. The hesitation, the uncertainty, the fear of misreading the distance between them.

    The tension drains out of her in an exhale that almost becomes a laugh.

    “Of course,” she says, warm and immediate. “Please.”

    That word travels through her, slow and undeniable.

    Zosia steps forward before she can rethink it, closing the space and lifting her arms slowly, leaving room for Carol to step back if she wants to.

    Her hands slide around Zosia’s waist, firm and certain. Zosia’s arms wrap around Carol’s shoulders and for a second it’s careful, like they’re pretending this is a normal reunion between friends.

    It holds, neither of them shifting.

    Then Carol exhales. 

    It’s subtle, barely a sound, but Zosia senses the shift in weight, the way Carol’s body relaxes fully against hers instead of keeping that protective distance.

    “You’re here,” like she half-expected her not to be. Like she needed proof.”

    “I told you I would be,” Zosia says, her voice softer now, every nerve suddenly aware of just how close they’re standing.

    Carol is the first to pull back.

    She clears her throat. Reaches for composure. Then reaches for the suitcase.

    “How was the flight?” she asks, choosing the safest possible question.

    The normalcy makes Zosia want to laugh.

    “Uneventful,” she says, because anything more honest would sound too charged right now.

    Carol’s mouth curves slightly.

    They begin walking side by side, careful to maintain a reasonable distance, the kind that looks unremarkable from the outside.

    Zosia notices the things most people wouldn’t. How easily their steps match, how their arms almost meet before drifting apart again.

    When their shoulders graze for half a second, it is accidental. But the contact ignites something low and immediate.

    She doesn’t pull away.

    Neither does Carol.

    Inside the car, cool air replaces the desert heat. The door shuts with a solid click, and the outside world falls away.

    The space shrinks. They are suddenly alone.

    “You could still panic and ask for the hotel,” Carol says, almost timidly now, her eyes fixed on the windshield.

    Zosia studies her profile. The line of her jaw, the faint tension at the corner of her mouth, the way her fingers tighten on the steering wheel.

    A hotel would be safer. A hotel would be easier. 

    “No.” Zosia admits. “I am panicking. But a hotel wouldn’t change that.”

    Carol’s mouth twitches, almost a smile.

    “Good,” she says, more to herself than to Zosia.

    Zosia turns toward her. “Good?”

    “I’d be worried if you weren’t panicking at least a little.” Carol keeps her eyes on the road. “I am.”

    Zosia studies her. “You are?”

    Carol nods once. “You flew across the country for me.”

    She exhales.

    “That’s terrifying.”

    The silence deepens.

    As the car pulls onto the road, Zosia leans back against the seat and watches the unfamiliar city unfold beyond the window.

    She crossed a line simply by landing. She crossed another with that hug.

    There is no version of this week that leaves her unchanged.

     

    ----

     

    The house is quieter than she expected. It’s large, a lot more spacious than her small New York apartment. It feels… comforting. Like a home should be.

    The front door clicks closed, sealing the moment.

    This is Carol’s space.

    And now Zosia stands in the middle of it, suitcase by her side, her pulse still uneven.

    Carol brushes past her, close enough that Zosia catches the warmth of her body before it’s gone.

    “Guest room’s down the hall,” she says lightly, gesturing with an easy tilt of her head as though this is ordinary.

    Zosia nods, taking it in.

    The guest room is warm and prepared. A folded towel at the end of the bed, space cleared in the closet, fresh flowers on the windowsill.

    “Make yourself at home,” she says, gesturing to the room around her. 

    “Thanks,” Zosia replies, offering a sheepish smile she cannot quite contain. “It looks lovely.”

    Carol’s smile shifts into something warmer.

    “I’m going to make something to eat,” Carol says after a moment, already stepping back toward the hallway. “Do you want something?”

    Food is the last thing on Zosia’s mind. The idea of sitting at a table, chewing and swallowing while her entire nervous system vibrates seems almost absurd.

    She hesitates too long.

    Carol notices.

    “Or a drink?” she adds, her voice easing.

    “Yes,” Zosia answers way too eagerly, the word tumbling out before she can temper it. “That would be great.”

    “Okay,” Carol says with a knowing smirk. “I’ll leave you to it. Take your time. I’ll be in the kitchen.”

    The door closes gently behind her, leaving Zosia alone with the low hum of the house and the evidence of Carol’s preparation surrounding her.

    Three days.

    The thought lands heavily.

    Three days in this house. Three mornings. Three nights.

    As she starts unpacking, she realizes that the hotel is no longer an option.

     

    ----

     

    When she reaches the kitchen entrance, she stops without intending to. She has the sudden sense of being an intruder, as if she has wandered into a private moment she wasn’t meant to witness. A version of Carol that exists when no one is looking.

    Carol is standing at the counter in a short-sleeved shirt, her bare arms moving in steady, practiced motions as she chops vegetables with careful precision. The rhythm of the knife is controlled and even, the sound soft but consistent, and there’s something unexpectedly calming about watching her focus on something so ordinary.

    Zosia lingers in the doorway a moment longer than she should. Just watching her.

    “You can come in,” Carol says without turning around.

    Zosia steps forward.

    The kitchen is open and generous, but she is suddenly unsure where a guest is meant to position herself.

    She settles near the edge of the counter, resting her fingertips lightly against the cool surface. Waiting.

    Carol keeps chopping, the knife moving in steady strokes. “I know you’re not hungry. But I’m making something anyway. I’d be a terrible host if I let my guest starve.”

    Zosia smiles. “Thank you.”

    She watches the controlled movement of Carol’s hands, the careful precision that looks steadier than she suspects it feels.

    Carol sets the knife down a little harder than she means to, the sound echoing in the space between them. She exhales, running a hand briefly over her face as if searching for the right words.

    “I’m sorry, I’m just…” She trails off, then takes a steadying breath. “This is so weird.”

    Her eyes lift quickly to Zosia’s.

    “Good weird,” she adds almost immediately, a nervous smile tugging at her mouth. “But still weird.”

    “Me being here?” Zosia asks.

    Carol shakes her head immediately. “Us being here,” she corrects. “I’m fifty-three years old and I’m so fucking nervous right now I don’t even know how I’m still standing.”

    The honesty catches Zosia off guard in the best way.

    “If you want me to go to a hotel…” she begins gently.

    “No.” Carol turns fully toward her, closing the space between them without quite thinking about it. Her hands come to rest on Zosia’s arms. “That’s not what I mean.”

    Zosia huffs a laugh, tension draining from her posture. “I know what you mean.”

    Carol exhales in visible relief. “Don’t do that. Don’t scare me like that,” she says, half joking, half serious.

    “I’m sorry, I…” Zosia falters for a moment. “I’m really happy to be here. With you.”

    She holds Carol’s gaze a beat longer than is strictly comfortable, but not long enough to look away.

    Carol studies her face carefully, as if weighing the sincerity in it.

    “That’s a relief,” she admits. “I don’t want you regretting this trip.”

    “I’m not.” Zosia says, a faint smile tugging at her mouth. “Can I help you with anything?”

    Carol gestures vaguely toward the corner of the room. “There’s a speaker somewhere over there. It’s usually on. I forgot.”

    Zosia nods and walks toward it.

    It takes her a second to find the small stereo tucked beneath the window, a short stack of CDs beside it.

    She glances at them, flips through a few cases, and pulls one out.

    After a brief mechanical hum, music drifts into the kitchen. Nothing big. Just something steady in the background.

    Zosia walks back toward the island, aware that Carol is watching her. A small smile flickering across her face. 

    On her way, something on the far wall catches her eye.

    A painting she hadn’t noticed before.

    A pale coastline under the aurora. Greens moving through the sky, touches of violet at the edges. It pulls her in without trying to.

    “That’s beautiful,” she says, almost to herself. “Is there a story behind it?”

    Carol follows her gaze.

    “Not really.” Carol hesitates. “My ex-wife bought it while we were in Norway.”

    The word settles between them.

    “You were married?”

    “I was,” Carol replies calmly. “For eight years. We were together for fourteen.”

    There’s no defensiveness in her tone. Just fact.

    Zosia processes that, staring into her wine as if the answer might be there.

    “And when did you get divorced?”

    “Two years ago.”

    Zosia steadies herself with a breath. “I’m sorry. I just… didn’t expect that.”

    Carol tilts her head. “Why?”

    “I don’t know.” She shrugs, a little self-conscious now. “It just never crossed my mind.”

    Carol’s smile is gentle, almost apologetic.

    There’s a pause.

    “So… do you still see her?” Zosia asks carefully.

    Carol doesn’t hesitate. “Yes. Quite a bit.”

    Zosia’s brows lift slightly.

    “She’s my manager,” Carol adds.

    She feels the story changing in real time.

    “Helen?” Zosia asks, her tone restrained.

    “Yes.”

    Zosia lets that sink in. Helen isn’t just an ex, it’s her ex-wife.

    “That sounds… complicated.”

    Carol thinks for a moment. “Yeah, it can be challenging at times. But we work well together.”

    Zosia nods, though she’s not entirely sure what that means in practice.

    “Okay,” she says, grasping for something more and finding nothing.

    Carol’s mouth softens, but there’s tension in it.

    “I should’ve brought it up sooner,” she says gently.

    Zosia shrugs. “Exes just… never came up before.”

    “No,” Carol agrees, her voice easy.

    A fragile silence hangs between them.

    Carol gives her a half-smile. “Let’s not talk about them tonight.”

    Zosia studies her.

    “Maybe a good idea.”

    They let it rest there.

    Carol reaches for the wine and holds up her glass. “To… whatever this is.”

    Zosia lifts hers, a measured smile appearing. She clinks her glass against Carol’s.

    “To finding out.”

    They sit at the table.

    Dinner unfolds gently. The food is good. Comforting in its simplicity. Zosia tells her so, and Carol smiles in that quiet, pleased way that seems almost shy.

    They talk.

    Carol asks real questions. About suppliers, about rent, about which albums actually move and which just look good on display. She wants to know what sells, what doesn’t, what people are listening to lately.

    And she listens.

    Zosia relaxes into that. It’s easier to talk about inventory than ex-wives.

    At one point Carol tells a story about a disastrous panel she once did in Chicago, and Zosia laughs genuinely, head tipping back, hand covering her mouth.

    For a few minutes, nothing intrudes.

    And then, in the gaps of silence, it slips back in.

    Helen. Ex-wife. Manager.

    Zosia doesn’t bring it up again. She doesn’t need to. It sits between them, like a third chair at the table no one acknowledges.

    She feels the subtle tightening in her chest, the instinct to read between the lines even when there aren’t any.

    Around ten, Zosia yawns before she can stop herself.

    Carol glances toward the clock. “It’s late.”

    “It’s midnight for me,” Zosia says. “My body still thinks I’m in New York.”

    “Jet lag,” Carol says, watching her. “We can call it a night if you want.”

    Zosia nods. “Yeah. I think I need to.”

    They clear the table together. Plates stacked. Glasses set in the sink. The music switched off.

    The house grows quieter with each small task.

    Carol walks beside her toward the guest room.

    Zosia pushes the door open. The bedside lamp casts a warm circle of light over the already-open suitcase, the book she set down earlier, her sweater folded on the chair.

    She steps inside.

    Carol lingers in the doorway.

    “You have everything you need?” she asks.

    It’s casual. But there’s something careful in it.

    “Yes,” Zosia says. “I’m good.”

    A short pause.

    “Extra blanket’s in the closet,” Carol adds. “And there’s a charger on the nightstand if yours doesn’t reach.”

    “You’ve thought of everything.”

    Carol shrugs, almost embarrassed. “I try.”

    Silence settles between them.

    “Thank you again for letting me stay here,” she says. She wants that to be clear.

    “You still don’t regret it?” Carol asks, and there’s something unguarded in it.

    Zosia shakes her head. “No.”

    It’s immediate. Genuine.

    Without thinking about it, she reaches for Carol’s hand and gives it a small, reassuring squeeze.

    The contact startles her. She withdraws, a quick, embarrassed smile appearing. “Sorry.”

    Carol glances down at where their hands were, then back up at her. “You don’t have to say sorry,” she says, a small smile pulling at her mouth. “I had a really nice time.”

    Something in her voice steadies her.

    “Me too.” 

    Carol shifts her weight back, almost retreating. “I hope you sleep well.”

    “Good night,” Zosia says, and steps into the guest room, closing the door behind her.

    She sits on the edge of the bed and exhales.

    Helen. Ex-wife.

    Not a ghost. Not a distant past. A real person. Present. Embedded in Carol’s life.

    Zosia drops her gaze to the floor, grounding herself in something still and ordinary.

    She isn’t jealous. Not yet.

    She’s scared because she sees exactly what could go wrong.

    And she wants it anyway.

    Chapter 12: Monday

    Zosia woke up with the heavy feeling of someone who hadn’t slept enough and had dreamed too much.

    10:46 AM

    She stared at the ceiling for a moment, blinking at the morning light coming through the curtains.

    Zosia had lain awake until the early morning, replaying the evening, their conversations, in detail.

    She rolled onto her side and pressed her face into the pillow, exhaling hard. Carol was in the other room. Probably already up. Probably already functioning like a responsible adult while Zosia had been unraveling in the dark.

    She pushed herself out of bed, ran a hand through her hair in a gesture that did absolutely nothing to improve it, and walked toward the kitchen with the determined posture of someone who intended to behave normally, as though nothing about the night before had unsettled her.

    The house smelled faintly of coffee and something green, something sun-warmed and alive, and the scent alone was enough to tighten something low in her chest.

    She filled the kettle, turned it on, and leaned her hip against the counter in what she hoped read as casual composure.

    And that’s when she saw her. Through the wide kitchen window, in the garden. Carol.

    Zosia froze.

    Carol was kneeling beside a low wooden planter, her tank top exposing toned arms that flexed with every movement. She was repotting something, hands deep in soil, shoulders shifting under smooth skin as she worked.

    Zosia swallowed, her fingers tightening almost involuntarily around the edge of the counter.

    “Why?” she whispered to the empty kitchen, as though the universe owed her an explanation.

    Carol wasn’t dressed up, she wasn’t even trying. Just manual labor and sunlight…and those arms.

    God, those arms.

    Every time she pressed soil down into a pot, her muscles tightened and released in an easy, unconscious rhythm.

    Zosia forgot about the kettle. Forgot about blinking. About breathing. Basic human functions seem to fail her.

    She felt like a teenager. Ridiculous.

    Carol wiped her forearm across her forehead, leaving a faint streak of dirt behind. The simple movement made something tighten in Zosia’s chest.

    And then Carol paused.

    She straightened slowly, as if sensing someone was watching her. Her head turned toward the house. Toward the window.

    That’s when their eyes met.

    Zosia’s stomach dropped, and for a second neither of them moved. Carol’s expression changed. First confused, then simply aware. A small, almost private smile appeared at the corner of her mouth.

    Zosia panicked. She was caught red-handed.

    She grabbed her coffee mug and turned away far too quickly, sloshing liquid over the rim.

    “Smooth. Very smooth.” She muttered to herself.

    She forced herself to walk, maybe run, toward the hallway without looking back. She could feel the way Carol was watching her leave, probably judging her.

    In the bathroom, she shut the door and leaned against it.

    Her heart was racing. Was it because she was watching Carol? Or because she was caught looking?

    Zosia stared at her reflection in the mirror.

    “You are an adult,” she said to herself under her breath. “Get it together.”

    Zosia closed her eyes and let out a slow breath. Why didn’t I go to a hotel?

    She stayed in the shower longer than necessary. Long enough to cool down the memory of flexing arms and sunlit shoulders.

    When she stepped out, towel-dried her hair, she intentionally chose neutral clothes. Jeans. A loose shirt. Nothing that would make her look desperate. Nothing that would require Carol’s attention.

    By the time she opened the bathroom door, she had rehearsed every possible scenario. Would Carol tease her about it? Would she make a sarcastic remark?

    Carol was at the counter, back turned, slicing bread. She had changed out of the tank top and into a soft grey sweater.

    Thank god.

    Carol glanced up at the sound of footsteps.

    “Morning,” Carol said lightly, as though the last fifteen minutes had not contained any shared eye contact whatsoever.

    “Morning,” Zosia answered, bracing herself for a comment. But nothing came.

    Carol didn’t mention it. Didn’t smirk. Didn’t tease. Didn’t raise an eyebrow.

    If she knew, and Zosia was certain that she did, she was generous enough to let it slide.

    “Did you sleep okay?” Carol asked casually. Almost too casual.

    Zosia hesitated for a second. “Terrible,” she admitted. “I couldn’t fall asleep until five.”

    Carol looked up again, concern. “Five? Why?”

    As if you don't know. Zosia thought.

    She shrugged. “Probably because of a different environment, different bed.”

    That part, at least, was plausible.
