    Carol studied her for a moment. Long enough for Zosia to wonder if she saw straight through the lie. Maybe she also hadn’t slept? Maybe she had been working in the garden since 6AM because of the same problem?

    “Yeah,” Carol said finally. “I know the feeling.”

    Zosia wasn’t sure if Carol bought it. Maybe it didn’t matter.

    “Want some breakfast?” Carol asked. “I made enough for two.”

    “Yeah,” Zosia said quietly. “I’d like that.”

    Carol slid a plate toward her. Their fingers brushed for the briefest second as Zosia took it.

    They both pretended not to notice.

    Zosia sat down at the kitchen table, forcing herself to focus on the toast and her second cup of coffee.

     

    ——

     

    Monday unfolded bright and cloudless, the sky an endless, unapologetic blue.

    Zosia hadn’t expected Albuquerque to feel this open. The horizon seemed to stretch in every direction, low buildings pressed beneath wide air and distant mountains. It made New York feel like a clenched fist by comparison.

    Carol drove with one hand resting lightly at the top of the steering wheel, sunglasses pushed into her hair. Sleeves rolled up again. Window cracked open.

    “This is Old Town,” Carol said as they stepped out of the car later, the air dry and sun-warmed. “Touristy, but tolerable on a weekday.”

    Adobe walls glowed soft beige in the morning light. Wooden beams. Small galleries tucked into corners. A church bell rang somewhere in the distance.

    Zosia walked beside her, hands in her pockets. She was aware of the space between them.

    “It’s… quiet,” Zosia said.

    “You say that like it’s suspicious.”

    “In New York, quiet means something is wrong.”

    Carol laughed.

    They moved slowly through town. Carol pointed out her favorite coffee shop, a bookstore that stocked her work in the window, a trailhead she liked when she needed to think.

    Zosia watched her more than the scenery.

    Carol here was different from Carol in interviews. Less composed. Less curated. She greeted the barista by name. She paused to ask an older man about his dog. 

    She existed in this place with ease. And it softened something in Zosia.

    At lunch they sat outside, shaded by a faded umbrella. Tacos. Lime. Cold water with condensation slipping down the glass.

     

    By the time they return from town, the light has softened into early evening gold, stretching long across the kitchen tiles. Carol drops her keys into the bowl near the door and automatically reaches for the fridge.

    “I’ll make something quick,” she says, already shifting into host mode.

    Zosia steps in front of the refrigerator before Carol can open it.

    “No. Tonight I’m cooking.”

    Carol pauses, hand still on the handle. “You’re cooking?”

    “Yes.”

    “In my kitchen?”

    Zosia folds her arms, attempting an authoritative stance, but the smile she can’t quite suppress gives her away.

    “Yes, now go relax.”

    Carol studies her for a moment, amused. “You don’t know where everything is.”

    “I’ll manage.”

    Carol doesn’t argue immediately. She leans her hip against the counter instead, arms loosely crossed, watching Zosia navigate the space with a focus that’s half confidence, half guesswork.

    “You’re two drawers off,” Carol says mildly.

    “You don’t even know what I’m looking for.”

    “Trust me.” Carol adds.

    Zosia opens the final drawer and finds the knives. “Fine, so you did know. That doesn’t change anything.”

    She hears Carol chuckle behind her.

    “Please sit down.” Zosia orders her.

    “I don’t sit in my own kitchen.”

    “Then stand somewhere that isn’t within arm’s reach.”

    Carol doesn’t move.

    She watches Zosia begin chopping vegetables, the precision of her movements hinting at an awareness she’s trying not to show.

    “You’re holding it wrong,” Carol says after a minute.

    Zosia exhales through her nose. “I am not.”

    “You are. Your fingers…”

    Before Zosia could defend herself further, Carol stepped forward and reached around her, her hand closing gently over Zosia’s to adjust the grip while her other hand settled at her waist with an ease that felt devastatingly natural.

    The contact is practical.

    Carol did not step away immediately, and Zosia became acutely aware of the warmth at her back, the faint scent of soap layered over earth, the steady rhythm of someone breathing close enough to alter the air.

    “Like this,” Carol murmured, voice low.

    Zosia’s pulse stumbles. “You’re very hands-on.”

    Carol’s mouth curves. “Only when invited.”

    “Did I, though?” she asked quietly.

    Carol’s breath shifted in what might have been a quiet laugh before she finally stepped back, leaving the space between them abruptly cooler.

    “You’re impossible,” Zosia says.

    She turns back to the cutting board, focusing a little too hard on the knife in her hand.

    A minute passes in relative calm, the soft rhythm of chopping, the low simmer from the pan. The domestic quiet returns, but it’s thinner now, stretched with awareness.

    “Where do you keep your serving spoons?” she asks, glancing toward the drawers.

    “Bottom drawer by the sink.”

    Zosia opens it.

    Not there.

    Carol pushes off the counter.

    Her hip brushes Zosia’s as she reaches past her, pulling open the deep drawer beneath the sink. Her arm slides lightly along Zosia’s side as she retrieves the serving spoon.

    “There,” she says. But she doesn’t move back.

    Zosia can feel the warmth of her, the steady rise and fall of her breathing.

    Carol’s hand, still holding the spoon, lowers to the counter. Her other hand rests briefly at Zosia’s waist. She reaches for the pan and dips her pinky into the sauce, tasting it with quiet concentration.

    “This is very good.”

    “You just put your finger in the food.”

    “My house, I can put my finger wherever I want.”

    The words hang there.

    Carol freezes.

    A flush rises instantly along her cheekbones, color blooming up to her ears as she realizes exactly how that sounded.

    “I was referring to the sauce.”

    Zosia turns to face her fully, one eyebrow lifting.

    “Of course,” Zosia says, stepping closer. “What else would you be referring to?”

    Carol’s composure tries to reassemble itself, but the faint red in her cheeks refuses to cooperate.

    Zosia steps half a pace closer. Teasing her a little longer.

    “Well,” she pushes again. “What else?”

    Carol’s gaze drops to Zosia’s mouth for a fraction too long before she forces it back up. “Careful Zosia. You don’t know wh…”

    Then the doorbell rang.

    It cuts through the kitchen like a blade, sharp and poorly timed, severing whatever fragile momentum had been building between them.

    Carol’s expression resets almost instantly. Heat shuttered, composure snapping back into place.

    She cleared her throat. “I’m not expecting anyone,” she said, though the faint tightening at her jaw suggested she might already have an idea.

    Carol walked across the room to the door without looking back.

    When she opened it, Helen stood just beyond the threshold.

    Composed, as always. Neutral expression, posture impeccable.

    “Hi,” Helen said, her voice calm and controlled, whatever warmth had once been there now trimmed into politeness.

    Carol blinked once, absorbing the interruption, and replied, “Hi.”

    “I found this in my suitcase when I got home,” Helen continued, lifting a notebook between two fingers. “I assume you’d want it before you started accusing me of intellectual theft.”

    The joke was light, but it landed with history behind it.

    Carol stepped aside to take the notebook, and the movement made Zosia impossible to miss in the kitchen, standing in the late sunlight, the air between them still carrying the trace of something almost intimate.

    Helen’s gaze flicked past Carol’s shoulder.

    “Oh,” Helen said mildly. “I didn’t realize you had company.”

    The sentence was constructed as though she had only just noticed, but the pause before it suggested otherwise.

    Zosia stepped forward, because retreat would have felt like guilt.

    “Hi,” she said, steadying her voice. “Zosia.”

    Helen regarded her for half a breath longer than socially necessary, then stepped inside without waiting to be invited.

    She extended her hand.

    “Helen,” she said, as though this were a business introduction rather than an intersection of personal timelines.

    Her grip was neither aggressive nor overly gentle. It was controlled. But something in the eye contact lingered a fraction too long, as though she were taking inventory.

    “It’s nice to meet you,” she said, professionally polite.

    “You too,” Zosia replied, hoping her smile didn’t look forced.

    Carol cleared her throat softly.

    “You didn’t have to come all the way back,” she said, holding up the notebook. “You could’ve brought it tomorrow.”

    Helen’s lips curved faintly. “I was in the neighborhood.”

    Carol’s look was level, unreadable.

    Helen’s gaze drifted across the kitchen. The open bottle of wine, two glasses, the stove still on low.

    “You’re cooking,” she observed.

    “Zosia is,” Carol corrected, too quickly.

    Helen’s eyes returned to Zosia.

    “In that case,” she said smoothly, “I won’t interrupt.”

    There was no visible emotion. Only a composure so complete it felt almost curated.

    Carol walked her back toward the door, the distance between them careful but not cold, the choreography of two people who had once shared a life and now shared only efficiency.

    At the threshold, Helen paused.

    Her gaze flicked once more to Zosia.

    “I hope you enjoy your time here,” she said, and the sentence carried a subtle emphasis that could have meant anything.

    Zosia held her gaze evenly. “I intend to.”

    Helen inclined her head slightly, as though that answer had confirmed something, then stepped outside.

    The door closed with a quiet finality.

    Zosia picked up the serving spoon and filled the plates, her movements steady despite the shift in the room.

    “That,” she said carefully, “was not accidental.”

    Carol stood with the notebook still in her hand, staring at the closed door as though replaying more than just the last five minutes.

    “No,” she said softly. “It wasn’t.”

    “She knew I was here.”

    “Yes, I told her.”

    “And she pretended she didn’t.”

    Carol finally looked at her.

    “Yep.”

    For a moment, neither of them moved.

    “Well,” Zosia said, lighter than she felt, “the food is getting cold.”

    Carol blinked, as if returning to the present. “Right.”

    She set the notebook down on the counter with careful precision and crossed back into the kitchen.

    Carol brought the wine to the table, and they sat across from each other.

    The first few bites passed in silence. The scrape of fork against porcelain sounded louder than it should have.

    “It’s very good,” Carol said after a moment.

    Zosia looked up. “You already tasted it, remember.” She teased.

    Carol laughed. “Can you please… Let that one go?”

    Zosia smiled. “Unlikely.”

    Carol shook her head, a reluctant smile tugging at her mouth. Some of the tension drained from her shoulders.

    They fell into a quieter rhythm after that. The food disappeared slowly. The wine level lowered. Conversation loosened in increments. Small stories, side comments, the kind of laughter that arrives cautiously at first and then more easily.

    At some point Carol stood to refill their glasses. Zosia stole a bite from Carol’s plate in retaliation for earlier corrections. Carol swatted her hand away with mock disapproval. They debated whether too much salt was a crime or a personality trait. The edges of the evening softened.

    Outside, the light thinned without either of them noticing. Gold faded to gray, gray to indigo.

    By the time Zosia glanced toward the window, it was fully dark. The late sunlight that had framed the kitchen earlier was gone. The glass now reflected only the room itself. The warm overhead lights, the half-empty wine bottle, two women leaning toward each other without realizing how close they’d drifted.

    “It’s late,” Zosia said quietly.

    Carol followed her gaze. “I didn’t notice.”

    “What time do we have to leave tomorrow?”

    Carol hesitated. “Helen will pick us up around 11:30.”

    Zosia flinched at the name.

    “In that case, I should clean up. I’ve taken over your entire kitchen.” Zosia was already on her feet, gathering plates before Carol could object.

    “Leave it,” Carol said, standing as well. “You cooked. I’ll take care of the rest.”

    “Not a chance.” Zosia carried the dishes toward the sink. “I’m not sitting down while you do all the work.”

    Carol followed her into the kitchen and reached for her, hands settling at Zosia’s waist in an attempt to stop her.

    “Please,” she said, softer now. “Go sit down. Or get ready for bed. I’ll take care of this.”

    Neither of them moved.

    The plates rested forgotten on the counter. The kitchen was quiet now, the night pressing softly against the windows. Carol’s hands were warm and steady at her waist.

    Zosia looked up at her.

    Slowly, she lifted her hand and brought it to Carol’s cheek. Her fingertips brushed along her skin, light and careful, as if testing whether this was allowed.

    Carol inhaled.

    Zosia leaned in.

    Unhurried. Close enough to feel the warmth between them, to sense the restraint thinning.

    For a second, it seemed like Carol might let it happen.

    Then Carol’s hand left her waist and came up between them, resting firmly against Zosia’s chest.

    Not pushing her away. Just stopping her.

    “I’m sorry,” she said softly.

    “What’s wrong?”

    Carol swallows. “I made a promise.”

    “To whom?”

    “To myself.” A brief pause. “And Helen.”

    “Excuse me?”

    “I know how that sounds,” Carol says quickly.

    Zosia stays quiet, her expression shifting from puzzled to irritated.

    “There’s something that happened,” Carol continues carefully. “After my divorce.”

    Zosia crosses her arms, bracing herself.

    Carol inhales slowly. “I went to a very dark place. I drank too much. I… slept with a few women I met at my book signings.”

    Zosia’s stomach drops. 

    “You slept with your fans?” She exhales sharply. “Seriously?”

    Carol could only nod. “It wasn’t my proudest moment.” She says quietly. “It got messy. Helen had to step in. She cleaned it up before it damaged everything, my career, my reputation.” Her jaw tightens. “And it wasn’t fair to her.”

    “That sounds…” Zosia searches for the word. “Awful.”

    “It was.” Carol nods once. “For everyone.”

    Silence stretches.

    “When I told Helen you were coming,” Carol continues carefully, “she reminded me of that time. And I saw how afraid she was that I might repeat it.”

    “This is not the same,” Zosia says immediately.

    “I know.”

    “Do you?” she asks, sharper now. “Because right now it sounds like you’re putting me in the same category.”

    “I know this is different,” Carol says, more quietly. “Please believe that.”

    Zosia isn’t convinced.

    “Please,” Carol says again, and it sounds dangerously close to a plea.

    Zosia drops her gaze, trying to sort through what she’s feeling. Hurt, embarrassment, anger, fear.

    Carol looks at her like she’s standing at the edge of something irreversible.

    “I…” Her voice falters, but she doesn’t look away. “I am crazy about you.”

    Zosia’s head snaps up.

    The confession hangs there, plain and honest.

    “And I don’t recognize myself anymore.” Carol continues, forcing herself to hold the eye contact now. “That’s why I need to slow this down.”

    Zosia’s heart starts racing.

    “I…” She drags a hand through her hair. “Fuck.”

    She turns away, taking a few steps toward the table, more to steady herself than to clear it.

    Carol’s expression softens, almost breaking.

    “Please talk to me, Zosia.”

    Zosia exhales sharply before meeting her eyes.

    “You can’t say something like that and expect me to what? Just nod?” Her voice rises despite herself.

    Carol stays silent.

    Zosia doesn’t know what else to say. She had imagined this confession a hundred times. Dreamed it. Built entire futures around hearing those words. 

    None of them ended like this.

    Carol holds her gaze.

    “I needed you to know that,” she says quietly. “Because if I’m asking to go slow… it’s not because I don’t feel this. It’s because I feel it too much.”

    Zosia’s throat tightens.

    “I don’t want to be another chapter you regret,” Zosia says finally. Her voice is calm, but there’s something raw beneath it. “Or something Helen has to manage.”

    Carol’s head lifts at that. “You won’t be.”

    “That’s what you said about them too, I assume.”

    The words come out sharper than she intended.

    Carol flinches, just enough that Zosia notices.

    “I didn’t know them,” Carol says honestly. “Not really. I was lonely. And reckless. And I liked being wanted without having to risk anything real.”

    Carol steps closer, but stops before crossing into her space.

    “But I do know you.” She continues.

    There’s no hesitation now.

    “And I don’t know what this is,” she gestures between them. “But I know it’s not something I’ve experienced before.”

    That stills Zosia.

    Carol swallows again, vulnerability written plainly across her features now. No polish. No public persona.

    It wasn’t like Zosia had never questioned whether she was just another fan. She thought about the interview Joanne had sent her, the way Carol’s answers had felt uncomfortably revealing.

    She had fallen for Carol long before Carol ever looked at her like this.

    “I’m not one of your fans,” she says under her breath, as if reminding herself. “Not here. Not in this.”

    Carol’s expression softens. “I know.”

    Zosia studies her for a long moment.

    “There’s a difference between pacing yourself and keeping me at arm’s length,” she says softly.

    Carol’s faint smile fades. “I’m afraid I don’t know the difference yet.”

    The air between them feels steadier now. Less fragile. More solid.

    Zosia steps forward before she can talk herself out of it.

    She slides her arms around Carol, pulling her in. Carol fits against her easily, her forehead brushing just below Zosia’s cheek.

    There’s a brief pause, then Carol’s arms circle her waist.

    They settle into it without urgency.

    Zosia’s chin rests lightly near Carol’s temple.

    They breathe.

    After a long moment, Zosia pulls back slightly, her hands still at Carol’s sides.

    “Let’s clean up,” she says quietly. “Before we make this harder than it needs to be.”

    Carol searches her face. “Are you okay?”

    Zosia nods. “Yeah. I just… I need a little time to process this. That’s all.”

    Carol nods, though something uncertain still lingers in her eyes.

    They clean in silence, careful but not distant.

    When they finally turn off the kitchen light, Zosia pauses in the doorway.

    She knows this isn’t simple.

    But it isn’t one-sided either.

    And for now, that’s enough.

    Chapter 13: Tuesday part 1

    Zosia wakes to the sound of the shower running.

    For a moment she doesn’t remember where she is.

    Then she does.

    Carol’s guest room. Pale morning light slips through unfamiliar curtains. The faint smell of coffee drifts down the hallway.

    Her chest tightens with a quiet sense of anticipation.

    Today is the book reading, followed by dinner with Carol’s publishers. Helen is coming around noon so they can go over a few details first. After that, they’ll drive to the bookstore together.

    The three of them.

    She sits up, smooths her hair, and listens.

    The shower stops. Doors open and close. Movement in the kitchen.

    Ordinary morning sounds.

    Zosia stays still for a moment, listening to the quiet rhythm of Carol moving around the house.

    Last night comes back before she can stop it.

    Carol’s hands warm at her waist. The way she had stopped her just when she was about to lean in.

    I made a promise.

    Zosia had been ready to be angry. Ready to walk away.

    But Carol had looked at her like someone standing on the edge of something she didn’t trust herself to cross.

    And it hadn’t felt like rejection.

    Zosia exhales softly. It’s the strangest position she’s ever been in.

    Wanted. And held back from.

    She hears Carol set a mug down. The quiet rhythm of someone making breakfast.

    Zosia slips into the robe draped over the chair and walks down the hallway.

    By the time she steps into the kitchen, Carol is already dressed. Dark jeans, soft sweater, glasses on. Coffee mug in hand.

    “Morning,” Carol says, voice still low with sleep.

    “Morning.”

    There’s a beat where they just look at each other.

    Carol clears her throat lightly. “I made omelets. I wasn’t sure what you wanted.”

    “Omelets are perfect.”

    They move carefully around each other in the kitchen, both a little too aware of the other’s presence.

    Then Carol clears her throat. “Listen, if you want to talk about last night…”

    Zosia looks up.

    Carol lifts a shoulder slightly. “We can. I mean, if it’s still on your mind.”

    A small smile pulls at Zosia’s mouth. “Carol,” she says gently, “of course it’s still on my mind.”

    Carol lets out a quiet breath.

    Zosia watches her for a moment, then her expression softens.

    “Let’s talk about it tomorrow,” she says gently. “You’ve got a long day ahead of you.”

    Carol studies her face, making sure she means it.“Are you sure?”

    “Yes.” Zosia leans back against the counter. “Today’s going to be busy. We’ll be home late, there’s probably wine involved.” She exhales softly. “My plane leaves at six tomorrow. We’ll have plenty of time.”

    That draws a small smile from Carol. “Alright.”

    Carol glances at the clock.

    “I should get dressed. Helen will be here in half an hour.”

    The name still lands a little wrong with Zosia. She keeps her expression carefully neutral, doing everything she can not to let it show.

    Carol disappears down the hallway.

    The kitchen grows quiet again.

    Zosia rinses her mug and sets it on the counter, lingering there a moment longer than necessary. She tells herself she’s not waiting.

    And yet she’s still standing there when the bedroom door opens behind her.

    Zosia turns.

    And for a second she completely forgets what she was about to do.

    Carol is wearing a deep red dress that falls perfectly along her frame. Simple, but impossible to ignore. The fabric gathers softly at the neckline, leaving her shoulders and arms bare. Her hair is tucked neatly behind one ear, and the color of the dress makes her eyes look almost impossibly bright.

    Zosia just stares.

    “You look…” she begins, then stops.

    Carol tilts her head slightly. “Is it that bad?”

    Zosia laughs quietly. “No. I just…” She exhales. “You look stunning.”

    A faint blush rises to Carol’s cheeks.

    For a second it feels like the kitchen has gone very still.

    Then the doorbell rings.

    Carol exhales softly. “That’ll be Helen.”

    Something flickers across her face before she smooths it away, the professional composure settling back into place as she walks to the door.

    Helen steps in already mid-sentence. “Traffic is worse than I expected, and the store owner just called…”

    Then she notices Zosia.

    Her gaze lingers for a moment.

    “Morning.”

    “Morning,” Zosia replies.

    Helen sets her bag on the counter and moves through the kitchen with easy familiarity. She straightens the strap of Carol’s dress without asking, then reaches up to smooth a loose strand of hair behind her ear.

    “Your reading starts at two,” she says, already glancing at the schedule in her hand. “They’d like you there a little early.”

    Zosia watches the exchange quietly.

    The movements are small, automatic. Years of habit in the way Helen adjusts the dress, the way Carol stands still long enough for her to finish.

    Carol doesn’t pull away.

    After a moment Helen gestures toward the hallway.

    “Let’s step into the office for a minute.”

    Carol starts to nod, then pauses. Her eyes drift to Zosia first, almost instinctively.

    Zosia lifts one shoulder in a small shrug, trying to look more indifferent than she feels.

    “Sure.” She follows Helen down the hallway.

    Their voices continue inside, quieter now.

    Carol opened the door without a word and turned away immediately, already walking back into the house.

    “Well, that’s a warm welcome,” Helen said dryly as she stepped inside and closed the door herself.

    Carol didn’t answer. She reached the cabinet and poured another whiskey.

    “You need a drink?” she asked without turning around. Then she shrugged. “You need a drink.”

    “I drove here,” Helen said flatly.

    Carol lifted one shoulder, poured the whiskey anyway, and downed it herself.

    Helen looked at her for a long moment.

    “You seem… peaceful.”

    “What do you want, Helen?” Carol asked, already irritated.

    Helen exhaled slowly.

    “I want to talk about an offer I received today. Apparently you made quite an impression yesterday. They want to take the tour overseas.”

    Carol barely reacted.

    “Where?”

    “Europe.”

    Carol gave a humorless laugh.

    “Great. More distance.”

    Helen’s patience snapped.

    “Look, I know you’re in a mood right now. And I’m very sorry your little Raban clone left town, but this is important. I do not have time to manage your emotional tantrums.”

    Carol scoffed.

    “Raban clone.”

    “Oh please,” Helen said. “You think I didn’t notice?”

    Carol let out a tired breath. “I’m not doing this with you.”

    She sank onto the couch.

    Helen stepped forward, hovering over her, anger rising.

    “No Carol, we’re not going down this road again.”

    Her voice hardened.

    “Whatever happened between you and that woman, it’s your responsibility. I’m not fixing another one of your fan situations.”

    Carol got to her feet, raising a finger in frustration.

    “There’s nothing to fix. And there is no ‘road’ to go down, because guess what, Helen… this is a road I’ve never been on before.”

    She hesitated, the words hanging in the air.

    “Not with anyone.”

    The moment the sentence left her mouth, Carol’s expression shifted.

    “I didn’t mean…”

    “Carol,” Helen cut in coldly. “Go fuck yourself.”

    She dropped a folder onto the kitchen counter. The papers inside fanned out slightly.

    Helen turned and walked to the door.

    Before opening it, she glanced back once.

    “Also,” she added coolly, “you might want to cut down on the drinking.”

    The door closed behind her.

    Carol walked over to the folder Helen had left behind.

    Inside were several printed pages, notes, schedules, a rough outline of what a potential European tour might look like. Cities, dates, meetings. The kind of plan Helen loved.

    Carol spread the papers across the kitchen counter and skimmed through them.

    Paris. Berlin. Amsterdam. London.

    Her eyes moved over the list, but her mind barely followed.

    The last thing she wanted right now was an ocean between her and Zosia. Another stretch of distance. And the thought of spending weeks traveling with her ex-wife made the whole proposal feel even heavier.

    She flipped to the last page.

    Four weeks. Leaving in two.

    She exhaled quietly and gathered the papers back into the folder before setting it aside.

    Then she turned and walked back toward the living room. Her phone was still lying on the table where she had left it.

    She picked it up.

    No message yet, which made sense because Zosia’s flight wouldn’t land until midnight.

    The screen lit up with their last conversation still open.

    Her eyes drifted to the final lines.

    If you’d let me, I would move my hand up higher.

    Just how high are we talking?

    High enough to feel how much you wanted me.

    Carol stared at the words for a moment.

    She could still remember exactly how it had felt. Walking down the hallway, the cool tiles beneath her bare feet. Pausing at the guest room door, her stomach tight with anticipation.

    For several seconds she had simply stood there, staring at the door in front of her.

    She had almost turned around.

    Instead she had typed:

    I’m outside your room.

    Her pulse had jumped the second she hit send.

    Then came the waiting. Those few seconds before Zosia opened the door had felt impossibly long.

    Long enough to wonder what she was doing. Long enough to consider leaving.

    But she hadn’t.

    Carol pulled herself back to the present and looked at the phone in her hand.

    Then she started typing.

    Carol: Please let me know when you land and get home safely. Doesn’t matter what time.

    She set her phone on the coffee table and lay back on the couch, staring up at the ceiling.

    The quiet of the house settled around her again.

    She exhaled slowly, letting the pleasant haze of alcohol dull the sharp edges of the day. Her eyes closed before she quite realized it, and sleep pulled her under.

    She didn’t know how long she had slept before the buzzing woke her.

    Still half asleep, she blinked toward the faint glow of her phone on the table.

    With a quiet groan, she sat up and pressed a hand to her temple as the first hints of a headache crept in.

    00:03 AM.

    She reached for the phone.

    Zosia: I’m home.

    A second message followed.

    Zosia: Is it too soon to say I miss you?

    Carol let the words sink in. A faint smile tugged at the corner of her mouth before she could stop it.

    Then the rest of the night caught up with her.

    The fight with Helen drifted back into her thoughts. The plans for Europe. Four weeks.

    A familiar heaviness settled in her chest.

    She wanted to tell Zosia about it. Wanted to hear her voice. But not after drinking half a bottle of whiskey and with her head still spinning from the fight.

    She typed back.

    Carol: You’re not the only one.

    After a moment she added another line.

    Carol: I miss you too.

    Three small dots appeared on the screen.

    Typing.

    They disappeared.

    Then came back again.

    Zosia: Good night.

    Carol let out a quiet breath.

    Carol: Good night. I’ll talk to you tomorrow.

    She pushed herself off the couch and headed down the hallway to the bedroom.

    The bed still carried the faint traces of the last few days. Slightly rumpled sheets, the quiet memory of someone who had just been there.

    Carol slipped under the covers and reached for the pillow Zosia had used.

    That, at least, was enough to convince her to sleep like a normal adult tonight.

    Chapter 17: For Science

    Chapter Notes

    Koumba was already leaning against a wall beside her front door when Zosia walked down the hallway with a bag of Chinese take-out.

    He pushed himself off it when he saw her and grinned.

    “Well, well,” he said. “There she is.”

    She laughed and pulled him into a quick hug before pushing the door open.

    “Come on.”

    Koumba followed her inside, watching her carefully as she kicked off her shoes and carried the bag to the kitchen.

    “So…” He leaned casually against the counter. “Tell me.”

    “Can I please just… settle in first?” Zosia said, already pulling containers out of the bag.

    “Fine.”

    Zosia groaned but nodded. “Thank you.”

    “You look different,” Koumba started. “Like someone who had loads and loads of sex in the past few days.”

    Zosia nearly dropped one of the containers.

    “Koumba!”

    He pointed a chopstick at her. “Tell me I’m wrong.”

    She sat down across from him and gave him a look.

    “You’re unbelievable.”

    Koumba shrugged. “I’m curious.”

    They started filling their plates. The familiar routine helped Zosia relax a little.

    For about ten seconds. 

    Then Koumba moved closer.

    “So,” he began, “first question.”

    Zosia pointed a dumpling at him threateningly.

    “If your first question is about sex, I’m throwing this at you.”

    Koumba paused.

    “…Second question then.”

    She snorted.

    “How was it seeing her again?”

    Zosia felt the smile before she could stop it.

    “It was… amazing,” she corrected. “Terrifying, but amazing.”

    “Was it awkward at all?”

    “Extremely,” she admitted. “But once we realized we were both feeling that awkwardness, it got easier.”

    Koumba made a satisfied sound.

    “So when did it happen?”

    Zosia closed her eyes briefly. “We’re not having this conversation.”

    He grinned.

    “Oh come on, did you sleep with her?”

    Zosia immediately looked around her apartment as if someone might be hiding behind the couch.

    She lowered her voice. “Fine, yes.”

    Her hands came up to cover her face.

    Koumba leaned in. “Well?” His eyebrows lifted. “Was it good?”

    A dumpling flew across the table.

    “What? That’s a very normal question between friends.” He grinned.

    “You’re absolutely not getting details.”

    He raised his hands in surrender. “A simple yes or no will do.”

    Zosia raised an eyebrow at him.

    He studied her, his expression softening.

    “You’re in love with her, aren’t you?” It wasn’t really a question.

    Zosia hesitated.

    “…Yes.”

    She laughed nervously. She hadn’t said it out loud, or even accepted it herself yet.

    “Is she?”

    Zosia shrugged slightly.

    “I don’t know.”

    “Well, she should be. Because you’re amazing.” 

    He lifted his glass. “I’ll be in charge of your bachelorette party.”

    “Let’s not get ahead of ourselves.”

    They ate for another half hour, Koumba occasionally sneaking in another question about Carol, about Albuquerque, about their days.

    Zosia answered most of them. Just not the ones Koumba really wanted.

    Eventually Koumba glanced at the clock on the wall.

    “I should probably go.” Koumba pushed his chair back. “Are we still on for Sunday night?”

    Zosia nodded and started stacking the empty containers.

    “Yes, but not to some loud sweaty club.”

    “Fine. A bar. No sauna, no screaming DJ.”

     

     

    ** Later that evening **

    “Europe?”

    A dozen thoughts collided in Zosia’s mind at once.

    One thought stood out immediately. The distance.

    Though realistically, would a few thousand extra miles even make a difference?

    “When are you leaving?” Carol sighed softly.

    “In twelve days.”

    “And you’ll be gone for four weeks?”

    “Yes.”

    Zosia didn’t respond. Her thumb traced the edge of her phone while she tried to find the right words.

    “Are you still there?” Carol said when the silence went on a little too long.

    “Yes, sorry.” Zosia rubbed her forehead. “I just need a second.”

    “Okay.”

    Carol waited patiently.

    Zosia suddenly let out a short, slightly bitter laugh.

    “You know, I somehow thought I might see you on my birthday next month.”

    A pause.

    “But I guess that’s not happening now.”

    Carol exhaled slowly.

    “No. It isn’t.”

    Zosia pressed her lips together.

    “Is Helen coming with you?” she asked before she could stop herself.

    There was a brief pause.

    “She’s my manager, Zos. Of course she is.”

    “Right.”

    “We had quite a fight yesterday,” Carol said eventually. “So I’m not exactly thrilled about it either.”

    “A fight about what?”

    Carol didn’t answer right away.

    “Let’s just say the timing of her visit wasn’t great. And I said a few things I probably shouldn’t have.”

    “What kind of things?”

    Carol hesitated.

    “Things that would sound completely out of context if you don’t know our history.” Carol exhaled softly. “And that’s not something I want to unpack over the phone.”

    Zosia drummed her fingers lightly on the counter, a little impatient before she could stop herself.

    “Look.” Carol continued softly. “I know you don’t do well with uncertainty.”

    Zosia stilled her hand.

    “But this was my marriage we’re talking about. Helen has been part of my life for nearly two decades. I’m not going to reduce that to a phone conversation.”

    “You’re right.” Zosia lowered her voice. “I’m sorry.”

    She rubbed her forehead.

    “I’m just a little lost right now.”

    “What do you mean?” Carol asked.

    Zosia took a slow breath.

    “I mean I have no idea when I’m going to see you again. It’s driving me insane,” she admitted. “And it’s only been a day.”

    Carol waited a beat.

    “I know,” she said softly. “I feel that too. And that’s exactly why I was so on edge yesterday when Helen showed up.”

    Zosia considered that.

    “She doesn’t like me, does she?”

    Carol let out a small laugh.

    “Not in the slightest.”

    Zosia smiled to herself.

    “Good. I don’t like her either.”

    Carol huffed an amused breath.

    “Very mature, Zosia.”

    “Hey, I never claimed to be the bigger person.”

    A quiet pause followed, but the tension between them had softened.

    Carol broke the silence. “Six weeks. Then I’ll see you again.”

    Zosia’s lips curved faintly. “Promise?”

    “Promise.”

    They stayed on the phone a while longer, drifting between small talk and comfortable silence.

     

     

    ** Sunday night **

    “God, I miss her so much, Koumba.” Zosia said, leaning heavily against the bar.

    He looked at her over the rim of his glass, wondering if that fifth cocktail had been a wise choice.

    “I know. But you will see her again.”

    “In six weeks!” She sounded desperate.

    Koumba shrugged. “Why don't you go visit her in Europe? You’re from the motherland, aren’t you?”

    “I have a store!”

    He pulled an arm around her shoulders. “And you have me.”

    Zosia signaled the bartender and ordered another round of shots.

    “Come on, last one,” she said, pushing a shot toward him.

    “Fine, but after that it’s water for you.”

    She downed the shot and set it down on the bar.

    “I miss her so much.”

    “Yeah, we’ve had this conversation already.”

    Zosia lifted her glass, realized it was empty, and set it down with unnecessary seriousness.

    “I miss her eyes, her smile, her hands. God the things she can do with her hands.” Zosia stared into the distance, clearly replaying something in her head. “Koum, the sex is so good. Sooo good!”

    He nearly choked on his drink. “No. You do not talk about this, remember?”

    “Well, now I do! And you always ask, so now you have to listen!”

    “I ask because it makes you uncomfortable, which is funny. Now you’re making me uncomfortable.” He glanced around the bar. “Not cool.”

    She ignored him.

    “Listen,” Zosia continued, gesturing vaguely. “I was not prepared for that level of skill. She knows exactly where to touch me. How long, how fast, how deep.”

    Koumba nearly spat out his drink. “Please stop.”

    “No! This is important information.”

    “For who?!”

    “For science,” she said seriously.

    He rubbed his face. “I hate it here.”

    “Anyway,” she continued. “She fuc…” Zosia stopped herself, lifting a finger. “No. She made love to me in the kitchen.”

    He slowly set his glass down. “I am going to walk into traffic.”

    “No wait, the best part was later in the bedroom.”

    Koumba grabbed his drink again. “Of course there’s a best part.”

    “I made her come three times in like five minutes.”

    Koumba froze.

    “With just my mouth.”

    “I’m begging you.”

    “She did that as well.”

    He looked around the bar helplessly. “I am going to pretend I didn’t hear that.”

    Zosia leaned forward proudly. “But you did.”

    She scooted closer, dropping her voice. “And then I sat on top of her and she…”

    “Zosia, no, please!” He held a finger to her lips. “I am really happy for you, but you are absolutely going to regret telling me all of this tomorrow.”

    She thought about it for a moment.

    “…Worth it.”

    “Here,” Koumba handing her a glass. “Drink some water.”

    Zosia took it but didn’t even look at the glass.

    “I should text her,” she muttered.

    Koumba immediately grabbed the phone out of her hand.

    “You will do no such thing.”

    He glanced over her shoulder toward the entrance. “Shit.”

    Zosia frowned. “What?”

    “Nothing,” he gestured toward the door. “Let’s just go.”

    He gently tried to guide her away from the bar, but it was too late.

    “Zosia.”

    She turned around and immediately wished she hadn’t. For several reasons.

    “Juliette.”

    They stared at each other for a moment.

    “Koumba,” Koumba said finally. “Since apparently nobody else was going to.”

    Juliette ignored him.

    “You weren’t in the store the other day,” she tilted her head slightly. “Joanna was there. It was lovely seeing her again.”

    Zosia forced a small smile. “I’m sure the feeling was mutual.”

    “She told me you were in Albuquerque for a few days.” Juliette tilted her head slightly. “What on earth were you doing there?”

    “The real question,” Koumba cut in smoothly, “is who she was doing there.”

    Juliette looked at him.

    Then back at Zosia.

    “I see.”

    The words were calm, but something in Juliette’s tone made Zosia’s stomach twist.

    “Well.” Koumba spread his hands. “Mystery solved.”

    Juliette ignored him again.

    “She must be really something.”

    Her eyes stayed on Zosia.

    “You wouldn’t even drive to Boston when I was there for three weeks.”

    Zosia lifted a shoulder.

    “Yeah, well. And you couldn’t even stay out of your ex’s pants while we were together.”

    For a second Juliette’s expression flickered.

    “Right. Because you were perfect.”

    Koumba stepped forward, placing himself between them with an easy smile.

    “Okay.” He gestured toward the door. “And that’s our cue to leave.”

    He rested a gentle hand on Zosia’s arm.

    “We were just heading out anyway.”

    Koumba steered her toward the door before the conversation could flare up again. “Come on.”

    The cool night air hit them as they stepped outside.

    Zosia stopped on the sidewalk and blinked.

    “Whoa.”

    Koumba kept a steady hand on her arm. “Yeah. That’s called fresh air.”

    “No.” She squinted slightly. “The street is… moving.”

    “That would be the alcohol.”

    She took a slow breath, the cold air cutting through the warm haze in her head.

    “I am making the same mistake, aren’t I?”

    Zosia ran a hand over her face.

    “She’s going to Europe for a month, with her ex-wife.”

    “Her manager,” Koumba corrected her.

    “She’s still her ex.”

    The alcohol was making her words spill out faster now.

    “They’ll be traveling together. Hotels, flights, events, dinners.”

    Koumba sighed quietly.

    “Zos…”

    “And I’m just… here.”

    She let herself fall against a parked car, pressing her palms over her face. After a moment she bent forward, bracing herself.

    “I think I’m going to be sick,” she slurred. “I drank too much.”

    “Yeah you did.” Koumba replied. “Come on, let’s take you home.”

     

    ——

     

    The next morning she woke up with a hangover of biblical proportions.

    She had no idea how she got into her bed, though she vaguely remembered Koumba being there at some point.

    Her head throbbed as she looked around for her phone.

    On the nightstand sat a glass of water, two paracetamol, and a note.

    For your own safety I put your phone in the living room. -K

    She smiled, but also hated him a little for making her walk all the way there.

    When she finally reached the living room, there was one message on her phone.

    Carol: Hi, how’s your head feeling?

    She typed back.

    Zosia: Not so good. How do you know that?

    Carol: Please call me when you have time.

    Zosia hit the call button right away.

    The phone barely rang before Carol picked up.

    “Hi.”

    Her voice sounded softer than usual, a hint of concern in it.

    Zosia leaned against the kitchen counter. “Hi.”

    “How are you feeling?” Carol’s voice was soft.

    “Like I was hit by a bus.” Zosia rubbed her temples. “And then the bus reversed.”

    Carol let out a quiet laugh.

    “I suspected as much.”

    “Why?”

    Carol’s voice softened even more. “Koumba sent me a DM in the middle of the night.”

    “What?” She winced, the sound stabbing through her head.

    “He’s a very good friend, Zos. You’re lucky to have him.”

    Zosia wasn’t so sure about that right now. “What did he tell you?”

    Carol chuckled softly. “He told me you had quite a bit to drink. That he brought you home and put your phone out of reach.”

    She took her time before speaking again.

    “He also said there’s something bothering you and that you should talk to me about it.” Her voice softened again. “He didn’t say what.”

    Zosia didn’t respond. She wasn’t sure what to think of that.

    “Right.”

    “Please tell me what’s bothering you.”

    Zosia hesitated. She considered brushing it off.

    Instead she took a slow breath and told Carol about Juliette.

    How she had cheated on her with her ex, how they had run into each other at the bar, and how she couldn’t stop comparing that situation to the one between Carol and Helen.

    Carol didn’t interrupt once.

    When Zosia finished, the line stayed quiet.

    “I’m really glad you told me that,” Carol said finally. “What she did to you was awful, and seeing her again must have brought a lot of that back. I understand why you’d be worried about that happening again.”

    Zosia leaned her forehead against the cabinet, letting out a slow breath.

    “But what happened with Juliette isn’t what’s happening here.” Carol kept her voice calm. “Helen and I have a long history, and we’ll probably always have some kind of connection because of work. But the relationship part of that ended a long time ago.”

    She paused for a moment.

    “Besides, if I still wanted to be with Helen, I wouldn’t have told you about what happened two months ago,” she continued. “I told you because I’m not confused about that. I’m not secretly trying to decide between you and her. That question doesn’t exist for me.”

    A brief silence followed.

    “Because I want to be with you.”

    Zosia closed her eyes, relief spreading through her chest.

    She hadn’t realized how badly she needed to hear that.

    “Thank you for saying that.” She closed her eyes briefly.

    Then she let out a small laugh that quickly turned into a groan. “Well… this might be the only good thing that came out of last night.”

    Carol smiled. “That laugh sounded painful.”

    “It was.”

    “Go drink some water. And please… you can relax about this.”

    “I’ll try,” “I’ll try.” Zosia’s voice was a little uncertain.

    Carol laughed quietly. “Good enough.”

    Not long after, the call ended.

    Zosia put her phone down and collapsed onto the couch, a helpless smile spreading across her face.

    She wants to be with me.

    Then suddenly she shot upright.

    “Oh no… no, no, no.”

    She reached for her phone and called Koumba.

    “Please tell me I didn’t.”

    “You’re alive,” he said. “How are you feeling?”

    “What did I tell you yesterday?”

    She heard him laughing on the other end.

    “You know… I barely remember.”

    “This is going to come back and haunt me, isn’t it?”

    “It might.” He chuckled softly. “But not today.”

    “Ugh… I am so sorry!”

    “Really, Zos. It’s okay. We’ll never mention it again.”

    She covered her face with her hand in shame.

    “Fine. Thanks for getting me home,” she added, “And for talking to Carol.”

    “Did everything turn out okay?”

    “More than okay.”

    “I’m glad.” He replied. “And Zos?”

    “What?”

    “Hypothetically speaking. Was it really three times in a row with just your…”

    She hung up and threw her phone into the corner of the couch with a frustrated groan.

    She knew Koumba was laughing right now.

    God, she hated this.

     

     

    Chapter End Notes

    Fun fact!

    When I was in New York a couple of years ago, I spoke to some random guy on the subway. He asked me where I was from, so I told him Amsterdam.
    Then he replied, “Oh, you’re from the motherland!”

    I have no idea if that’s a common phrase, but I liked it and it stuck with me, so I decided to use it in this story. :)

    Chapter 18: Berlin

    The next eleven days passed faster than Zosia expected.

    They texted constantly. Called every night. Sent photos of small things. Morning coffee, cloudy skies, dinner choices, whatever happened to be in front of them.

    Then the eleven days ended.

    Time zones were going to be a problem. Carol’s schedule wasn’t exactly flexible either. The next four weeks weren’t going to be easy.

    And just like that, the strange little bubble they had been living in for the past eleven days burst.

    Zosia woke up to a message from Carol.

    Carol: Landed in Berlin.

    She typed back.

    Zosia: How was the flight?

    The three dots popped up right away.

    Carol: Long. Loud. The person next to me snored like a dying tractor.

    Zosia: Weren’t you sitting next to Helen?

    Carol: Yes.

    Zosia let out a quiet laugh and rolled onto her back.

    Zosia: I shouldn't laugh about that, but I did.

    There was a short pause before the typing dots appeared again.

    Carol: I miss your laugh.

    Zosia stared at the message.

    Her fingers hovered over the keyboard before she answered.

    Zosia: I miss you.

    In these past few days they had become almost sickeningly affectionate, with constant ‘miss yous’ and ‘thinking of yous’. Zosia never would have guessed she would be that kind of person. 

    Yet, here she was.

    She had started referring to Carol as her girlfriend when talking to people, though she wouldn’t dare say it in front of her yet.

    Joanna had already informed their parents about Carol.

    Which was how Zosia now found herself sitting at the dinner table, feeling vaguely like a suspect in an interrogation.

    She tried to leave the part of the Wycaro books from her grandmother out of it. But she should have known that was Joanna’s favorite part of the story. 

    So soon the conversation had drifted from mild shock, into fond memories of her grandmother.

    Eventually, the nostalgia faded and the interrogation resumed.

    “So, she’s famous?” Her father asked.

    Zosia hated that question. Helen’s accusation about her being just another fan still echoed in her mind.

    “She’s well known in her field.”

    “And how old is she?” Her mother continued.

    Zosia sighed. “She’s nine years older. Fifty-three.”

    Her father made a quiet, disapproving sound.

    “Do you have a photo of her?” Her mother asked.

    Zosia quickly googled Carol and held up her phone, showing them a few pictures.

    Her mother shook her head.

    “No. I mean a picture of the two of you.”

    Zosia hesitated. “I… don’t have one.”

    Her mother raised an eyebrow.

    “You spent four days together and didn’t take a single picture?”

    Zosia shifted in her chair.

    “Well… no.”

    “That’s awkward,” Joanna murmured, just loud enough for Zosia to hear.

    Zosia shot her a warning look.

    She scrolled through her camera roll anyway, pretending to check.

    Food. More food. The New Mexico desert. Drinks. The Alamo. Even more food.

    But not a single picture of her and Carol together.

    She sighed and opened their chat.

    Zosia: I just realized I don’t have a photo of you.

    It was almost midnight in Berlin, yet the typing bubbles appeared immediately.

    Carol: There are plenty of those on the internet.

    Zosia: You know what I mean.

    A photo popped up in the chat.

    Carol was in bed, half hidden under a blanket, her hair slightly messy, clearly tired—but smiling.

    Zosia felt her heart skip.

    Zosia: You’re so beautiful.

    She studied the screen before typing again.

    Zosia: Unfortunately, I can’t show that one to my mother.

    Carol: Zosia!! No, please don’t show that!

    Zosia tried to suppress a laugh.

    Zosia: Relax. I won’t.

    Carol: Wait… are you talking to your parents about me?

    Zosia blinked at the screen.

    Right. That was information Carol definitely hadn’t had before.

    Zosia: Technically my sister told them. I had very little control over the situation.

    Zosia held her breath, waiting.

    Carol: So your parents know about me?

    Zosia: They do.

    Carol: And?

    Zosia glanced up at the table.

    Her mother and Joanna were both pretending not to stare. Her father wasn’t even pretending.

    She glanced back at the screen.

    Zosia: I’m pretty sure they’re about thirty seconds away from asking to meet you.

    The typing bubbles appeared instantly.

    Carol: I woke up in Berlin, and somehow gained in-laws overnight.

    Zosia gave a small grin.

    Zosia: Let’s maybe slow down before you start calling them that.

    Carol: Too late. I’m part of the family now.

    Zosia smiled at her phone like an idiot. 

    When she looked up again, three identical expressions of quiet curiosity were staring back at her.

    Zosia slowly lowered her phone. “What?”

    “You tell us.” Joanna said.

    It felt quiet.

    “Is it about her?”

    Zosia’s breath caught. She turned the volume all the way up but couldn’t hear Carol’s answer.

    Then Helen spoke again, colder now.

    “Because if it is, you need to shut it down immediately.”

    Zosia’s fingers tightened around the phone.

    Zosia froze.

    Zosia’s heart skipped once.

    “Wait…”

    The screen went black.

    The call ended.

    Zosia sat very still on the couch, staring at her own reflection in the dark screen.

    “This isn’t good.” she murmured quietly.

    Chapter 20: Carol's POV

    Carol felt her stomach drop.

    She reached for her phone to check.

     

    Hi Carol, I’m a friend of Zosia's. We’ve met once. 

    I just wanted to let you know Zosia had a bit too much to drink tonight. It got to the point where she was getting emotionally distressed. I got her home safe and put her to bed. 

    As her friend, I felt like I needed to step in and let you know something’s been affecting her.

    It’s nothing bad, just her own insecurities getting in the way.

    I think it would really help if you asked her about it. She won’t bring it up herself.

    - Koumba

    The next morning, when they finally spoke, it started to make sense. Zosia had been through something before. Cheating, empty promises, the kind of experience that sticks with you and changes how you see everything that follows.

    And now she thought she recognized the same pattern in Carol and Helen.

    It wasn’t irrational. If anything, it was painfully understandable.

    Carol wanted, whatever this was between her and Zosia, to work. She didn’t fully understand it herself yet because it had all unfolded so quickly. But she knew it felt right.

    So she did her best to reassure her.

    After the call ended, she sent a quick message to Koumba, letting him know the conversation had gone well, and that Zosia was okay.

    She added, almost as an afterthought, that she really was trying to do the right thing here.

    She hadn’t made any real effort to understand who Zosia actually was. In Helen’s mind, she was nothing more than a fan with a crush. Someone temporary who would inevitably break Carol's heart.

    They did talk about logistics. Four weeks of traveling together required at least that much. Dates, transport, schedules, the practical things that couldn’t be avoided.

    Usually, this was the part they enjoyed. Planning their free time, mapping out museums, choosing restaurants. It had once felt effortless.

    This time, they kept their free time separate. They would each do their own thing outside of work, and that was, according to her, perfectly fine. Maybe even healthier, considering her ex-wife had a tendency to be either overly protective or quietly jealous.

    Carol wanted that, of course. But with every message, she found herself choosing her words carefully, hoping they would reassure Zosia. Each text, each photo, a quiet way of saying she was thinking of her, not Helen.

    A tight feeling in her throat came up. This was serious and scary territory. She had broken any form of contact with her own family over 25 years ago, and Helen's family was also something she had always wanted to keep at a safe distance. So this was not something she was very thrilled about.

    She had two options. Option one was letting Zosia know this was a bit out of her comfort zone. But knowing how fondly she had spoken about her family, this could backfire immensely. Option two, she could deflect. Make a joke. Wrap it in sarcasm, the way she always did when something got too close, too real.

    I woke up in Berlin, and somehow gained in-laws overnight.

    Too late. I’m part of the family now.

She knew how to play the part, how to keep them at just the right distance. In photos, though, there was no distance. Carol always knew how to hide that.

    It didn’t take long for her to understand Jana’s real intentions.

    What are you doing? Zosia had asked her.

    She checked her phone after she hung up and smiled.

    Helen’s voice was suddenly very close by.

    She went back to her seat and put on her headset as a silent reminder to Helen that they did not need to talk anymore. She started answering the messages.

    Comic Con was a special kind of hell. Too many people, too much noise, the constant echoing of the halls pressing in on her until it became almost unbearable. And the energy of it all, the endless stream of faces and voices, drained her faster than she could recover.

    Still, she smiled and pushed through. She always did.

    Jana. 

    Carol couldn’t even begin to count how many books she had signed for her over the years. All she knew was that it far exceeded anything she herself had ever written.

    And the worst part? They were always personalized. Every single one addressed to Jana. Not for resale, not for show, just… kept.

    “Listen, Carol,” she said, stepping a little closer, “if you want, we could also meet a little earlier for drinks. Just the two of us.”

    “But I’d still love to join you and the others tonight,” she added gently, resting a hand on Jana’s shoulder. “If that’s okay.”

    A small reassurance. A boundary, slightly crossed with that single touch. But it was enough. A small smile reappeared on her face as she nodded.

    Carol’s eyes flickered toward the laptop briefly, wondering if Zosia was still there.

    Chapter 21: Birthday dinner

    Zosia stood in the kitchen, halfway through cooking her birthday dinner. The dinner itself wasn’t anything remarkable. The real achievement was getting through it without sending something clattering every thirty seconds in the cramped space.

    Voices came from the table behind her. Koumba was already two glasses in when her parents arrived, her sister not long after, and the wine had him doing most of the talking while the others listened.

    Carol had called her early that morning from the airport, waiting for her flight to London, just to wish her a happy birthday. She had a full day ahead and wouldn’t be able to call again until late that evening, though she promised to try. The call lingered long after it ended, making it impossible for Zosia to ignore the one thing that mattered most. Carol wouldn’t be there.

    She checked the oven one last time and was reaching for the plates when the doorbell rang.

    “Can somebody get that?” she called over her  shoulder.

    Joanna was on her feet before anyone else moved. “I’ve got it!”

    Zosia heard the front door open, followed by a brief exchange of voices. Then her sister laughed. “Oh wow.”

    Zosia frowned slightly, wiping her hands on a kitchen towel. “What is it?” she asked, raising her voice.

    The door opened a fraction and Joanna leaned in. “You might want to come here.”

    Zosia stepped out, and Joanna shifted aside to let her see.

    A delivery man stood there holding an enormous bouquet of red roses. Zosia just stared, caught off guard. There had to be dozens.

    “Delivery for Zosia?” the man said, checking the card attached to the bouquet.

    “That’s me,” she said, still trying to process what she was looking at.

    He pressed the roses into her hands. They felt unexpectedly heavy, their scent quickly taking over the space.

    Her mother appeared behind them, eyebrows rising. “Good lord.”

    “How many are there?” her father asked from the table.

    Joanna leaned closer, counting quickly.

    “…Forty-five.”

    Zosia felt a slow warmth spread through her chest when she found the card. She read it out loud.

     

    Happy birthday, Zosia.

    Forty-five roses for a woman who has made my days brighter, my thoughts more interesting, and my life far more delightful than it was before.

    — Carol

     

    Koumba let out a long whistle. “The romance author strikes again.”

    Her father stepped into the hallway and looked at the flowers. “That must have cost a fortune.”

    Zosia rolled her eyes, a small smile tugging at her mouth.

    Her mother gently took the card and read it again.

    “Well,” she said, handing it back. “She certainly knows how to make an impression.”

    Zosia felt the warmth rise in her cheeks.

    “And where are you even going to put them?”

    Zosia looked down at the enormous bouquet in her arms, then around her small apartment. Clearly at a loss.

    “Everywhere, I guess?”

    Zosia stepped back to look at them. Insane.

    “Is dinner still happening tonight, or are we just admiring flowers for the next hour?” Her father called out.

    Zosia huffed a soft, amused breath, then picked up her phone.

    Zosia: Thank you for the roses.

    Zosia: This might be the most over-the-top thing anyone’s done for me.

    Carol: I’m glad you like them.

    Carol: You deserved something more than a call.

    Zosia: They’re beautiful.

    Another short pause.

    Carol: Enjoy your evening, Zosia.

    Zosia: I will.

    Joanna leaned back in her chair. “Dinner is getting cold!”

    Zosia slipped the phone into her pocket and carried the last dish to the table.

    Koumba immediately raised his glass. “To Zosia.”

    Glasses clinked, and the conversation shifted back to dinner. They were a few minutes into the meal when the doorbell rang again.

    Everyone paused.

    Zosia frowned, a little annoyed. “No.”

    Koumba immediately straightened. “I’ll get it. Maybe it’s more roses.”

    “God, I hope not.” Her father muttered.

    “So,” he said. “You’re the famous author.”

    — Carol

    Relief flickered across Carol’s face, followed by a small smile.

    Laughter returned to the table, and the evening settled into something easy and warm. More wine was poured, and someone brought out the last of the dessert.

    Chapter 22: The Divorce

    Chapter Notes

    Thank you all so much for the comments (and the one marriage proposal).
    It might sound like a cliché, but you’ve genuinely brightened my week. 😊

    Morning light had already started to creep through the curtains, soft and pale against the sheets.

    Zosia collapsed on top of her, breathing hard, her forehead resting against Carol’s shoulder like she didn’t have the energy to hold herself up anymore. Carol’s chest rose quickly beneath her, a soft, breathless laugh slipping out as her hands came up to steady her, keeping her close.

    For a moment, it was just that, uneven breathing, the quiet shift of sheets, the small sounds neither of them bothered to hide. Their skin was still warm, bodies close, not quite ready to move apart yet.

    Carol’s fingers slid slowly up Zosia’s back, tracing absentminded lines through the thin sheen of sweat. “I can get used to this,” she murmured, voice low and rough, more breath than sound.

    Zosia didn’t answer at first. She just pressed closer, turning her face into the curve of Carol’s neck, breathing her in slowly. Her arm tightened where it rested over Carol’s waist, holding on.

    “Good thing you bought me those plane tickets then,” she said finally, softer now, her voice steadier than her breathing.

    Carol smiled faintly, though Zosia couldn’t see it. Her hand stilled at the nape of Zosia’s neck, thumb brushing back and forth in a slow, soothing rhythm. The urgency from moments before had burned itself out, leaving something quieter in its wake.

    Gradually, the tension drained from them.

    “I was afraid you were going to say ‘no’,” Carol admitted.

    Zosia lifted her head, studying her. “Really?”

    Carol nodded. “Koumba and Joanna were convinced you wouldn’t. So… I took the risk.”

    Zosia’s expression softened. “I’m glad you did.”

    Carol brushed her fingers along Zosia’s cheek, lingering there. “Doesn’t it scare you?” she asked quietly. “Spending three weeks with someone you barely know?”

    Zosia pushed herself upright, searching her face. “Is that how you feel? That you barely know me?”

    Carol hesitated, her gaze softening. “No,” she said gently. “But also… a little.”

    A small, almost uncertain smile tugged at her lips.

    “I just have this feeling,” she continued, her fingers trailing down Zosia’s arm, raising goosebumps in their wake, “that this is something special.” She exhaled softly. “And it scares me. But then I thought… what do I even have to lose anymore?”

    Zosia raised an eyebrow. “That’s slightly dramatic.”

    Carol held her gaze, something apologetic settling in her expression.

    “How much of my conversation with Helen did you hear, on FaceTime?”

    Zosia’s cheeks turned slightly red. “I heard some of it.”

    Carol stared up at the ceiling, drawing in a slow breath before letting it out.

    “She doesn’t know I’m here,” she said after a moment. “My phone’s somewhere in my bag, and I just know that if I check it, there’ll be thirty angry messages, twenty voicemails, and a flood of furious emojis waiting for me.”

    Zosia frowned.

    “I sent her a message saying I was leaving for three days,” Carol went on. “And I added you to every hotel booking on the tour. I didn’t tell her that part… but she’ll know by now. She gets all the confirmations.”

    “Carol…” Zosia’s voice softened, concern creeping in. “Why would you do it like that?”

    “Because I had to.” The answer came too quickly, like if she hesitated, she might not say it at all.

    “But why?” Zosia pressed gently. “She’s your manager,” her hand found Carol’s, fingers lacing together. “You can’t just disappear for three days and add me to the tour without talking to her first.”

    “It will be fine,” Carol assured her.

    “What do you mean?”

    “What I mean is…” she paused. “I think it’s time you told me a little bit more about this weird dynamic between the two of you.”

    Zosia’s hand moved slowly through Carol’s hair, fingers threading, then smoothing, over and over in an easy, steady rhythm. No pressure behind it. Just presence.

    Chapter 23: The Graham Norton Show

    Zosia was staring out of the window, overlooking the ocean. They had four more hours left before landing.

    She turned her head.

    Carol sat beside her, laptop open, fingers moving steadily across the keys. Focused. Unbothered. Like the world outside the window didn’t exist. She had clearly done this many times before.

    Zosia watched her for a moment.

    She straightened a little, nudging her knee lightly against Carol’s. “Do you know what kind of questions you’re going to get on the Graham Norton show?”

    “I do,” Carol said. “Helen sent them to me, along with the answers. Of course.”

    Zosia raised an eyebrow. “What are the questions?”

    Carol glanced at her, that same quiet, knowing look settling in again.

    “What fun would it be,” she said, “if you knew everything beforehand?”

    Zosia gave a low, playful hum, her gaze narrowing just a touch.

    She glanced back out the window, the ocean unchanged, endless, then turned her head toward Carol again.

    A small breath.

    She shifted closer, her knee pressing more firmly against Carol’s, letting it stay there.

    “When can I read the first draft?”

    Carol didn’t look up immediately. “Who says you can?”

    “Oh, come on.” Zosia turned fully toward her.

    Carol glanced up, a small smile forming on her lips. Easy and familiar, the kind that made Zosia’s heart melt every time.

    “You more or less told me I was the inspiration for your new book.”

    “Did I, though?” Carol said lightly.

    Before Zosia could respond, Carol leaned in and pressed a quick, casual kiss to her mouth.

    Zosia blinked, caught off guard. Not by the kiss itself, but by how easily it happened.

    She studied her. “You never did that in public before.”

    Carol leaned back like it was nothing, setting her focus back on the screen in front of her. “We’ve hardly been in public before.”

    Zosia huffed a small breath, realizing that was true.

    She shifted closer, angling into Carol’s side, her shoulder brushing against hers.

    “Am I going to be the hero in your story?”

    “No,” Carol said without hesitation. “You’re going to be one of those little goblins that pop up from the purple sand.”

    Zosia stared at her, then smacked her arm. “Shut up.”

    “I stand corrected,” Carol continued, completely unfazed. “A goblin with a bad temper.”

    Zosia’s mouth curved faintly as she leaned in, resting her head against Carol’s shoulder.

    For a moment, Carol didn’t move. Her fingers hovered over the keyboard before she closed the laptop in one smooth motion.

    “I don’t trust you.”

    Zosia lifted her head slightly. “What? Why?”

    Carol turned her head, studying her. 

    “You’re trying to distract me so you can read it.”

    Zosia’s laugh came a fraction too fast. “No, I wasn’t.”

    Carol raised an eyebrow.

    “Fine,” she admitted. “I just want to know what it’s about.”

    Carol slid an arm around her and drew her back in, settling her against her shoulder again.

    “Alright… just stay here a second.” She pulled her a little closer. Then exhaled, almost like she needed it. 

    “It’s not necessarily a character that’s based on you,” she said. “Or me.”

    Zosia didn’t move, her attention shifting entirely to her voice.

    “It’s about what happens when something interrupts everything you thought you understood.”

    Carol took a breath before continuing. Like she was figuring it out as she said it. “That moment where your brain tries to keep up, but it’s already too late. Because something already changed before you had words for it.”

    Zosia lifted her head from Carol’s shoulder, looking at her.

    “When did that happen?”

    Carol didn’t look away. “When I first saw you.”

    Zosia studied her face, searching for the part that might be exaggerated. She didn’t find one.

    Then Zosia leaned in slowly, leaving space for Carol to stop her.

    She didn’t.

    The kiss wasn’t rushed this time. Tentative at first, then deepening into something steadier, something they both seemed to recognize without needing to name it.

    When they pulled back, they stayed close.

    “You know you just made it worse, right?”

    Carol let out a quiet breath. “How?”

    “Now I really want to read it.”

    Carol met her eyes. “I’d like to get it right first.”

    Zosia settled back onto her shoulder. She shifted once, just enough to get comfortable, then went still.

    And for once, she didn’t try to fill the silence.

    The studio felt too bright.

    Not just the lights, but how everything felt more exposed under them.

    Zosia sat in the front row, hands loosely clasped in her lap. Close enough that she could see everything. Close enough that there was no hiding if something went wrong.

    Her eyes stayed on Carol.

    Carol shifted slightly on the red couch, crossing one knee over the other, her hand smoothing over it as the applause faded. She looked calm. She always did. But Zosia knew she wasn’t. Not this time.

    Across from her, Graham Norton studied his cards with theatrical interest.

    “So,” he said, glancing up, “your new book, Bloodsong of Wycaro, is doing very well. Congratulations. People seem to love it.”

    Zosia watched the small smile that appeared on Carol’s face.

    “Thank you.”

    “And I’ve also heard you’ve already started writing the next one.”

    Zosia’s attention sharpened.

    Carol let out a soft laugh. “Yeah… I might have mentioned that.”

    “Oh, you did,” Graham said immediately.

    The audience cheered.

    Zosia felt the sound move through her chest, but she did not react. Her focus stayed fixed on Carol.

    This was the part Helen had gotten angry about in Amsterdam. The part that Carol probably had to prepare for. 

    Damage control.

    Zosia glanced to the side.

    Helen stood just offstage, perfectly still, but not relaxed. Her posture was too straight, her focus too sharp. Watching. Waiting.

    “Well,” Graham gestured toward the crowd, eyebrows raised. “either purple is back in style… or your fan club is here.”

    The audience laughed. 

    Zosia’s lips moved into the smallest smile.

    “I think we can safely assume which one it is,” Graham said.

    Then, he leaned in slightly, back to Carol. “Now, you said something interesting. You said it’s set in the Wycaro universe…but,” he added, raising a finger, “it’s more personal.”

    Zosia felt something tighten in her chest. 

    Out of the corner of her eye, she saw Helen shift. Her arms folded, jaw setting just a little tighter.

    He looked at Carol. “How does that work?”

    A few laughs.

    Carol smiled, but she took a second before answering.

    “I think,” she said slowly, “it’s about identity in a way that’s less abstract. Less disguised.”

    The room stayed with her.

    “But it’s still a romance?” he asked, though he clearly knew the answer.

    “It is.”

    “Well, thank God,” he replied, placing a hand on his chest. “You had us worried for a second.”

    The audience laughed.

    “But,” he continued, glancing sideways, “when authors say ‘personal’ in romance, it usually means one thing…”

    Zosia’s stomach dropped. She knew where this was going. 

    Helen moved.

    Not much, but enough. One step closer to the edge of the stage, into Carol’s line of sight now. A silent reminder. A warning.

    “So…” Graham turned toward the audience. “Do I ask the question?”

    They cheered immediately.

    “The room has spoken,” he said. “Is there someone special in your life that inspired the story?”

    Chapter 24: The Promo

    Zosia was still half-asleep when her phone started vibrating against the nightstand. Once, twice, then continuously, like it had decided she wasn’t getting any more sleep.

    Chapter 25: Cracks

    Zosia felt herself pause for a second, just taking it in.

    Two days ago, Carol had fallen apart in her arms.
