    “What do you mean?”

    Zosia swallowed. “She drinks more than she lets on. Not just… casually. She…” she searched for the word, then gave up. “She does it for the wrong reasons.”

    Koumba didn’t interrupt.

    “And her mood just… switches,” Zosia went on. “One moment she’s fine, or she’s trying to be, and then something shifts and she just pulls away from everything.”

    She thought of the other night. The bar. The silence. The tears.

    There was a pause on the other end.

    Koumba frowned slightly. “Have you talked to her about it?”

    Zosia let out a small, humorless breath. “I tried.”

    “What does that mean?”

    “It means she just closes off,” she said. “Or deflects.”

    Koumba’s expression shifted, something more serious settling in.

    Zosia let out a small breath. “I don’t know what I’m supposed to do.”

    “This is not something you can fix for her.”

    Zosia closed her eyes for a second.

    “I know,” she said quietly. “But I can’t just do nothing.”

    “I didn’t say you should,” Koumba replied.

    Zosia shifted slightly, turning her head toward the empty side of the bed.

    “Carol is scared,” she said after a moment. “Of everything changing. Of not knowing what happens next.”

    “Makes sense.”

    “And when she gets scared, she withdraws,” Zosia added. “Or drinks. Or both.”

    Silence stretched for a second.

    “Do you still like her?” he asked then.

    Zosia let out a quiet, almost tired laugh. “No… I love her.”

    “That’s unfortunate.”

    “Very.”

    He smiled a little. “Just… don’t lose yourself, okay? Be careful.”

    Zosia nodded. “I will.”

    “Good.”

    Zosia’s eyes flicked toward the door as it opened.

    She looked back at the screen. “I should go.”

    Koumba didn’t ask, he just gave a small nod. “Yeah. Call me later.”

    “I will.”

    The call ended.

    Zosia looked up from the bed.

    Carol stepped in. She seemed closed off, like she was keeping everything in and holding herself together.

    “Hey.” Zosia sat up a little.

    Carol didn’t answer right away. She closed the door behind her, then dropped her bag somewhere near the chair.

    “Hey,” she said after a moment, her voice flat, with little energy behind it.

    Zosia watched her for a second. There was definitely something off.

    “Did the interview go okay?” she asked, keeping her tone light.

    Carol shrugged. “Yeah. Fine.”

    She didn’t elaborate. Didn’t look at her for long either. Her gaze flicking away, already somewhere else.

    Zosia shifted, turning toward her a bit more. “What questions did you get?”

    “Same things,” Carol muttered. “Book. Show. My private life.”

    Zosia moved a little closer.

    For a second, she thought she smelled it. Or maybe she just expected to. Not enough to question. Not enough to be sure. She hesitated for half a second.

    “You…” she started, softer now. “Have you been…”

    Carol didn’t let her finish.

    Her hands came up, finding Zosia’s face as she leaned in and kissed her. It wasn’t gentle. It was quick, a little desperate.

    Zosia froze for just a second, caught off guard.

    Then Carol kissed her again, deeper this time. To distract and replace whatever had almost been asked.

    Zosia’s hands hovered for a moment before settling at her waist, holding her there rather than pulling her any closer.

    Zosia pulled back just enough to look at her. “Hey…”

    Carol’s eyes flickered, something guarded sitting just beneath the surface.

    “Don’t,” Carol murmured, almost pleading.

    A pause. Then, quieter, almost as if the words were hard to get out, “Please… make love to me?”

    It wasn’t casual. Not playful. There was something in it Zosia didn’t understand.

    She searched her face for a second. Saw the shutdown. The avoidance. The need underneath it.

    Her hand slid up, brushing lightly along Carol’s cheek.

    “Hey,” she tried again softly. “Talk to me.”

    Carol shook her head, just once. “I don’t want to talk.”

    Her voice wavered, just slightly.

    “I just want you.”

    Before Zosia could respond, Carol leaned in again, brushing a kiss against her lips, not quite giving her the chance to speak.

    Zosia pulled back a fraction, meeting her eyes. They were soft with tears.

    “Whatever is wrong, I don't think sex is the solution,” Zosia whispered.

    “I just need to know you love me,” Carol whispered.

    Zosia stroked her cheek. “You know I love you.”

    She held Carol’s gaze for a second longer, taking in the way she looked. It was clear she was holding herself too tightly together.

    Then she moved.

    Her hand slid to the back of Carol’s neck as she pulled her into a tight embrace.

    “Come here,” she whispered, holding her close.

    Carol resisted for half a second before she gave in, her body softening as she pressed into her. A shaky breath left her, uneven, like she’d been holding it in for too long.

    “I’m here,” Zosia said quietly, one hand moving up and down her back in slow, steady strokes.

    Carol’s fingers curled into her shirt, holding on. “I don’t want you to leave me,” she said, her voice small against Zosia’s shoulder.

    “I’m not going anywhere.”

    Carol held onto her a little tighter, like something was caught in her throat. But she didn’t say it.

    Instead, she exhaled softly. “I’m having a really hard time,” she murmured.

    “I know.”

    “And I want you to know that I’m really trying to do what’s best,” Carol said, pulling back just enough to meet her eyes, searching her face for understanding.

    “What happened, Carol?”

    Carol paused, as if choosing her words carefully. “I just had a really rough hour.”

    She leaned in, kissing her, lingering a second longer than necessary. When she pulled back, she didn’t move far.

    “Let’s go out,” she said, a little more certain now. “Just us… in this beautiful city. Dinner somewhere nice.”

    Her thumb brushed lightly over Zosia’s hand.

    “Let’s make tonight ours.”

    Zosia studied her for a second. There was something off, but hard to place.

    She nodded anyway, her hand tightening slightly around Carol’s.

    “Okay. Let’s do that.”

     

    ——

     

    Soft, warm light settled over the city as they stepped outside. They decided to walk, taking it in.

    Their hands found each other easily, like they always did. At crossings, they’d turn in toward each other, exchanging small looks, brief smiles. A quick kiss when no one was watching.

    “This is perfect, isn’t it?” Zosia said softly.

    Carol looked at her. There was still something in her eyes, something heavy. Not gone, just quieter now.

    “It is,” she said after a moment. “I needed this. Something normal.”

    “I’m glad.”

    They found a small restaurant and sat down. Carol ordered a Diet Coke.

    “I’ll have the same,” Zosia said, relieved in a way she didn’t question.

    After that, it was easy.

    Carol relaxed into it slowly, one arm draped over the back of her chair, her foot nudging Zosia’s under the table until it stayed there. She reached across now and then, absentminded, tracing the back of Zosia’s hand with her thumb while they talked.

    Zosia watched her more than she realized. She noticed how the tension left her shoulders, how her smile came easier the longer they sat there.

    At some point, Carol just looked at her.

    “What?” Zosia asked, a small smile pulling at her mouth.

    Carol shook her head. “Nothing.” A pause. Softer. “I’m just… Happy.”

    Zosia huffed quietly, but she didn’t look away this time.

    The sky dimmed, the streetlights flickering on one by one, casting everything in that soft, golden glow. Around them, the city kept moving.

    At their table, things stayed still.

    And for a little while, it felt simple.

     

     

    Chapter 27: Carol's POV

     

    Present

    “I won’t stay and lose myself trying to love you.”

    She watched the woman of her dreams walk away.

    Within two hours she lost her job, her dignity, and now the love of her life.

    Carol sat there. Swallowed. Trying to catch up on everything. Wondering how the hell she got to this point.

     

    ——

     

    The Plane

    New York - London

    Carol blinked at the screen in front of her. A neat list of potential questions for The Graham Norton Show stared back at her. The questions themselves were harmless, predictable, even.

    It was the answers that got to her. The answers Helen had ever so carefully fabricated.

    Question 1:

    Why did you decide not to include Raban on the cover this time? You had people panicking.

    Answer:

    It would have spoiled the whole plot of this book, wouldn’t it?

    She scrolled. Most of them were about the book, answers she knew by heart now.

    Question 8:

    I’ve heard you’re writing a new book already. 

    Answer:

    You can safely answer this with ‘yes’.

    Question 9:

    You said something interesting. You said it’s set in the Wycaro universe… but, it’s more personal. How does that work?

    Answer:

    It’s a lot about family dynamics. We will see more background of one of the main characters. Which is partly about my own family dynamics.

    “What the hell?” she muttered. This was not what her book was about.

    Question 10 (in case of romance):

    Is the person in question aware that you’re writing a book about them?

    Answer:

    Won’t be necessary because of answer no. 9.

    She closed the laptop. She didn’t even want to know the rest. This was going to be one of those standard interviews where she never said anything useful or anything she hadn’t said before.

    She glanced beside her. Zosia was staring out of the airplane window, quiet but restless. She had heard her sigh a few times already.

    It seemed Zosia wasn’t built for long stretches of waiting. The impatience had started back at the airport. Pacing, checking her phone, checking her passport, then checking it again as if something might have changed.

    Carol found herself watching her, noticing the rhythm of it, the small tells she hadn’t known before. She liked this. Learning new things about her. Ordinary things.

    God, she thought as she watched her, she’s incredible.

    She opened her laptop again, hesitated for a brief moment, then started writing.

    Question 9:

    You said something interesting. You said it’s set in the Wycaro universe, but it’s more personal. How does that work?

    Answer:

    It’s a lot about family dynamics. We will see more background of one of the main characters. Which is partly about my own family dynamics.

    She added some possible answers for herself:

    - I am finally writing the story as it was meant to be written.

    - It’s about identity in a less abstract way. Less disguised.

    - It’s about my current relationship, and how that changed my view of my current life.

    Question 10 (in case of romance):

    Is the person in question aware that you’re writing a book about them?

    Answer:

    Won’t be necessary because of answer no. 9.

    She added:

    - They are.

    - Absolutely not.

    She felt Zosia watching her now. Carol kept her eyes on the screen, letting the silence stretch just long enough to steady herself. Then she exhaled, made her choice, and deleted her two potential answers. Because there was only one.

    - She is.

     

    ——

     

    London - The day after The Graham Norton Show

    Meeting with Helen.

    She knew she’d blown the TV show. Helen had made that perfectly clear the night before. And at that moment there had still been a sliver of doubt. But not anymore. Because this morning, Zosia had told her she loved her, and suddenly nothing else seemed to matter.

    She stepped into the meeting with a steadiness she hadn’t felt before.

    Helen was already seated at the desk in her hotel room, her publisher, Val, on screen.

    “Hi,” Carol said.

    “Carol,” Val began, her expression tight. “I have no words for this.”

    “Really?” Carol replied, dry. “Maybe Helen can help. She had plenty yesterday.”

    “This isn’t the time for jokes,” Helen reminded her.

    Val’s expression tightened, subtle but noticeable. “Helen and I have been talking…”

    Of course you have, Carol thought, rolling her eyes.

    “…and we think we should lean into this.”

    “Excuse me?”

    Beside her, Helen exhaled slowly. “We’ve been monitoring social media. You’re already trending, and the show hasn’t even aired yet.”

    “We can either ignore it,” Val continued, “or we can run with it.”

    “You mean exploit it,” Carol corrected her.

    Val didn’t hesitate. “We prefer to call it rebranding.”

    Carol’s jaw clenched. “So what does that mean exactly?”

    “It means,” Helen said, cutting in, “that your next book will be a lesbian romance. And we’ll be marketing it that way.”

    Carol looked at her, stunned.

    “It also means,” Val added, “we’ll be arranging interviews around that.”

    “So… around my private life?” Carol asked, caught off guard.

    “Yes,” Helen said. “We’ll guide certain questions. Shape the narrative around you.”

    “So let me get this straight… no pun intended, obviously.” She said dryly. “My life still isn’t my own.” 

    Carol looked between Helen and the screen.

    “I’m still not in control of my own work, and now we’re just adding my private life on top of everything?”

    “That’s something you set in motion yourself,” Helen replied evenly. “We’re just managing it.”

    Carol went quiet for a second, then pushed her chair back and stood.

    “This is just… fucking wonderful,” she said as she walked out of the door.

     

    ——

     

    London - Day after the show aired

    The Reading.

    The reading had been awful. Too many variables. Too many people wanting something she couldn’t give.

    She wasn’t some rock star, casually announcing to the world she was dating a supermodel. She let out a quiet huff at the thought. Zosia could pass for one, sure, but she wasn’t made for this. Not the spectacle. Not the performance.

    And then there was Helen, hovering, steering, nudging her toward answers she didn’t want to give. Every word on stage had felt controlled and monitored.

    At the end of the evening she was glad to step out of that cab, and couldn’t wait to get back to the hotel room with Zosia. But Helen stopped her before she could take more than a few steps.

    “We had an agreement,” she said, just low enough that Zosia wouldn’t catch it.

    “I never agreed to anything,” Carol shot back, just as quietly.

    “There’s nothing to agree to. You signed a contract years ago. This is part of it.” Helen’s voice sharpened, controlled but cutting.

    Carol went still, her gaze flicking briefly toward Zosia’s back, a few steps ahead. Close enough to hear if they slipped.

    “I need you to bring her into this,” Helen continued. “People seem to like her.”

    Carol’s jaw tightened.

    “She’s… good on camera.” Helen swallowed, adjusting. “If people ask, you don’t brush it off. You show her. You let them see.”

    Carol said nothing.

    “Spotlight, Carol.” Helen pressed.

    Spotlight.

    Something in Carol just… shut off.

    Not anger. Not even frustration. Just a quiet, familiar kind of numbness settling in, like a door closing somewhere deep inside of her. Helen’s voice, the questions, the expectations, all of it flattened into background noise she didn’t want to engage with anymore.

    Her gaze dropped, unfocused, as if she’d already checked out of the moment, and Zosia standing there had nothing to do with her anymore.

    She turned.

    No hesitation. No explanation.

    Just… away.

    Toward the bar.

     

    ——

     

    Paris

    The Magazine Interview.

    “Where is she?” Helen asked as Carol stepped into the café.

    “She’s not coming.”

    Carol didn’t stop, walking straight past her toward the table set up in the corner.

    “Zosia was supposed to be here.”

    Carol paused, just enough to look at her. “She’s not a prop, Helen.”

    Inside, they were shown to a small seating area where the interviewer was already seated. Carol pulled out a chair and sat across from him.

    She had read the interview notes. Every question, every answer. None of them felt like hers.

    “Can I get you something to drink?” someone asked.

    “Coffee,” Carol said.

    “No wine?” Helen added lightly.

    Carol didn’t look at her. If she did, she’d lose it.

    A brief nod. A pen clicked.

    “Alright,” the interviewer said. “Shall we begin?”

    Carol gave a short nod.

    At first, it was easy. General questions about the books, about the tour. Safe ones.

    But then she noticed the phrasing. The angle. The way certain words were chosen.

    Community. Representation. Visibility.

    Her eyes flicked down to the notes in front of him for just a second.

    The publication name sat at the top. A gay magazine.

    Carol leaned back slightly. That changed things.

    Not in a bad way. Not something she wanted to shut down or disrespect. If anything, it made it more important. Zosia would surely think so.

    But it also made one thing very clear. She couldn’t sit here and repeat something that wasn’t true. She couldn’t turn this into a version of herself Helen had written.

    “Is this your first relationship with a woman?”

    Carol glanced up at the man in front of her, then at Helen. “No,” she said simply.

    “So why did you decide to come out?”

    She knew the answer they wanted. She had memorized it.

    Instead, she said, “Because I was tired of lying.”

    A brief pause. The interviewer looked up, more attentive now. Helen did too. Up until now, Carol had insisted she hadn’t meant to come out. Now Helen knew that it had indeed been intentional. 

    “Your new girlfriend, if we may call her that, was on Graham Norton as well,” he continued. “How does she feel about all of this?”

    “You can call her that,” Carol kept her voice even. “She doesn’t care for it. The attention.”

    From the corner of her eye, she saw Helen shift.

    “Can you tell us a little about her? How did you two meet?”

    Carol exhaled. “I’d rather keep that…”

    “Excuse me,” Helen cut in smoothly. “Carol, it’s okay. You can talk about Zosia.”

    Carol’s jaw tightened. “I wasn’t going to…”

    “It’s an amazing story,” Helen continued, placing a hand on her shoulder. “A true romance.”

    Carol went still.

    “They met at one of her readings. Zosia was a fan of the books, and Carol fell for her the moment she saw her. Because, as you might’ve noticed, she looks a bit like a female version of her character, Raban.”

    Silence.

    Carol felt it drop in her chest.

    There it was. It didn’t matter what she said anymore, it was already out there. A lie, one that was too good to ignore.

    “Is that true, Carol?” the interviewer asked.

    “I…” She looked at Helen, then back at him. “I really don’t think this is…”

    “And was it love at first sight?”

    Carol hesitated. She could still speak up. Correct it before it went further. But she was too tired. Numb.

    “It was.” She finally said. At least that answer was true.

    Helen flinched beside her. That reaction alone was worth it.

    The interviewer smiled slightly, satisfied, and moved on.

    The rest blurred into something tighter, more careful. Carol chose her words one by one now, keeping them close, giving away as little as possible. Every answer measured. Every sentence controlled.

    She didn’t look at Helen again.

    When it was finally over, the interviewer closed his notebook.

    “Thank you, Carol. That was great.”

    Carol gave a small nod. “Thank you.”

    He stood, exchanged a few polite words with Helen, and then left them there at the table.

    Silence settled in almost immediately.

    Carol stayed where she was, hands wrapped loosely around her cup, though the coffee had long gone cold.

    All she could think was… Zosia is going to read this.

    Helen sat back down across from her.

    “Why did you do that?” Carol asked, her voice low.

    Helen didn’t hesitate. “Why did you go off-script?”

    Carol looked away, out through the bar’s tall windows, her jaw tight.

    Helen signalled a waiter. Carol sat stiffly, already wanting to leave.

    “Carol,” Helen said after a moment. “Please trust me when I say we only want what’s best for you.”

    Carol let out a quiet, disbelieving scoff.

    “Oh, you don’t believe me?” Helen’s tone turned almost amused. “Let me be blunt, then. We have no use for you if this fails.”

    Carol blinked, then let out a short laugh under her breath. “Wow. That’s… wow.”

    A glass of wine and a whiskey were put in front of them.

    Carol shook her head. “So what exactly is good about Zosia leaving me? Because if she reads this, and she will, it might push her away.”

    “If she really loves you,” Helen said evenly, “though I’m not sure whether she’s there yet,  then she’ll stay. Better to find that out now than after eight years of marriage, right?”

    Carol glanced down at the untouched drink in front of her. After a moment, she pushed it aside and stood.

    She didn’t say another word and made her way back to the hotel, to the elevator and toward her room.

    When she reached her floor, she couldn’t bring herself to open the door. This was the point where things could get ugly. She had to tell Zosia somehow. But how do you tell someone that strangers are about to take pieces of her life, twist them, misread them, and print them for everyone to see?

    Carol turned away, pacing the hallway. Back and forth. Again and again. Trying to burn off the tension.

    She glanced at her phone. She had been doing that for 20 minutes. 

    “This is insane,” she muttered.

    She took a breath, then finally slid the keycard past the reader.

    Zosia was on the phone when she entered. She hung up quickly. Too quickly.

    “Did the interview go okay?”

    “Yeah. Fine.”

    There was still one evening, one night, where she didn’t have to be afraid that Zosia would walk out on her.

    Zosia walked toward her. Giving her a questioning look.

    “You…” she started, softer now. “Have you been…”

    No, she hadn’t. She had left the drink downstairs, untouched.

    Her hands came up, finding Zosia’s face as she leaned in and kissed her. Her kiss turned into something more desperate. She needed to hold on as long as possible.

    “Please… make love to me?”

     

    ——

     

    PRESENT - EARLIER

    The evening before had been perfect. Carol couldn’t remember a time when she had actually said she was happy out loud.

    But last night, the city, Zosia by her side, dinner… She had actually been happy.

    Now, morning had arrived. The interview would be made public.

    Carol opened her laptop and opened the link.

    There it was. Everything.

    She skimmed, questions, answers, the usual polished narrative. And then she found it. The part she’d been dreading. Zosia’s name. Where she was from. How they’d met. Details she had never given, laid out like they belonged to the public now.

    And then a photo.

    Carol went still.

    Something in her snapped.

    “I’ll be right back,” she called out, her voice competing with the noise of the shower.

    She heard a faint, “Okay.” 

    She grabbed her laptop and stormed out, down the hall, straight into the elevator. By the time she reached Helen’s room, she didn’t knock, she slammed her fist against the door. As soon as it opened, she barged in.

    “Look what you did!” she snapped, pushing past her as she opened the laptop. “Look at it.”

    Helen barely flinched. “You knew this was coming.”

    “I didn’t want this,” Carol shot back. “And I definitely didn’t agree to a photo of her.”

    Her hands were shaking now, anger sharp and immediate.

    Helen sat down, scanning the screen. “I didn’t know they’d include that. They probably found it online.” Helen shrugged. “Besides, she was also on TV, right?”

    “This is a private photo! Where do they even get that?” Carol’s voice cut through the room, pointing at the article. “This… isn’t normal!”

    “Lower your voice.”

    “No.” Her voice rose even further. “No, I’m done, Helen. I’m done with you, with Val, with writing in general at this point.”

    Helen held her gaze, unimpressed.

    “You’re fired!” Carol finally said.

    A beat.

    “You can’t fire me,” Helen said, almost amused.

    Carol let out a short breath. “I just did.”

    She didn’t wait for a response. Didn’t give Helen the satisfaction of one. She turned and walked out.

    Not back to her room.

    Downstairs.

    To the café across the street.

    It wasn’t about drinking. Not at first. She just needed space. A place to think.

    Carol slid into a quiet corner table, opened her laptop, then her e-mail. Val’s name stared back at her. She started typing.

    The first draft was all anger, sharp, cutting, reckless. She deleted it.

    The second tried to be measured, professional. That didn’t last either.

    The third… she didn’t even finish.

    She closed her eyes, exhaling slowly, like she could force herself into clarity.

    A glass touched down in front of her with a soft clink.

    “From those women over there,” the bartender said, nodding toward a couple across the room.

    Carol glanced over briefly, then back at the drink.

    Whiskey.

    She shouldn’t.

    She knew that immediately.

    Still, she lifted the glass slightly in acknowledgment, a silent thank you. The women smiled. It would be rude not to. That would create more bad publicity.

    Just one sip.

    The burn was familiar as it went down.

    She set it back down and returned to her phone.

    The words came easier now. Not better. Just… easier.

    Her fingers moved faster. The tension in her shoulders eased a little.

    Without thinking, she reached for the glass again.

    Empty.

    She exhaled through her nose, almost annoyed. The bartender noticed, already moving, placing another in front of her before she could say anything.

    Carol stared at it.

    She shouldn’t. Not now. Not after everything. She’d promised herself, she'd promised Zosia.

    “Thank you,” she said anyway.

    And drank.

    After that, the line blurred.

    The email still had to be written. She stopped overthinking it. The words came quicker. The anger dulled. Then she hit send.

    She shouldn’t have had the next drink.

    Or the one after that.

    But she did.

    Time slipped.

    It must have been over an hour before Zosia walked in.

    Zosia didn’t react right away.

    She just watched her.

    Carol could feel it, the way her gaze moved over the table, the glass in her hand, the way she was sitting. Taking it all in. Putting it together without being told.

    “I fucked up,” Carol repeated, quieter this time.

    Zosia leaned forward slightly, not judging, not yet. “What happened?”

    Carol let out a breath that almost sounded like a laugh, but there was nothing amused about it. Her eyes dropped to the table.

    Everything pressed at the edges of her mind. The article, Zosia’s name written out, the photo, Helen’s voice, the way she’d burned everything down in the span of an hour.

    She could say it. She could tell her.

    They put you in it. Your name. Your face. I let it happen. I made it worse. I fired everyone and now I don’t know what that means. I’m a failure. I failed you. I hurt you.

    The words sat there, heavy, waiting.

    If she said them out loud, it would all become real and unavoidable.

    And Zosia… she might walk away from it. From her.

    Carol swallowed.

    “Just… work stuff,” she said instead, the words coming out dull, insufficient.

    Zosia didn’t move. “Work stuff?”

    “Yeah.” Carol nodded, too quickly. “The interview. It’s… it’s not great.”

    Not great.

    It felt ridiculous the second it left her mouth.

    Zosia’s eyes searched her face, like she was trying to find the part that was missing from that explanation. Because it wasn’t enough. It didn’t match what she was seeing.

    Carol could feel it unraveling.

    So she reached for the glass instead. Took another sip. Anything to fill the space.

    Zosia’s hand caught her wrist.

    “Don’t,” she said quietly.

    Carol stilled.

    “You don’t get to hide behind that,” Zosia added, her voice steady, firmer now. “Not with me.”

    “I’m not hiding.” She gestured at herself being right there.

    Zosia looked at her. “You are. You’re already doing this without me.”

    She held her gaze, even as her voice dropped, more vulnerable now, but no less firm.

    “Because if you really want to do this alone,” she said, pausing just long enough for it to land, “then you’re doing all of it alone.” She swallowed, paused. “And I won’t be here in the morning.”

    Silence.

    Carol blinked slowly. “So that’s how it ends.”

    “That’s entirely up to you,” she said, holding her gaze.

    Zosia drew in a steady breath and reached for her hand.

    “The first time I saw you… I just… knew. I didn’t understand it, but I felt it. It was like my whole world narrowed down to one point, to you. And it scared me.” Her voice softened. “Because I didn’t think love worked like that. I didn’t think it could be that simple. But with you… it was.”

    She shook her head faintly, almost to herself.

    “It wasn’t supposed to happen like this. Not this fast, not this strong.” A small, breathless exhale. “But it did. I’m completely, undeniably in love with you. And I want this. I want us. A future.”

    Another pause. This one steadier.

    “But there’s something I love more,” she added quietly.

    She shook her head faintly, like she was finally sure of it.

    “Myself.”

    She met Carol’s eyes again. Her hand gestured toward the drinks between them.

    Zosia watched her.

    Chapter 29: Poland - the Cabin

    The first thing Zosia noticed was how quiet it was. Calm. She could hear the wind in the trees, and water somewhere in the distance. The porch creaked softly under her weight, familiar in a way she didn’t expect after all those years.

    The last time she had visited Poland was twenty years ago. And this particular cabin probably thirty years ago.

    Her gaze shifted to Carol who stood a few steps away, her suitcase beside her, watching the lake.

    “Are you okay?” Zosia asked. Her voice came out softer than she intended. That had been happening a lot lately, out of fear of what the answer might be.

    Carol let out a breath that almost turned into a laugh. “Yeah. I just…” She shook her head slightly. “I didn’t realize how much I needed this.”

    Zosia leaned against the doorframe, arms loosely crossed. She hadn’t seen her like this in a while.

    The last two days had felt different.

    Not bad or wrong, just fragile. There had been no shouting, no crying, no arguing. But no hiding either.

    They had talked. A lot.

    They kept circling back to the things that didn’t quite settle, choosing to ask again instead of letting them slide just to keep the peace.

    The kind of conversations you can’t just walk away from. The kind of conversations that had to happen, all out in the open. It took them a full day to talk everything through. All cards on the table.

    Why Carol did what she did. How she saw her professional future. How she had gotten to this point in the first place.

    They talked about how it made Zosia feel. Seeing Carol like that, dealing with Helen, dealing with the sudden interest people had shown in her. And mostly how everything had damaged her trust.

    She hadn’t softened it this time, hadn’t buried it under something easier or less confronting.

    And Carol hadn’t flinched. She hadn’t tried to explain it away or reshape it into something smaller.

    She had just listened.

    And when Carol spoke, it had been different too, without the big promises or dramatic declarations that tried to fix everything in a single moment.

    Somewhere in those two days, something had shifted. It wasn’t fixed or fully healed. But there was a foundation of understanding and trust that wasn’t there before. And it felt good.

    “This is where I used to come every summer,” Zosia said, breaking the silence. “When I was a kid.”

    Carol turned to her slowly. “You’re kidding.”

    Zosia shook her head. “My grandparents owned it. After they passed, it just… stayed in the family. No one really uses it anymore.”

    “It looks amazing.”

    Carol took her time crossing the space between them.

    “Thank you for this.” She said as she stepped into Zosia’s space, one hand already sliding up to her jaw, and then she kissed her, like she’d been holding it in since the moment the cabin came into view.

    Zosia smiled, a little crooked, a little breathless. “Shall I show you around?”

    Carol let out a quiet laugh, brushing her thumb once along Zosia’s cheek before stepping back, though her hand lingered, sliding down to lace their fingers together instead of fully letting go.

    “Lead the way.”

    The porch creaked softly beneath them, the wood still warm from the sun. Zosia pushed the door open without ceremony, like she’d done it a thousand times before.

    Inside, it smelled faintly of pine wood that had been closed up for a while, holding onto the last bit of summer.

    “There’s even a fireplace,” Carol said, a little impressed. Then she glanced at Zosia. “How close are the neighbors?”

    Zosia knew what she meant. “There aren’t any. Not really. It’s very private here.”

    Carol’s mouth curled into a knowing grin. “Good.”

    Zosia shook her head, amused.

     

    ——

    A moment later, there was a lot of noise from the other room. Things shifting, something dropping. Then Carol came back holding a pack of cards, grinning.

    “This is going to be amazing.”

    Zosia glanced up. “What is it?”

    Carol dropped the cards onto the table. “Getting to know you.” She wiggled her eyebrows. “Couples edition.”

    Chapter 32: Poland - Old Friends

    For a second, she just lay there, staring at the space like she’d imagined the whole thing. Then she pushed herself up, grabbed a robe, and followed the faint sounds coming from the kitchen.

    Carol looked up when she walked in. “Good morning.” She kept moving like she’d been up for hours.

    Zosia stopped in the doorway. “Okay,” she began. “So you moved in, what… ten hours ago? And I’m already waking up to an empty bed?”

    She walked over, slipping her arms around Carol from behind and pressing a kiss to her neck.

    “I made you breakfast,” Carol said, nodding toward the plate on the counter. “And coffee.”

    Zosia leaned in to peek, clearly impressed. “Wow. I could get used to this.” She kissed Carol’s cheek before reaching for the mug. “My very own trad-wife.”

    “Careful,” Carol warned, smiling.

    Zosia took a sip, then studied her over the rim of her mug. “Will you be okay today?”

    Carol exhaled softly. “Yeah. I just have to deal with whatever damage I caused back in Paris.”

    Zosia’s jaw tightened a fraction. It was still a sensitive subject.

    “You can do the meeting over video, right?”

    “I can,” Carol said. “They won’t be thrilled, but I can.”

    Zosia nodded. “Good.”

    They ate breakfast together, talking easily and letting the morning settle without rushing it. Zosia walked her through the apartment in bits and pieces, where everything was, how the front door needed a slight shove to lock, which side of the TV to tap when it started flickering, how long it took for the hot water to kick in.

    Carol smiled. “I think I’ll manage.”

     

    ——

     

    She pushed the door open and stepped inside, the familiar bell sounding overhead.

    Her gaze dropped to the counter almost immediately.

    A note.

    Zosia paused, then walked over, picking it up with a small huff of amusement.

    “How vintage,” she muttered. 

    The first part was from Joanna.

     

    Zosia,

    Everything is in order. I’ve kept things consistent while you were away.

    There are a few things that might need your attention. I’ve marked them clearly.

    Glad you’re back.

    — Joanna

     

    Zosia smiled faintly, flipping the page.

     

    You’re welcome. I didn’t burn the place down.

    — K

     

    She let out a quiet laugh, shaking her head as she set the note back down. “It’s good to be back,” she murmured to herself.

    Her phone buzzed in her pocket.

    Koumba: Drinks tonight? I want to hear everything.

    Zosia exhaled softly. Oh…

    Zosia: Yes, but I’ll have to check with Carol.

    Koumba: Zosia…?

    Zosia: Yeah. She’s at my place.

    Koumba: ???

    Zosia: She kind of moved in.

    Koumba: You’re a walking cliché.

    Zosia: I know.

    Koumba: Fine. Let me know if the wife wants to go out tonight.

    Zosia shook her head, a smile lingering as she slipped her phone back into her pocket. 

    It was kind of insane. She was fully aware of that. But right now, she didn’t really care what anyone else thought.

    She settled behind the counter, letting the quiet of the store ease around her.

    The morning moved slowly, which Zosia didn’t mind. The jet lag still clung to her. Customers came and went in small waves. Browsing, asking questions, occasionally buying something. She passed the time fixing a few sections Koumba had “reorganized.” As usual, that meant dismantling any existing system.

    When the bell chimed again, Zosia looked up automatically. “Hi, let me know if you…”

    She stopped mid-sentence.

    Three girls stood inside the door, not looking at the records but at her, and not even attempting subtlety.

    “…need any help,” she finished.

    One of them smiled too quickly while another nudged her, barely containing her excitement. “Hi,” she said, before adding, “Sorry, we’re just looking.”

    “Mm,” Zosia replied, unconvinced, watching them drift further inside. As they turned, a Wycaro pin on one of their backpacks caught her eye. A flicker of private amusement crossing her face before she smoothed it away again.

    From behind the counter, Zosia caught the whispers as they carried just enough.

    “That’s her.”

    “I told you.”

    “Oh my god, she’s so pretty.”

    Zosia closed her eyes for a second, then leaned lightly against the counter. “If you’re looking for something specific, I can help.”

    “Actually…” One stepped closer. “Are you Zosia?”

    Zosia met her gaze without hesitation. “Yes.”

    That was enough. One girl pressing her lips together to contain a smile, another glancing down like she needed a second.

    “Oh my god.”

    Zosia raised an eyebrow. “Can I help you with something?”

    “You’re Carol Sturka’s girlfriend, right?”

    Zosia didn’t answer immediately, instead leaning back against the counter and folding her arms loosely. “I’m not going to discuss my personal life with customers.”

    It wasn’t sharp, but it was final.

    “Yeah no, of course, sorry,” the girl said quickly.

    Zosia almost smiled at that.

    One of them picked up a record, actually looking at it this time. “We’ve been here before… a couple of times.”

    “To see if you were here,” another added.

    Zosia studied them for a moment, something in her expression softening just slightly. “Well, I am, so you can stop coming back for that reason.”

    That earned a small laugh.

    “We’re big fans… of her work,” the girl with the pin added, brushing it unconsciously.

    Zosia simply nodded once. “I figured.”

    They lingered for another moment, as if reluctant to let the interaction end, but this time they didn’t push.

    “Sorry again,” one of them said. “We didn’t mean to be weird.”

    Zosia tilted her head slightly. “And yet…”

    The girls exchanged a look, still smiling. Then drifted back toward the shelves. This time they actually flipped through the records, slower, like they meant it.

    “Let me know if you need anything.”

    Zosia watched them briefly before shaking her head faintly and reaching for another stack of records. It wasn’t invasive, not exactly, but it was intense, a kind of attention that didn’t belong in a place that had always felt entirely her own.

    When the bell chimed again, Zosia looked up, and stilled.

    Carol stepped inside, a little pleased with herself, a paper bag in one hand and two coffees in the other. Her eyes found Zosia immediately.

    “Hi,” she said, already smiling, “I figured you might…”

    Her voice trailed off as Zosia gave a subtle warning, tilting her head toward the girls behind her.

    “Oh my god,” one of them whispered.

    Carol followed Zosia’s line of sight, and while it took her a second to understand exactly what she was looking at, the shift was immediate. Like a switch being flipped. She slipped into full professional composure, this time edged with something protective.

    Zosia exhaled softly. “You picked a good time.” She let her eyes close for the briefest moment, a flicker of amusement betraying her.

    Carol adjusted her grip on the bag. “I’m starting to realize that.” She stepped further inside, no longer treating the place as anonymous.

    She set the coffees down on the counter, keeping her focus on Zosia.

    “I brought lunch.”

    “Thank you.”

    Carol smiled at her, a little tighter than usual. Then she glanced briefly over her shoulder, just enough to acknowledge the others without inviting anything.

    “I can’t believe this is real,” one of the girls whispered a little too loud.

    “We love your work,” one of them said, now clearly addressing Carol.

    Carol glanced briefly at Zosia before looking back at them, her expression settling into something practiced. “Thank you.”

    One of the girls stepped forward. “I’m so sorry, we’re not trying to be weird, it’s just…” She hesitated, then pushed through. “We’ve been coming here for like three weeks. Almost every day. In the hope you’d be here.”

    A stillness settled.

    “That’s a lot of commitment,” Carol said carefully.

    A nervous laugh followed. “Would you maybe sign something?” she asked, pulling a book from her bag.

    Carol nodded once. “Yeah, that’s okay.”

    Relief showed immediately.

    “Who should I make it out to?”

    “Oh… uh… Lena.”

    Zosia smiled at that, the memory surfacing before she could stop it. She’d asked Carol the same question once, after weeks of rehearsing it in her head, only to falter the second it actually mattered.

    She felt kind of sorry for the girl.

    Carol wrote something brief and neat before handing it back, and Lena took it like it was way more than just a signature.

    “Thank you,” she said quietly.

    “You’re welcome.”

    “We should probably go,” one of them said, trying to reset the moment.

    Another small, slightly awkward laugh followed.

    “Sorry again,” the third added.

    “It’s fine,” Carol said, though her tone had settled into something more reserved.

    That seemed to be enough. They turned toward the door, slower than necessary, and finally stepped outside as the bell chimed behind them.

    The store fell quiet again.

    “Well,” Carol said, a little flat, “I’m not thrilled about that.”

    Zosia took a sip of her coffee. “They got what they wanted. They probably won’t come back.”

    After a brief pause, Zosia pushed off the counter and stepped closer. “Hi,” she said more softly now, her hand settling at Carol’s waist.

    Carol smiled. “Hi.”

    Zosia leaned in to kiss her, then eased back and nodded toward the bag. “Thank you for lunch.”

    Carol huffed a quiet breath. “You’re welcome.”

    Zosia lingered there for a moment, her thumb brushing absentmindedly against Carol’s side before she added, a little more casually, “If your meeting goes well… do you want to get drinks tonight? Koumba suggested it.”

    Carol’s mouth curved slightly, something lighter returning to her expression. “Sure. Let’s go either way. Celebrate if it goes well, or drown my sorrows if it doesn’t.”

     

    ——

     

    Zosia opened the door to her apartment, the smell of dinner hitting her immediately.

    That was a good sign.

    “Hi,” she called out as footsteps approached.

    Carol appeared, wearing an apron, a faint smear of some food on her arm.

    Zosia smiled. “You’re really committing to this whole trad-wife thing, huh?”

    Carol just smiled and leaned in for a soft welcome-home kiss.

    “So,” Zosia said, slipping off her shoes, “how did the meeting go?”

    “Fine,” Carol replied, already turning back toward the kitchen.

    “Just fine?” Zosia followed her, frowning slightly. It didn’t sound like something that would be just fine.

    “Yeah,” Carol said. “Nothing too interesting. Next meeting’s in a week.”

    Zosia slowed, not convinced. “So what happened? Did you talk to Helen? Your publisher?”

    “Yes.”

    “What does ‘yes’ mean?” she stepped closer, her tone sharpening. “I don’t like how vague you’re being.”

    Carol kept moving, focused on the counter, cleaning. “I spoke to both.”

    “Sweetheart,” Zosia reached for her, gently turning her toward her. “What happened?”

    Carol sighed, her shoulders tensing as she pulled away again. “I don’t want to get into it right now.”

    Zosia watched her for a moment. “No. We’re not doing that,” she said. “You promised.” 

    There was a hint of hurt in her voice, more than she meant to show.

    That made Carol stop. She turned, finally meeting her eyes. “There’s a lot going on, and I want tonight to feel… special. Let's go out as planned.”

    “No,” Zosia said quietly. “I’ll cancel.”

    Carol reached for her wrist. “No, please don’t.”

    A beat.

    “Please.”

    Zosia studied her face for a moment. “Promise me you’re okay.”

    “I’m okay.”

    Zosia held her gaze for a moment longer. She didn’t believe her. Not even a little.

    By dinner, it was obvious.

    Carol kept talking. About nothing important. Just whatever came to mind. The food, the wine, random stories that didn’t really go anywhere.

    Zosia barely responded. She didn’t need to.

    Every time things started to quiet down, Carol jumped in again, a little too fast. 

    Filling the space. Avoiding it.

    Zosia let her.

    ——

     

    “Well, well, well,” Koumba said, spreading his arms wide as they approached. “If it isn’t the newlyweds.”

    Zosia leaned in slightly toward Carol. “You sure you don’t want to run?”

    Carol smiled. “Tempting.”

    Up close, Zosia caught it again, that flicker behind her eyes. Gone almost as soon as it appeared.

    “Sooo,” Koumba went on, dragging out the word, “how was the trip? Saw your TV moment, by the way. Incredible. The memes?” He shook his head. “Top tier.”

    “I stayed off social media,” Zosia said.

    “You missed out. I’ll send you the good ones.”

    “Please don’t.”

    Carol glanced toward the bar. “Should I…”

    “No need,” Koumba cut in as a waiter appeared beside them with a wine cooler. “I took the liberty.” He gestured proudly at the bottle of champagne being set down. “This is a celebration, after all.”

    Zosia gave him a look. “Of what, exactly?”

    Koumba raised an eyebrow. “You two moving in together?” He let out a short laugh. “I mean, I’m celebrating. You’re welcome to join me.”

    Carol let out a soft laugh, something more genuine slipping through. “You’re right,” she said, reaching for her glass. “We should.”

    She turned to Zosia then, her expression settling into something quieter, more focused. The noise around them seemed to fall away.

    “To you,” she said.

    Zosia stilled.

    “To the way you walked into my life and somehow became everything in it.” A small pause, like she was choosing not to say more. “To waking up next to you and realizing I don’t ever want a version of my life where you’re not there.”

    She held her gaze.

    “I love you, Zosia.”

    Koumba blinked, looking between them. “Is this… a regular occurrence?”

    “Yeah,” Zosia said, lifting her glass. “Romance novelist.”

    “Damn.”

    Zosia clinked her glass lightly against Carol’s. “I love you too.”

    She leaned in, kissing her just long enough to make it feel like it meant something, then wrapped her arms around her. “You’re everything,” she murmured into her ear.

    Carol’s fingers tightened slightly around her glass.

    There it was again. Subtle. Almost invisible. But not to Zosia.

    She didn’t push. Not here. Not now.

    The conversation drifted after that, easy on the surface. The trip, the cities, the small moments that were safe to revisit. Koumba filled in the gaps with stories from the store, each one more ridiculous than the last.

    Carol smiled along, nodding at the right moments, even laughing once or twice, but Zosia could feel it. The effort behind it. The way she kept the conversation moving whenever it got even a second too quiet.

    Like silence was the one thing she couldn’t afford.

    And for now, Zosia let her.

     

    ——

     

    The cab drive back felt charged.

    They barely spoke. Just the occasional comment about something outside, or something Koumba had said earlier. Small, safe topics. Every conversation came from Carol, like she was trying to keep something at bay.

    Trying not to leave space for what actually needed to be said.

    Eventually, even that stopped.

    Silence settled between them. Zosia kept her eyes out the window, but she could feel Carol looking at her every now and then, only to look away again when she didn’t respond.

    “Thank you for tonight,” Carol said eventually. “For… giving me space.”

    Zosia nodded. “Sure.”

    “We’ll talk at home,” Carol added.

    There was something in the way she said it. Softer.

    Zosia caught it.

    “Home,” she repeated quietly.

    Carol let out a small laugh. “I know.”

    By the time they stepped inside, the tension hadn’t lifted.

    Zosia kicked off her shoes and dropped onto the couch, waiting.

    Carol lingered. Checked her phone. Grabbed a drink. Checked it again.

    “Quit stalling,” Zosia said, sharper than she meant to.

    Carol looked up, then set her phone down on the counter a little too hard.

    “Fine,” she said, sitting down. “I fucked up.”

    Zosia nodded. That much was clear.

    “I spoke to my publisher,” Carol continued. “I’ve still got fifteen months left on the contract.”

    “Okay…” Zosia said slowly, though she had no idea what that actually meant.

    Carol inhaled. “If I want out, it’s going to cost me.”

    “How much?”

    “A lot.”

    “Carol.”

    She glanced down, jaw tightening. “Close to half a million.”

    Zosia blinked. “I’m sorry… what?” Her voice cracked slightly.

    “I know,” Carol said. “And I mean, I could pay it. That’s not the problem.”

    Zosia just stared at her. “You… could pay it?” A pause. “You have that kind of money?”

    “I do.”

    Silence settled between them.

    Zosia tried to recalibrate. She knew Carol had money, that had been obvious these past weeks. But not like this. Not half a million without flinching kind of money.

    “The thing is,” Carol went on, “I don’t want to pay it. It’s not fair.”

    “No,” Zosia said, still catching up. “It’s not.”

    Carol exhaled. “I’ll get a lawyer. I have emails, proof of their misconduct.” She ran a hand through her hair. “I think I have a strong case.”

    Zosia nodded automatically, even as her brain lagged behind.

    “Okay.”

    Carol studied her. “Do you?”

    “I’m sorry, what?” Zosia said, still catching up.

    “Do you think I have a strong case?”

    Zosia hesitated, choosing her words carefully. “I mean… this isn’t exactly my area,” she admitted. “I don’t know your contract, or how any of this works.”

    She saw it then, the flicker in Carol’s expression. Not doubt. Disappointment. She wasn’t asking for expertise. She was asking for backup.

    Zosia softened. “But… what’s the worst that can happen?”

    “I pay.”

    “Right.” Zosia nodded. “Then it’s still worth trying, isn’t it?”

    She gave a small nod.

    Zosia hesitated again. “Just so I’m clear… if it does come to that, paying it I mean, would it ruin you? Financially.”

    Carol let out a short, humorless laugh. “God, no.”

    Some of the tension drained from Zosia’s shoulders. “Okay. Then yeah,” she said, more firmly now. “It’s definitely worth it.”

    Carol held her gaze for a moment, something steadier settling in her expression this time.

    “Yeah,” she said. “I think it is.”

    Chapter 35: Carol's POV

     

     -- CAROL’S POV --

     

    JFK Airport

    Ladies and gentlemen, we’d like to begin boarding for Flight 482 to Albuquerque.

    Carol didn’t move.

    Around her, people stood up. Boarding passes in hand, bags slung over shoulders. Conversations picked back up as they moved, and a line started to form.

    She looked at the ticket in her hand. Albuquerque. The word stared back at her. It should have felt like home. But it didn’t. Not anymore.

    She frowned. A few weeks didn’t get to rewrite everything. One person couldn’t be this important. And yet…

    Her grip tightened slightly, making the paper crinkle.

    The thought of landing in Albuquerque, of waking up there without her, of going back to that version of her life… No. Even thinking about it made her feel sick.

    Final call for passengers traveling to Albuquerque.

    Carol looked up at the gate, at the last few people handing over their boarding passes. She was the only one left now. A ground steward tried to catch her eye, but she looked away. Meeting their eyes meant deciding. She wasn’t ready for that yet.

    Her mind kept insisting on reason. It’s your home. You’ll see her again.

    But it didn’t matter.

    Carol let out a breath, shaking her head slightly, almost at herself. It didn’t make sense.

    But neither was getting on that plane when everything in her was telling her not to.

    She looked down at the ticket one more time. Then she tore it, quick and decisive, before she could stop herself.

    She stood without hesitation, then turned away from the gate and started walking.

     

    ——

     

    Day 1

    The apartment door closed behind her. She was still slightly annoyed about the three girls who had shown up at the store. Tracking her down was one thing, but dragging Zosia into it… that crossed a line she wasn’t okay with.

    She mentally added it to the list of complications she’d brought into Zosia’s life.

    Speaking of… she checked the time. Twenty minutes until her meeting with Val. After that, Helen.

    They’d both been trying to reach her all week. Which is why, back in Poland, she’d blocked their numbers for a while. She needed a little space, distance from everything. 

    Everything except for Zosia. 

    Val had already sent her the available options for her contract days ago, but she hadn't read them until this morning. It had been the right decision, because none of the options were promising.

    She sat down, opened her laptop, and briefly glanced around the apartment. It didn’t feel like hers. She had only been here once before. 

    The reality of her decision sank in, a quiet anxiety creeping in alongside it.

    What the fuck… She exhaled and ran a hand over her face.

    The Teams chime cut through her thoughts, making her flinch.

    Val appeared on screen a second later. She said nothing, but her expression spoke volumes.

    “Hi,” Carol made it sound almost like a peace offering. It didn’t change anything.

    The call went exactly as expected. There was a contract. A very clear one. And according to Val, the publisher had done nothing wrong. Carol still owed them one book. If she delivered it, she could walk away. If not, she’d have to buy herself out.

    For Carol, the problem was the book itself. It wasn’t far from what she was already writing. But the fact that they wanted to have control over it… that was simply not an option. It was too personal to give any of it away.

    So far, no surprises.

    Then came the buyout fee. She kept her face still when she heard the number, but under the table, her hand was gripping her knee hard enough to sting. 

    She should have paid more attention to the contract when it was signed. The truth was, she hadn’t paid attention at all. She had a manager she trusted blindly, and at the time, that had been enough.

    Now there was this. Almost half a million.

    It was another thing she could mentally add to her ongoing list of problems she had brought into Zosia’s life.

    When the call ended, she just sat there. Trying to grasp the information she had just been told. She could either stay with her publisher for another 15 months and be miserable, or she could pay the money and be done with it. Neither felt right.

    But there was always a third option. Fight it legally.

    Her next meeting popped up on her screen.

    Helen.

    In all those years that they had known each other, there had never been an official meeting. 

    While they were together, these ‘meetings’ took place in the kitchen while they both cooked. Or on the couch, during a commercial break. After their divorce, they just called or texted, whenever something needed to be said.

    This time, however, Helen had been an actual name in her calendar.

    “Carol.”

    Helen’s voice filled the room.

    “Hi,” she replied, without much emotion behind it.

    “I don’t even know where to begin.” Helen reached for something off-screen. A cup of tea, she guessed. She was right.

    "You’re in New York.”

    “I am.”

    Helen nodded. “You didn’t waste any time.”

    Carol sighed, pinched her nose, and tried to hold back any form of annoyance that might be readable on her face. “I’m not in this meeting to talk about my personal life choices.”

    Helen visibly flinched at the word ‘meeting’. She softened her voice. “Isn’t it kind of the same thing when it comes to us?”

    She was clearly trying to reach her emotionally this time. And it worked. Because no matter how angry Carol was, and how much she hadn’t changed her mind, Helen was still her ex-wife.

    “I know,” Carol said. “It’s the same thing. Or it was.” She corrected herself.

    “It doesn’t have to be over, you know,” Helen tried. “We were good together. At least professionally.”

    Carol swallowed, feeling the familiar anger rise again, but she kept it down. She took a deep breath, pressed her lips together for a moment, then spoke.

    “Privately,” she started, “as well as professionally… we are done.”

    Helen exhaled, the sound clearly audible through the microphone.

    “You’re making a mistake, baby.” The old nickname slipped out, like nothing had changed. A flicker of regret crossed Helen’s face. It clearly hadn't been intentional.

    “Don’t,” Carol said immediately. “You don’t get to call me that anymore.”

    Helen stilled for a second. “Right,” she said quietly. “Sorry.”

    The word hung there, unfamiliar coming from her.

    “This cannot be fixed anymore, Helen,” Carol finally said. “And frankly… I don’t want it to. I’m tired.”

    “So this is it?”

    “This is it,” Carol said, leaving no room for doubt.

    For a moment, it seemed like they both wanted to say something, but neither spoke. An awkward silence settled in. 

    Then Helen exhaled softly. “I hope all of this works out for you, Carol. I mean that,” she said. “Goodbye.”

    Carol swallowed.

    “Goodbye.”

    The screen went dark, and Carol stared at her own reflection for a moment. This was it. Sixteen years of dating, marriage, working together. Over.

    She dragged a hand over her face, trying to hold back the tears. “Fuck.”

     

    ——

     

    Day 3

    Things were good. Mostly.

    Zosia left for work around nine each morning, slipping out of bed early with a quick kiss and a promise to be back before dinner. Carol stayed behind in the quiet apartment, filling the hours with calls and emails, talking to lawyers who all seemed to have a different opinion on the same problem.

    A handful sounded optimistic, but kindly declined. Others didn’t even bother pretending.

    In the end, it all came down to the same thing.

    She had broken her contract.

    Carol tried to stay positive, especially around Zosia. She kept her tone light, her answers short, her worries tucked neatly away. But she knew Zosia saw right through it.

    The exhaustion. The tension. It wasn’t something she could hide.

    And on top of all that, there was something else she tried not to think about too much.

    She needed to go back to Albuquerque. At some point, she would have to. But every time the thought crossed her mind, she pushed it away.

    She wasn’t leaving. Not now. Not yet.

    Then her phone buzzed against the table.

    Koumba: You home? I’m bored.

    A photo followed, showing an expensive bottle of whiskey, the label turned toward the camera.

    Carol stared at it. “You’re joking,” she muttered. 

    She’d met him three times. Great guy, truly, but it was Zosia’s friend. Not hers. They had nothing in common.

    Another text followed.

    Koumba: I know what you’re thinking… this is Zosia’s best friend. And you’re right. But I feel like I should get to know her partner a little better.

    Oh, he’s good. Carol huffed a quiet laugh.

    Carol: Sure. I’m home. And I share your boredom.

    Koumba: I’ll be there in a bit.

    She exhaled, leaning back against the counter, and closed her eyes for a second, already wondering if this was a bad idea.

     

    ——

     

    “Good afternoon, 'reason I’m suddenly second place’!”

    Carol was already regretting this.

    She stepped aside, letting him in.

    Koumba didn’t hesitate. He walked straight past her, claimed the counter with the whiskey, grabbed two glasses, and poured without asking.

    “It’s three in the afternoon,” Carol reminded him.

    “Not in Poland.”

    She paused, then nodded. “Solid argument.”

    “Thank you.”

    Carol took the glass and tipped it slightly. “Cheers.”

    “Cheers.”

    She watched him over the rim of her glass as they both took a sip. “So… what brings you here?”

    Koumba met her gaze, completely unfazed. “Like I said. I’m bored.”

    “So you just decided to spend your afternoon drinking with your friend’s partner?”

    “You say that like it’s a bad thing.” He looked genuinely offended.

    “Koumba,” she said, more evenly now. “Why are you really here?”

    He sighed, rolling the glass lightly in his hand. “Yeah, Zosia told me this wasn’t going to work,” he admitted.

    “She knows you’re here?”

    “Yes, and she tried to stop me.”

    That, at least, brought a smile to Carol’s face.

    “But,” he went on, gesturing at her with his glass, “I care about her. So that extends to you.”

    Carol huffed a quiet laugh.

    “And since you’re clearly not telling your wife what’s going on,” he added, “I figured you might tell me. And if not…” He lifted the bottle slightly. “Then at least I get to enjoy this in good company.”

    “That’s painfully honest,” Carol said. “Also… she’s not my wife. And I do talk to her.”

    Koumba shook his head. “She feels like you’re protecting her.”

    Carol scoffed. “Funny. Now who’s the one not talking.”

    “Carol,” he said, softer this time. “She’s worried.”

    “It’ll be fine.”

    “That’s what you keep telling her,” he pressed. “But she knows you’re struggling.”

    “So she sends her friend to fix it?”

    He lifted a finger. “Best friend, thank you very much. And again, she didn’t send me.”

    A beat.

    Carol pushed off the counter, grabbing her phone. Two messages were already waiting.

    Zosia: I’m so sorry.

    Zosia: I tried to stop him.

    Carol smiled, thumbs moving quickly.

    Chapter 40: For the Record

    “I just got the call,” Eleanor said, not bothering with anything else.

    Carol went still. “Okay…”

    Eleanor looked at her steadily. “You’re out of the contract.”

    Carol blinked, the words not quite landing all at once. “I’m what?”

    Zosia looked between them, her confusion obvious now as she tried to follow.

    “They’re dropping it,” Eleanor said. “The lawsuit. All of it.”

    For a moment, no one spoke.

    “I think the media pressure did it,” she added, almost as an afterthought.

    Carol stared at her like she was waiting for the catch. “Are you serious?”

    “Yes.”

    “Completely? No conditions, no clauses, nothing buried somewhere?”

    “No conditions,” Eleanor said. “You’re out.”

    Carol let out a breath that sounded like it had been stuck in her chest for weeks, her hand coming up to cover her mouth as she shook her head once, then again, trying to catch up to what she was hearing. “Oh my god.”

    Zosia was already smiling, the tension in her shoulders dropping all at once as she stepped closer.

    Carol looked at her, eyes wide and almost disbelieving. “I’m out.”

    “I know,” Zosia said, her voice softer now.

    Carol closed the distance without thinking, pulling her into a kiss that was quick and a little unsteady, like she couldn’t quite hold still, and then into a tight hug that followed immediately after. “I’m out,” she said again, pressing the words into Zosia’s shoulder.

    Zosia held on just as firmly. “You’re out.”

    Carol let out a short, breathless laugh against her. “This is… I don’t even know what this is. This is insane.”

    When Zosia looked over, Eleanor was watching them, her face tightening briefly before she smoothed it out again. The hurt in her eyes was clear enough.

    “I should get back.” Her tone stayed professional. “There’s a lot to prepare, and I’ll need you to come in tomorrow to go over everything and sign.”

    Carol nodded, still catching up. “Yeah. Of course. Tomorrow works.”

    She walked Eleanor to the door, stopping her just before she stepped out. “And Eleanor… thank you. For coming by, I mean.”

    Eleanor gave a small, measured smile that didn’t quite linger. “It seemed appropriate.”

    Then she left.

    Carol closed the door slowly and stood there for a second with her hand still on the handle, like she needed the pause before turning back.

    “I can’t believe this,” she said, more to herself than anyone else.

    Joanna was already getting up, heading toward the kitchen with a kind of practical certainty. “Well, whether you believe it or not, this is exactly the kind of situation that requires a drink.”

    “I’ll call Koumba,” Zosia said, already reaching for her phone, though she didn’t look away from Carol just yet.

    Carol moved to the table and braced her hands against it, her head dropping forward for a second as everything finally caught up with her all at once.

    “It’s actually over.”

     

    ——

     

    Zosia had just finished with a customer when the phone rang.

    Koumba.

    “Sorry to say this, but you have to close the store,” he said as soon as she picked up.

    She glanced at the clock. “Why? It’s only four.”

    “You can’t ask questions. I’m picking you up by Uber in 15 minutes.”

    Zosia pinched the bridge of her nose. “I can’t just close the store.”

    “Trust me.” He cut in.

    There was a small pause.

    “Make sure you’re waiting on the corner of the street.” He hung up after that.

    Zosia put the phone in her pocket and waited for the last customer to leave. She then made her way to the door, flicked the sign to closed, and walked toward the corner of the street. She was still unsure what this was about.

    A car pulled up, and Koumba leaned out of the open window, waving enthusiastically.

    “Get in!” he called out.

    She slid into the seat. “What’s happening?”

    Koumba smiled at her. “Good things only, mon chérie!” he said. “I just want to show you something.”

    “Where are we going?”

    “I’m not allowed to say.”

    “Carol?”

    He looked at her. “I’m not allowed to say,” he said, slower now.

    Zosia let out a quiet breath through her nose, leaning back in her seat, but her eyes stayed on the window. The city thinned out around them, streets widening, buildings lower.

    She frowned, then leaned forward, looking more closely now, scanning stores they passed, the school, the red house with the pool.

    Something about this looked familiar.

    “Wait.” She frowned. “This is the neighborhood where my grandma used to live.”

    Koumba glanced at her briefly. Then back at the window.

    Zosia turned toward him fully now. “What are we doing here?”

    He gave her nothing.

    The car turned into the driveway. Zosia’s breath caught at the sight of her grandmother’s house.

    It looked the same, but somehow completely different. 

    “Koumba?” Her voice came out tense.

    The car rolled to a stop and the engine clicked as it turned off.

    “Please, step out of the car and ring the doorbell.” His voice was contained.

    Zosia blinked at him. “What?”

    “Please,” he repeated, calm as ever.

    She stared at him for a second longer, then back at the house. “I think people live here now.”

    “Zosia…”

    She didn’t move right away. Her eyes drifted back to the house. To the garden. It used to be wild and overgrown. Now it was neatly trimmed. Flowers everywhere. The bushes shaped, the path cleared.

    Someone had cared for it.

    Slowly, she pushed the door open and stepped out.

    The porch had been repainted. The windows were not only clean, they were new, no longer dulled by age.

    Zosia swallowed, something tight forming in her chest as she started walking up the path.

    Memories came in flashes.

    Running up these steps as a kid. Her grandmother’s voice from inside. The smell of some Polish cuisine cooking.

    At the door, she hesitated. Her hand hovered near the bell, then she pressed it. She recognized the chime, it hadn’t been changed.

    A few seconds passed. Nothing.

    Koumba was no longer in the car. “It’s open.” His voice came from behind her now.

    Zosia frowned slightly, turning back to the door. Her hand moved to the handle, and she pushed it open.

    “Hello?” she called carefully, still expecting strangers to suddenly show up.

    She stepped inside, and stopped. Her eyes moved slowly across the space.

    The walls had been repainted, the color was warmer now. The floor had been restored. Even the light coming through the windows felt brighter somehow.

    She took another step. Carefully. She felt like an intruder.

    The kitchen came into view. It looked modern. New counters. New cabinets. The layout still felt familiar. Someone had taken the memory of it and made it… better.

    She suddenly heard footsteps approaching.

    Zosia hesitated. She started turning away. Then…

    “Carol?”

    “Hi.”

    Zosia’s breath hitched, coming faster now, uneven. “What…” She tried again, her voice trembling under the weight of it. “What is happening?”

    Carol slowly walked toward her, closing the distance one step at a time, like she didn’t want to startle her. “What do you think?” she said as she gestured around them.

    Zosia turned in place, her eyes sweeping the room again. The light, the space, the care in every detail.

    “It’s…” She swallowed. “It’s beautiful.” Her gaze found Carol again, searching her eyes for answers. “But I don’t understand.”

    Carol didn’t answer right away. She just looked at her, unmistakable pride in her eyes.

    Zosia suddenly understood. “You did this?”

    Carol nodded. “With a lot of help.”

    Zosia let out a breath that almost broke into a laugh, her hand coming up to her mouth for a second. “This is…” She shook her head. “I don’t even have words.”

    Carol took another step closer.

    “I wanted to help your parents sell it,” she said. “So I fixed it up.” A small pause. “I’ve had plenty of time on my hands.” She looked at her hands. “And cuts and bruises as you’ve noticed.”

    She paused a moment.

    “If you want it,” she paused. “It’s yours.”

    Zosia’s eyes widened slightly.

    “And if you don’t,” Carol continued, lifting a hand to stop her before she could respond, “we’ll sell it. Get them a better price than they could’ve before.” She gave a short nod. “Either way, it’s fine.”

    Carol took another step closer. Then added, “You don’t have to decide right now.”

    Zosia stared at her. Her lips parted, breath shallow, overwhelmed in the best way.

    “I love it,” she said, almost immediately. She turned around again. The house was actually perfect. She could already see it. Mornings in the kitchen. Evenings on the couch. Their house. 

    “Zosia…” Carol continued behind her, her voice turned more serious, more vulnerable. “I do want an answer to something else.”

    Zosia frowned slightly, still trying to catch up.

    She turned. And stopped.

    Carol was on one knee.

    Her hands were steady, but there was tension in her eyes. She held up a small box, opening it carefully.

    The ring caught the light, impossible to miss.

    “Zosia…” There was a faint tremble in her voice. 

    “Will you marry me?”

    Zosia stopped breathing for a moment.

    Her vision blurred almost instantly. She felt tears rising faster than she could stop them. Her hands trembled at her sides, her whole body catching up with the moment all at once.

    “I…” She let out a quick, nervous laugh. “I…”

    Her eyes flicked, just for a second, to Koumba, who stood further back, half in the doorway, failing to hide the emotion on his face.

    Then she looked back at Carol. And everything settled.

    “Yes,” she breathed, the word slipping out with a small disbelieving laugh. “Yes…”

    She took a deep breath to steady her voice.

    “Yes. Absolutely yes.” She said again, clearer now.

    She stepped forward, closing the distance in one motion.

    Carol rose to her feet, barely giving her time before Zosia reached her, hands coming up, pulling her in, and kissing her.

    Tears slipped down her cheeks. Zosia didn’t know whose tears they were, and she didn’t care. She laughed into the kiss, breathless, overwhelmed, her hands tightening at Carol’s back.

    “I really want to marry you,” Zosia whispered against her lips, smiling through the tears.

    Then she kissed her again.

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks everyone for reading! I hoped you enjoyed their journey as much as I did.

    I have to admit, somewhere around chapter six the story changed completely. The ending stayed the same, but the path to get there became something else.
    There was an actual reason I called it 'Off the Record', beyond Zosia owning a record store. But well...

    I also want to give a special thanks to my two beta readers. My wife, and my best friend.
    The latter I can't look in the eye anymore after writing all that smut. 😅
